# GIOVANNI FRANCESCO PI-
# co della Mirandola, Lord and

-> Count of Concordia, etc. <note>Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola (1469–1533) was a philosopher and the nephew of the famous humanist Giovanni Pico della Mirandola.</note> A Dialogue divided into three <-
-> Books: The title is <-
-> **THE WITCH,** <note>original: "STRIX"</note> <-
-> or, on the Deception of <-
-> Demons. <note>This text is one of the earliest and most influential intellectual treatises on the "new" heresy of witchcraft, written in the form of a Platonic dialogue.</note> <-

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory material for Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola’s "Strix" (The Witch). While the previous page provided the title and initial context, this page features a formal dedication by Leandro Alberti, a prominent Dominican scholar, to Altobello Averoldo. Alberti explains the immediate cause for writing this book: public unrest following a local witch trial.</meta>

### LEANDRO ALBERTI OF BOLOGNA, OF THE ORDER
### OF PREACHERS, TO THE MOST DISTINGUISHED FATHER AL-
### TOBELLO AVEROLDO OF BRESCIA, BISHOP OF PULA
### AND VICE-LEGATE OF BOLOGNA, GREETINGS. <note>Altobello Averoldo (c. 1465–1531) was a powerful Italian prelate and diplomat who served as the governor of Bologna (Vice-Legate).</note>

---

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut drop cap 'H' within a square frame featuring foliate and floral motifs.</image-desc>While our fellow Dominican Girolamo Faentino, <note>Girolamo Faentino was an inquisitor active in Northern Italy during the early 16th century.</note> the Censor of Heretics, most excellent Prelate, was in recent days at Mirandola taking action against many who held wicked views concerning the faith of Christ the Best and Greatest—and who most shamelessly subjected His most sacred body to various mockeries—and while the magistrate was sentencing them to the fire, no small amount of grumbling <note>original: "muffitationes." This refers to the quiet, discontented whispering or murmuring of a crowd.</note> arose among the people. It was as if Girolamo were acting unjustly by pursuing this class of men with such severity. They claimed these people had done nothing wrong against the Catholic faith for which they should be punished with such a cruel penalty, since they otherwise appeared simple and completely uneducated; and if they had indeed fallen into any guilt, they should be said to have been deceived by diabolical tricks. For this reason, the most illustrious Prince of that town, and the "phoenix" of the learned men of our age (if I may say so without offending others), Gianfrancesco Pico <note>Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola (1469–1533), the author of this work and nephew of the famous philosopher Giovanni Pico della Mirandola.</note>—that most excellent defender and cultivator of the Christian religion—composed these three books of Dialogues on these matters, in emulation of Plato, so that he might counter such grumblings equipped with the shield of truth. He finished them with such speed that he produced them before anyone could even know he was writing them. In these books, he both uncovers the deceits of demons and clearly shows...

<summary>In this dedication, Leandro Alberti explains that Gianfrancesco Pico wrote "Strix" to justify the execution of accused witches in Mirandola after the local public expressed sympathy for them. Pico aims to prove that these individuals were not merely "simple and deceived" but were active participants in demonic heresy.</summary>

<keywords>Leandro Alberti, Altobello Averoldo, Girolamo Faentino, Mirandola, witch trials, heresy, Inquisition, Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola, demonology, public dissent</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page concludes Leandro Alberti’s dedicatory letter to Altobello Averoldo. Alberti continues his praise of Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola’s work, which defends the actions of the inquisitor Girolamo Faentino against local critics. The letter ends with a formal dedication and the date and location of writing: the Dominican Priory in Bologna, April 21, 1523.</meta>

...the ancient hater <note>original: "osorem antiquum." A traditional epithet for the Devil or Satan.</note> himself, who has deluded the human race in every age with various arts and different machinations, in previous centuries no less than in these times of ours. Therefore, the Prince <note>Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola.</note> reviews the cause of this sect <note>The "sect" refers to the alleged society of witches.</note> and, in the course of his review, condemns it. By this condemnation, he demonstrates that Girolamo the Censor <note>Girolamo Faentino, the inquisitor mentioned on the previous page.</note> acted rightly in punishing that pestilential kind with such a penalty.

Since, therefore, most distinguished Father, I have come from Mirandola to Bologna in these days, I wished to approach you in imitation of that most praised custom of the Persians: never to visit a superior without a gift. Thus, I bring you these three little books <note>The "Strix" (The Witch) is composed as a dialogue in three books.</note> by so great a Prince and so great a man. I have no doubt that you will find great pleasure in reading them. For it is only fitting that the works of a prince and a man of letters should come into the hands of one who is himself both a prince and a man of letters, to be handled and studied with care.

Now, for the third time, on account of your extraordinary virtues, you have been sent to rule this most grand city. The city first pleaded for your return, and then, when its prayer was granted, this most flourishing community erupted in applause. You now govern and guide it with justice and holiness, as has always been your custom. Indeed, you have joined a wonderful dexterity to that vivid and vigorous genius of yours; you have paired uprightness with dexterity, and gravity with uprightness, so that you conduct all affairs as befits a gentle, just, and serious prince. For this, we give great thanks to God the Most High, who has given you to us once again and set you over us. 

But enough on these matters. Therefore, hail, Prelate and most excellent Prince, and farewell. Be mindful of your Leandro, and receive this gift of ours with a joyful countenance, as we are certain you shall.

->From the Dominican Priory<-
->April 21, 1523<- <note>original: "xi. Cal. Maias. M.D.XX.III." The 11th day before the Kalends of May is April 21st.</note>

<summary>In this concluding dedication, Leandro Alberti presents Gianfrancesco Pico's "Strix" as a gift to the Vice-Legate of Bologna, praising both the author's defense of the Inquisition and the recipient's virtuous governance.</summary>
<keywords>Leandro Alberti, Altobello Averoldo, Bologna, Dominican Order, witchcraft trials, Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola, Inquisition, 1523</keywords>

### GIOVANNI ANTONIO FLAMINIO SENDS GREETINGS TO THE ILLUSTRIOUS PRINCE GIOVANNI PICO OF MIRANDOLA <note>Though addressed to "Giovanni Pico," this refers to Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola (1469–1533), the nephew of the famous philosopher and author of the work on witchcraft being discussed here.</note>.

I have read with great pleasure your brilliant little work, Illustrious Prince, which you wrote against that wicked and pestilential race <note>The "pestilential race" refers to witches and sorcerers.</note>, a truly beautiful and most useful book. In it, you uncover the illusions and fallacies of demons in an admirable way. Through the numerous testimonies of both Greek and Latin writers, you teach and demonstrate to be true those things which most people believed were merely old wives' tales and delirious ravings. Indeed, that divine intellect of yours, striving for the common good, always brings forth something that not only assists the world of letters but also—what is much more beautiful and more wonderful—adorns the Christian religion and makes it more illustrious. If our other Princes were to walk this same path, how much greater value, veneration, and worship would be granted not only to literature and learned men, but even to the Catholic faith?

Moreover, I was greatly gladdened that this famous monument to your genius was, with your permission, handed over to the printers <term>Chalcographis</term> <note>Literally "those who engrave in brass/copper," a common Renaissance term for printers.</note> so that its utility might be spread far and wide. This was done by our close friend Leandro Alberti of Bologna, a man, as you know, distinguished in the Order of Preachers <note>The Dominicans.</note> and most fond of both you and us.

Proceed then, most famous and most learned Prince, to bestow such clear and precious gifts upon both the Republic of Letters and the Christian Republic, and [to make] not only yourself and your most noble...

<summary>Giovanni Antonio Flaminio praises Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola for his scholarly work against witchcraft, arguing that the book validates the reality of demonic influence against skeptics who dismiss it as myth. Flaminio highlights the role of the Dominican Leandro Alberti in bringing the manuscript to print.</summary>

<keywords>Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola, Giovanni Antonio Flaminio, Leandro Alberti, witchcraft, demonology, Republic of Letters, Dominican Order, printing, Renaissance philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the dedicatory letter from Giovanni Antonio Flaminio to Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola. The text continues from the previous page's mention of Leandro Alberti, suggesting that the publication of the work will bring honor to the author's heritage.</meta>

...your family and your homeland, but also bring renown to the Roman <note>original: "Romanam... linguam". Referring to the Latin language, which was the international language of scholarship and the Church.</note> language and all of Italy. These clearly are the things which, day by day, increase and strengthen the splendor you have gained and the glory you have long possessed; because of them, men believe that nothing more is lacking for the immortality of your name, just as they acknowledge and proclaim it. Farewell. Bologna, the tenth day before the Kalends of May <note>Using the Roman calendar, the "tenth day before the Kalends of May" corresponds to April 22nd.</note>, 1523.

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a letter from Giovanni Antonio Flaminio to Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola, dated 1523. This page begins a new prefatory letter written by Gianfrancesco Pico himself, addressed to his friend and collaborator Giovan Francesco Mainardi (Maynardus).</meta>

### GIANFRANCESCO PICO SENDS GREETINGS TO HIS FRIEND MAINARDI.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'L' featuring intricate interlacing vine, floral, and leaf motifs within a square border.</image-desc>

I have recently trifled, <note>original: "Lusi". Pico uses the verb for "play" or "amusement" to describe the act of writing dialogues, a common trope of modesty among Renaissance humanists.</note> Maynardus—if indeed we believe that those famous men, both among the pagans and within our own religion, who published dialogues, were merely playing. But this genre of writing seems like play to me when I compare it to those matters discussed with greater gravity, where we inquire plainly into the nature of things themselves: whether they exist in the nature of things, what they are, upon what they depend, and what happens to them either intrinsically or as a result of external circumstances. 

When the question once arose during a conversation about my late-night studies <note>original: "lucubrationibus". This refers to scholarly work conducted by candlelight, symbolizing the dedicated life of an intellectual.</note> as to why I did not write in the form of a dialogue—but rather in a continuous discourse, divided into chapters, theorems, and even some questions and annotations—I replied frankly that it pleased me more to have the subject matter one deals with digested into its own constituent parts, and that a dialogue seems akin to poetry. 

I am well aware that sometimes fables are presented in prose, and occasionally the most serious matters are brought forth in verse—a practice I took care to observe in my hymns, and in that poem which I recently composed <note>original: "cecini", literally "sang".</note> and now send to you, concerning the awakening from the sleep of this present life. Yet, it does not escape me that if something fictional is mixed into a dialogue, it is not a lie; for by a certain silent consensus, it is made known and indicated to the reader that the event did not take place exactly as described, but could easily have occurred in such a way. 

So too, I am mindful that in dialogues it is not customary to proceed in that order which is most fitting for understanding the nature of things. Instead, a <term>Miscellaneous discipline</term> <note>A varied or "mixed" approach to learning, rather than a strictly linear or systematic one.</note> is presented, which in some way seems similar to the <term>Homeomeries</term> <note>original: "Omiomeriis". A philosophical concept from Anaxagoras suggesting that every substance contains minute parts of every other substance.</note> of <term>Anaxagoras</term> <note>An ancient Greek philosopher (c. 500–428 BC) who taught that "Mind" (Nous) was the ordering force of the universe.</note>. Among the Greeks, however, this genre of writing was held in particularly high honor (so eager is the human mind for variety, which was greatly charmed and captivated by <term>Socratic eloquence</term> <note>The persuasive and conversational style of teaching associated with Socrates, famously recorded in the dialogues of Plato.</note>). For the sake of this variety, things that were naturally dispersed were brought together as if into a single body. Since I lack such a faculty of speaking, I have also avoided that genre of writing. For the present, it has pleased me

<summary>Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola explains to Giovan Francesco Mainardi his preference for systematic, scholarly writing over the more "poetic" and "playful" format of the dialogue, citing his commitment to philosophical rigor.</summary>
<keywords>Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola, Giovan Francesco Mainardi, Dialogue, Philosophy, Anaxagoras, Homeomeries, Socratic eloquence, Literary Theory</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the prefatory letter from Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola to Giovan Francesco Mainardi. The previous page ended mid-sentence as Pico discussed his experimentation with the dialogue form.</meta>

...nevertheless, I decided to make the attempt. I was particularly moved by an opportunity that presented itself, arising from the mockeries of <term>witches</term> <note>original: "strigū" (strigum). The "strix" was originally a mythical screech-owl in Roman folklore believed to suck the blood of infants; by Pico's time, it became the standard Latin term for a witch.</note> and the evil deeds that have been perpetrated in our jurisdiction in recent months. From this, the title *The Witch* <note>original: "Strix"</note> was given to the work—or, if you prefer, *On the Delusion of Demons*.

Into this delusion, all those were said to fall headlong who were reportedly carried to the **game of Diana** <note>original: "ludum Dianæ". This refers to the "Society of Diana," a common medieval folk belief where women claimed to fly through the night following a goddess. The Church increasingly interpreted these as demonic hallucinations.</note> (which they now call "the course" or "the run"). This matter is certainly different in name from that ancient impiety, but whether it differs in its own nature or not at all, and in what way it should be considered similar or dissimilar, will be gathered from the discussion itself. Likewise, it will be understood what it has in common with other ancient superstitions of the nations, and what has been suggested by the recent malice of the malignant demon, the hater of the human race; and in repelling him, how much progress is made for the truth of religion.

But if you, and my other friends who are outstanding in learning and judgment, do not consider this time—roughly ten days—which I spent on these three discussions (or rather, little books contained in a dialogue), to have been wasted: then perhaps, as leisure permits, I shall write other dialogues on other matters. If not, I shall spend my hours on better things.

In this little work, you may be sure that when you hear the **Witch** speaking, you are hearing a pure history <note>original: "meram historiam". Pico is claiming that the dialogue is based on actual trial records and confessions, not just fiction.</note>, which I partly drank in with my eyes and partly with my ears while the records of the interrogations were being read to me. But lest I keep you from it any longer, listen now to **Apistius**, **Phronimus**, the **Witch**, and **Dicastes** <note>The characters' names are symbolic: Apistius (The Unbeliever/Skeptic), Phronimus (The Wise/Prudent Man), and Dicastes (The Judge).</note> as they discourse together on the 

->delusion of demons.<-

<summary>In this preface, Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola introduces his dialogue "Strix," explaining that it is based on recent local witchcraft trials and aims to distinguish modern witchcraft from ancient pagan superstitions.</summary>
<keywords>Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola, witchcraft trials, Strix, Game of Diana, demonology, Renaissance humanism, Apistius, Phronimus, Dicastes</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the dialogue itself, following the prefatory letter. The previous page concluded the author's introduction, where he explained his motivations for writing about the recent "delusions of demons" and the local witchcraft trials in Mirandola.</meta>

# DIALOGUE OF GIANFRANCESCO PICO DELLA MIRANDOLA, LORD [OF MIRANDOLA] AND COUNT OF CONCORDIA, ETC., TITLED *THE WITCH*, OR *ON THE DELUSION OF DEMONS*.

->Characters of the Dialogue: Apistius, Phronimus, Strix, Dicastes.<- <note>The names are symbolic: Apistius means "The Unbeliever" or "Skeptic" in Greek; Phronimus means "The Prudent" or "Wise" man; Strix is "The Witch"; and Dicastes is "The Judge."</note>

->APISTIVS.<-

<image-desc>A large square woodcut initial 'Q' containing a central medallion with a stylized plant or coat of arms, surrounded by an ornate frame of dense foliate and vine motifs.</image-desc>

**W**here is such a large crowd rushing, Phronimus, as they pass through the vegetable market over there? PHRONIMUS. Let us approach for a little while until the reason for such a great gathering becomes known to us. Can the loss of a few steps be so great? APISTIUS. They will not be few, if we continue all the way to the temple which they recently began to build for the Virgin Mother of God <note>original: "Virgini Deiparæ"</note>, which has been named for her miracles. For it is situated beyond the first milestone.

<margin>Construction began on a monastery for the Order of Preachers at Saint Mary of Miracles in Mirandola, 1522.</margin> <note>The Order of Preachers refers to the Dominicans, the religious order most frequently tasked with conducting the Inquisition.</note>
Indeed, I seem to see some members of that brotherhood who have chosen that sacred house as their dwelling. Therefore, I conjecture that all those we see are heading there. PHRONIMUS. I believe you conjecture correctly; for unless I am mistaken, I have spotted officers <note>original: "Satellites," referring to the secular guards or bailiffs who assisted the Inquisition.</note> mixed in with the crowd of boys—those who are accustomed to assist the priest whose habit it is to hold inquiries concerning evildoers <note>original: "maleficis hominibus." While this can mean "criminals," in this context it specifically refers to those accused of harmful magic or witchcraft.</note>. But what misfortune will come of it if we go there? On the contrary, I think this journey will bring us some benefit—if not a great one, then certainly something so pleasing that it should be regarded like a side dish <note>original: "opsonii uice," a metaphor for a pleasant intellectual diversion or "treat" added to a meal.</note> when we have returned. Moreover, it will perhaps be worth the effort, since it will be easy to learn something new. For I am led to believe that a **witch** <note>original: "Strigē" (Strigem).</note> has been captured, and this diverse crowd, mixed with boys, is rushing to look at her. APISTIUS. Witches? Do they dwell in these parts? I would not mind [walking] ten thousand paces...

<summary>Apistius and Phronimus observe a crowd rushing toward the new Dominican monastery of St. Mary of Miracles, suspecting that a witch has been captured and is being brought for questioning.</summary>
<keywords>Mirandola, St. Mary of Miracles, Order of Preachers, Dominicans, witchcraft trials, Apistius, Phronimus, Inquisitor's assistants</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the conversation between Apistius and Phronimus. Having spotted the inquisitorial officers and discussed the crowd's movement toward the new monastery, the dialogue now shifts to the specific subject of the "Strix"—a term that bridge the gap between ancient folklore about predatory birds and the contemporary obsession with witches.</meta>

### ON THE SCREECH-OWLS <note>original: "DE STRIGIBVS"</note>

...to see. PHRONIMUS. Therefore, if you have never caught sight of one, perhaps now you will be able to have your wish granted. APISTIUS. Oh, if only it might happen that I find the bird I have sought with such zeal and never found! PHRONIMUS. Which bird do you mean? APISTIUS. The screech-owl <note>original: "Strigem." In Latin, *strix* refers both to a legendary owl-like bird that sucked the blood of infants and, by extension, to a witch.</note>. PHRONIMUS. You are joking, Apistius. APISTIUS. I am serious; you should not think this was spoken in jest. Since it ought to be most pleasing to a man, especially a curious one, to know what antiquity was ignorant of. PHRONIMUS. So, you are struggling with ignorance of a thing that everyone knows? APISTIUS. Do you think I wish to claim for myself something that great men of old, who deserved excellently of the Arts <note>original: "de Iris," likely a contraction or OCR error for "de literis" (of letters/learning)</note>, affirmed they had never caught—nor was it even certain if they had ever caught one? PHRONIMUS. What?

<margin>
From the *Metamorphoses*. <note>A work by Ovid.</note>
From Lucan.
From the Tragedies of Seneca.
</margin>
APISTIUS. The screech-owl bird. For I have read: *And the infamous wings of the screech-owl with its very flesh.* And: *What the trembling owl and the nocturnal screech-owl lament.* And: *The grim omen of the ill-omened screech-owl resounds*, and: *The heart of the mournful owl, and the entrails of the hoarse screech-owl, cut out while alive.* I also know that "screech-owl" was used as a term of abuse among the ancients. Nevertheless, what it actually is, or what its nature is, is not agreed upon. Pliny thinks that what was handed down to memory regarding screech-owls was a fable—namely, that they milk the breasts of infants with their lips—and he clearly testifies that it is not certain what bird the screech-owl is. PHRONIMUS. I am surprised that you, who are skilled in the poets as I see, have not read that screech-owls were traditionally driven away from doors with a branch made of white-thorn; and that they are greedy birds with a large head, staring eyes, a hooked beak, feathers flecked with gray, and hooked talons; and they were called by that name because they are accustomed to screech <note>original: "stridere"</note> horribly at night. Thus, you see the name, the reason for the name, its nature, and its appearance are all preserved in the monuments of literature.

<margin>
From Ovid's *Fasti*.
</margin>
APISTIUS. I follow what you are suggesting. But perhaps those screech-owls are of a different kind and a varied nature. For they are not said to milk the breasts of infants with their lips, but to suck out their blood. Whence that poet sang: *They fly by night and seek boys lacking a nurse; they violate bodies snatched from their cradles; they are said to pluck the milky entrails with their beaks, and they have a breast full of the blood they have drunk.* And this has been observed to exist even from heroic times, for these verses move me: *They came into the chambers of Procas; Procas, born in them, was fresh prey for the birds for five days; and they suck out the infant breasts with greedy tongues. And the unlucky boy wails and seeks help; the nurse, terrified by the voice of her nursling, runs to him and finds his cheeks cut by a stiff claw.* <note>Procas was a legendary king of Alba Longa; this story from Ovid's *Fasti* describes a "strix" attacking him as an infant.</note> Does it not seem to you that these duties, so contrary to one another, show a varied and contrary nature and species of screech-owls: some beneficial...

<margin>
From the *Fasti*.
From the *Fasti*.
</margin>

<summary>Apistius and Phronimus debate the nature of the "Strix" (screech-owl/witch), citing classical authorities like Ovid and Pliny to determine if it is a biological bird or a supernatural creature. Apistius highlights the contradictions in ancient texts regarding whether these creatures suck milk or blood from infants.</summary>
<keywords>Strix, screech-owl, folklore, Pliny the Elder, Ovid, infants, blood-sucking, classical literature, skepticism</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the skeptical Apistius and the more traditional Phronimus. They are moving from a discussion of ancient literary references to the screech-owl (Strix) toward the contemporary belief in witches who fly at night and harm children.</meta>

# BOOK ONE
B ii

...it was right to judge those birds as harmful, though they pretend to perform the duty of a nurse; but these are especially dangerous, as they kill infant boys by drinking their blood. **PHRONIMUS.** To me, both seem like fables, or if there is any solid truth in the story, I believe those birds were not born that way, nor were they created by the "Song of Nenia" <note>Nenia was the Roman goddess of funerary laments; here it refers to magical incantations</note> when the false old woman transforms them into winged creatures. Rather, I think it is done by the power of evil demons, so that those birds appear as such, carrying the likeness of a lurking nurse. 

<margin>From the *Fasti* <note>A poem by Ovid about the Roman calendar.</note></margin>

And this idea pleases me more because a demon, pretending to be the god Janus, taught the remedy for such a "fascination" <term>fascini</term> <note>From *fascinatio*, referring to the "evil eye" or a magical curse.</note>. He touched the doorposts three times with a branch of the strawberry tree <note>Latin: *arbutea*</note>, marked the thresholds three times with the same branch, sprinkled the entrances with water, and performed other tasks which were not sacred rites but rather cursed portents. The physicians also have something to say on where those things come from. Furthermore, "if the black screech-owl <term>Strix</term> presses upon the children by night, milking its poisonous breasts into their untouched lips." 

<margin>From Quintus Serenus <note>A 3rd-century Roman medical author.</note></margin>

Indeed, the screech-owls of our own time are accustomed to do just this: when they are said to be carried off to the "Game of Diana," they harm the crying newborns in their cradles. Soon after, they apply remedies which seem to me to depend on these same spirits, so much so that even the name is derived from them; for among us, the name of "screech-owl" <note>original: "strigis". Here Phronimus is noting that the word for the mythical bird is now being used to describe women accused of witchcraft.</note> is now widely used and accepted for the women who commit such crimes. 

**APISTIUS.** And so, Phronimus, you seem to be deluded by the same error that leads most of our countrymen astray, leading you to believe that what the common people say is true: that there are I-know-not-what little women who fly together in the dead of night to banquets and to the beds of ghosts <term>lemures</term> <note>In Roman mythology, these were the wandering and vengeful spirits of the dead.</note>, in order to bewitch infants. 

**PHRONIMUS.** Speak better of them! For what not a few men—who are well-regarded in letters, experienced in affairs, and endowed with good character—hold as a proven fact and openly declare, should not be dismissed as a mere error of naming. 

**APISTIUS.** Truly, I could never be persuaded to let such things into my mind. 

**PHRONIMUS.** For what reason? 

**APISTIUS.** Because it seems ridiculous that—by making a circle, applying some kind of ointment to the body, and uttering words in some kind of murmur—these little harlots meet with the demons of the underworld. They are said to ride at night upon a wooden stick (of the sort used to pound flax and hemp), or upon a she-goat, a roar, or a ram; that they are carried through the air and skim across the reaches of the winds to take part in the dances of Diana or Herodias <note>Herodias, the biblical figure, was often paired with the goddess Diana in medieval folklore as a leader of the nocturnal "Wild Hunt."</note>; and that they play, eat, drink, and delight in obscenities. Add to this that the stories do not agree, as I have heard. For some say they are carried through the high tracts of the air, others that they are carried closer to the ground. Some testify that they go in spirit, not in body. Later, they are said to stop above Lake Benacum <note>Now known as Lake Garda in northern Italy.</note> on the highest mountains. I wonder why they did not say they were on Mount Mycale with Thales, or on Mount Mimas with...

<summary>The speakers debate whether the "Strix" (screech-owl) is a physical bird or a demon-influenced witch, touching on classical poetry and contemporary folklore about the "Game of Diana." Apistius remains highly skeptical of the logistics of the witches' Sabbath, such as flying on sticks and meeting demons.</summary>

# ON WITCHES

<margin>

From Plutarch.
</margin>

...to dwell with Anaxagoras <note>An ancient Greek philosopher known for his scientific theories about the cosmos.</note>. Some women repeatedly claim they are carried to the Beneventan nut tree <note>The famous walnut tree of Benevento, which in Italian folklore was the central gathering place for witches' Sabbats.</note> (unless I am mistaken). But what reason is there that they are not placed rather in the territory of Arpinum <note>The birthplace of Marcus Tullius Cicero.</note>, which is closer to our shores (I think), brought to the Marian Oak—unless they are reluctant to travel further all the way to Chaeronea to the Oak of Alexander <note>A tree mentioned by the historian Plutarch near the site of Alexander the Great's victory.</note>. Furthermore, they say they have intercourse with demons who are (unless I am mistaken) separated from the body. What contact can there be with such beings? What lust? Or in what way can women of flesh and blood take delight in some empty phantom? For I have learned that ghosts <term>larvae</term> <note>In Roman mythology, *larvae* were the restless, frightening spirits of the dead.</note> are accustomed to wrestle with the dead, not with the living. **PHRONIMUS.** If I dissolve your arguments for you, I hope that you will agree. **APISTIUS.** Why not? **PHRONIMUS.** For it is the mark of a wise man to be moved, led, and established by reasons, examples, and the authority of our ancestors which has been confirmed by consensus—and much more so for one endowed with talent who has devoted himself to literature. Therefore, if I lead you by your own principles to accept that which you now mock, what will you do? **APISTIUS.** I will throw up my hands <note>original: *manus dabo*, a Latin idiom meaning to surrender an argument or admit defeat.</note>. **PHRONIMUS.** I think you will give your feet as well. **APISTIUS.** By no means into shackles! **PHRONIMUS.** I would never aim for that, but rather that you might come over to my opinion "with hands and feet" <note>A Latin expression meaning to agree completely or with one’s whole being.</note>, as the saying goes; I wish for that to happen easily. **APISTIUS.** I do not deny it will happen, if you prove what you claim. **PHRONIMUS.** From the conversation we have had while walking, you seem to me to be thoroughly familiar with the poets of the nations, and not to shrink from philosophy. **APISTIUS.** I do not claim for myself that I am an expert in the poets. For that profession is too great for anyone to claim it out of nothing. For first, it would be necessary to know both the Greek and Latin languages. Then, one must have learned the hidden meanings frequently pulled from the storehouse of philosophy—of which the poets are full, especially Homer. I have heard that Homer was illustrated with grand commentaries by Aristotle and certain philosophers from the Porch <note>The Stoics, who often used allegorical interpretations of Homer.</note>, and I have heard that Plutarch, in one quite large book, strove to attribute all science, every art, and finally all things divine and human to that "Blind Man" <note>A traditional epithet for Homer.</note>. Therefore, while I deny that I possess such knowledge, I do not deny that I have sometimes spent time reading them—but rather to gain a knowledge of languages, or to soak up some precepts fit for morals as if doing something else <note>original: *quasi aliud agens*, meaning incidentally or while focusing on other things.</note>, so that in a circle of friends I might not seem ignorant of literature when the occasion arises to speak. As for the philosophy said to lie hidden there: if I have not drunk it, I have at least touched it and, as the saying goes, "tasted it with the tips of my lips" <note>A common metaphor for a superficial or introductory acquaintance with a subject.</note>.

<summary>Apistius mocks the absurd locations of alleged witch gatherings and the possibility of physical contact with demons, while Phronimus prepares to use Apistius's own literary and philosophical education to prove the reality of these supernatural claims.</summary>

# BOOK ONE

...lips. **PHRONIMUS.** I do not think you are led by arrogance or by irony, but by truth—which Aristotle places as a virtue in the middle between those vices. <note>Phronimus refers to Aristotle's "Golden Mean," where virtue is the balanced center between two extremes.</note> For you do not pretend to know nothing, nor do you boast that you possess all knowledge. <margin>Plato, Aristotle.</margin> Furthermore, what you say regarding the knowledge of poets does not shrink from the truth. For Plato himself and Aristotle are full of testimonies from the poets: Homer, Hesiod, Simonides, Pindar, Euripides, and others. Therefore, I fear you have embraced a philosophy that you are hiding. I suspect that more is stored up in the inner recesses of your mind than you display on the surface. **APISTIUS.** Nature sometimes grants us either virtues, or certain seeds of virtue, without any study at all. **PHRONIMUS.** With this response, you have given me an even greater doubt. **APISTIUS.** Which one do you mean? **PHRONIMUS.** That I am speaking with a philosopher. But I will remove that doubt immediately, taking my start from here: if you will agree to respond to those things I wish to ask about concerning the matters we began to discuss. **APISTIUS.** I profess that I will respond. **PHRONIMUS.** Have you ever read in Homer that Ulysses reached the Cimmerians? <note>Ulysses is the Latin name for the Greek hero Odysseus. The Cimmerians were a mythical people living at the edge of the world in perpetual darkness.</note> **APISTIUS.** I have read it; he went to a people living in a misty air, impervious to the rays of the sun. **PHRONIMUS.** What did he do? **APISTIUS.** Many things. **PHRONIMUS.** Are these not his words? "I, drawing my sharp sword from beside my thigh, dug a pit about a cubit's length this way and that. And around it I poured libations to all the dead." <note>original Greek: Ἐγὼ δ' ἄορ ὀξὺ ἐρυσα-σάμενος παρὰ μηροῦ. βόθρον ὄρυξ' ὅσσον τε πυγούσιον ἔνθα καὶ ἔνθα. Ἀμφ' αὐτῶ δὲ χοὰς χεόμην πᾶσι νεκύεσσιν?</note> If you approve, I will perhaps translate these verses into Latin. **APISTIUS.** You would do me a favor. **PHRONIMUS.** "I myself drew the sword from my thigh, then immediately began to dig a pit a cubit in measure around here and there; and I poured libations to the shades." <note>original: "Ipse ego de femore extraxi, tunc protinus ensem. Et foueam cubiti ad mensuram scalpere circum. Hinc atq; hinc cœpi: atq; umbris libamina fudi."</note> **APISTIUS.** You have expressed the meaning and the words well. **PHRONIMUS.** I think you have read more than once of Diana's games and dances with her companion nymphs. **APISTIUS.** You think correctly. **PHRONIMUS.** And also that Anchises met with Venus, and that many heroes of the ancient time were born from those deceptive gods. **APISTIUS.** I have read that quite often. **PHRONIMUS.** They used to delude men devoted to rural and pastoral care in wondrous ways—as was the life of most of those who were said to have flourished in the heroic ages. <margin>From Ovid.</margin> Thus also a Demon deluded the shepherd Peleus <note>A hero of Greek myth, father of Achilles. He captured the sea-nymph Thetis by holding onto her while she changed shapes.</note>—who, as that poet sang, left his flocks of sheep and herds not far from the walls in a shaded valley—under the image of the sea-goddess Thetis (as she was thought of among those nations). He had also been taught how to less notice the trick by another Demon, the shapeshifter <term>versipellis</term> Proteus: by whom... <note>*Versipellis* literally means "skin-changer" or "turn-skin," used here to describe the shapeshifting nature of the sea-god Proteus.</note>

<summary>Phronimus uses classical literature and mythology from Homer and Ovid to argue that ancient "gods" were actually deceptive demons who interacted with and deluded humans.</summary>

# ON WITCHES

<margin>From Ovid.</margin> ...the way Thetis herself was captured: having feigned a hundred shapes. <note>original: "pacto ipsa caperet̃ Thetis: cẽtū mētita figuras." Thetis was a sea nymph known for changing her form to escape her suitors.</note> But consider another fraud, by which she deceived him more thoroughly: when she did not pretend to debauchery or adultery, but instead simulated a marriage. This was celebrated by <margin>Hesiod. Regarding Catullus.</margin> Hesiod in a poem, as has been handed down in the Greek records. And from there, by probable conjecture, we say that the *Epithalamium* <note>A song or poem written specifically for a bride on her way to the marital chamber.</note> of Catullus was derived from the model of Hesiod. This is suggested by the very tenor of the poem, which displays that ancient simplicity, and the fates themselves declare Catullus’s zeal for imitating the Greeks. Indeed, he expressed entire elegies of Callimachus, sometimes rendering the sense and sometimes the word. In the same way, a <term>Demon</term> <note>In this context, the author views the figures of classical myth as deceptive spiritual entities or "demons" rather than mere stories.</note> easily imposed upon the shepherd Paris under the likeness of three Goddesses. As <margin>Coluthus.</margin> Coluthus the Theban sang in his book on the *Abduction of Helen*: "he was pasturing his father's sheep." <note>original Greek: "ἐνόμευε πατρῴια μῆλα."</note> Nor was he only pasturing his father’s sheep, but also bulls, and he was dressed in such clothing that you would recognize a simple shepherd and ox-herd. These are things he [Coluthus] follows at length. Similarly, that Lydian royal shepherd, by turning the bezel of a ring inward, made himself invisible, <margin>From Plato and others.</margin> which allowed him to commit adultery with the queen. <note>This refers to the Ring of Gyges, a mythical artifact mentioned by Plato that granted invisibility.</note> Thus, the Demons took on various forms: sometimes of Goddesses (as they were called), sometimes of terrestrial nymphs, and sometimes of maritime ones. Because they were believed by their very nature to have hidden beneath the waves, so that they might be seen and burn [with passion] more strongly, they stood out from the white whirlpool as far as their breasts. And they appeared in the specter of a cloud, as they tell the story of Juno and Ixion, from whom they feigned a counterfeit centaur emerged. <note>In Greek myth, Ixion was deceived by a cloud-phantom (Nephele) shaped like the goddess Juno/Hera; the resulting offspring were the Centaurs.</note> They prepared other delusions by which they might deceive the nations, and by which they might impose doubled frauds upon both the unlearned and the learned crowd. Nor was there any image of feigned divinity that did not press upon that primitive age with its wantonness. <margin>From Catullus.</margin> For it has been discovered that even Diana herself—whose hatred of the sport [of love] and whose virginity (which she feigned to love) we have uncovered as a Demonic trick, perhaps to entice those who <margin>The many frauds of demons.</margin> recoiled from foul lust—prostituted herself. Thus, under the name of the Moon—who was undoubtedly also called Diana—they said she was embraced by Endymion. <note>Endymion was a beautiful shepherd who was granted eternal sleep so that the Moon goddess could visit and embrace him every night.</note> Firmianus <note>Referring to Lactantius (Lucius Caecilius Firmianus Lactantius), an early Christian author who critiqued pagan myths.</note> also invites us to consider Hippolytus, under the name of Diana, which he thought pertained to that matter: both the name "Virbius" and the place where he was hidden (which was sought most diligently), and the healing hands of Aesculapius applied to his wounds. All those things must be attributed to the stories of Demonic Magic—if indeed any foundation for them exists—and to this Aesculapius was given the "reward of the magi," which is to say: a horrible death. For it is agreed among all authors, even the most ancient, that he perished by a lightning bolt, although there is much disagreement as to the cause of such a sacri- <note>The text breaks here to the next page.</note>
legies

It links Greek literary tradition to Roman poetry while framing mythical figures like Diana and Aesculapius as warnings against demonic magic.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Phronimus (PH) and Apistius (AP) regarding the nature of ancient myths and the influence of deceptive spirits. Having previously discussed the illusions surrounding figures like Ulysses and Thetis, they now turn to the legends of Aesculapius and the presence of magic in the modern world.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

AP. Virgil reads that Hippolytus was called back to life. Pliny 
<margin>Various disagreements concerning the death of Aesculapius.</margin>
and others have attributed it to the sake of the Tyndaridae <note>The sons of Tyndareus, better known as the twins Castor and Pollux.</note>. PH. Stesichorus, Panyasis, Polyathus, Philarchus, Telesarchus, and others have recorded it in different ways. Do you know why Aesculapius was struck by lightning from heaven? AP. Tell me, if it comes to mind. PH. There are those who prefer to believe it was because he had returned Tyndareus to life (rather than one of the Tyndaridae <note>This refers to a debate over whether the physician resurrected the father, Tyndareus, or one of his children.</note>) that he was struck by the bolt. Staphylus records that no one was raised from the dead by Aesculapius; instead, he merely healed Hippolytus as he fled from Troezen, and for that reason he received the lightning strike. Polyanthus preferred the cause to be the curing of the daughters of Proetus from their madness; Philarchus because he brought aid to the sons of Phineus. Among those, however, who claimed he had performed cures so that the dead returned to life: there are those who were pleased to suggest it was many of those who fell in the Trojan war. Others say it was those killed in the Theban battle. Yet Telesarchus is not lacking, who attributed the lightning received not to the success, but to the mere *attempt* at bringing Orion back to life. That well-known point of Pindar is repeated by <term>Tertullian</term> <note>An early Christian author (c. 155 – c. 220 AD) who often criticized pagan myths as demonic or fraudulent.</note>: that Aesculapius was burned by heavenly punishment because he had practiced medicine harmfully. Thus, in this matter, we find even more varied accounts than in the death of Romulus. Yet both were counted among the gods by the nations, however much the latter was a thief and the former a magician. 
<margin>Concerning the death of a noble magician within the memory of our grandfathers.</margin>
Because of this, I wonder all the more that a certain distinguished man did not look out for himself—one who, in the memory of our grandfathers and at the great expense of a certain prince, promised to recount the Trojan war and the whole siege of Ilium. While he was describing the surroundings, to show where the descendant of Aeacus <note>Achilles, the grandson of Aeacus.</note> and where Ulysses had pitched their tents, he was snatched away by a <term>Demon</term> and never appeared again. AP. You tell of wonders. PH. But they are facts. For that prince sent searches for that man to various peoples of Italy and to the Germans—and where did he not send? One of his disciples, after the master perished, came to this very town and left traces of his deeds right up to my own memory. For he used to paint the image of whoever had committed a theft for the person seeking it; and in a phial of water, by employing certain sacrileges, he would show the figure, the clothing, and the whole manner which had been maintained by the thief in carrying the thing away. I knew a man who was betrayed by him after he had stolen some amulets and hidden them at home in a chest, with no other person present. And I remember the time when he renounced his superstition and abjured the magical discipline. If we were to go on for ten days together, I do not think it would be enough for me to express the traps of the Demon which I have observed, betrayed in various ways...

<summary>Phronimus discusses the various mythical reasons for Aesculapius's death by lightning before pivoting to a "real-world" account of a contemporary magician who vanished mysteriously and a disciple who practiced scrying in a water bowl.</summary>
<keywords>Aesculapius, Hippolytus, scrying, Trojan War, magic, demons, Tertullian, Pindar, theft-detection</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Phronimus (PH) and Apistius (AP). Having previously discussed mythological resurrections and medical miracles, the conversation now shifts to the nature of the adversary (Satan), the reality of demons as discussed by Church Fathers like Augustine, and ancient precedents for magical flight and communication with the dead.</meta>

# ON WITCHES

<margin>Why the name of Satan is used.</margin>
For a most just reason, he is called **Satan** <note>original: "Sathan"; a name derived from the Hebrew word for "adversary" or "accuser."</note>, as he is always standing in opposition to the human race. This is true in all matters, but especially in this power of sexual union which we had intended to discuss. Those who deny this—acting in opposition to so many men who testify to what they have experienced or what they have heard from those who were experts—are charged with impudence by **Augustine**. Writing on the testimony of very frequent rumors in the fifteenth volume of *The City of God*, he states that **Silvani** and **Fauns** <note>Forest deities in Roman mythology, often associated with wild nature and fertility.</note>, whom the common people call **incubi**, have often behaved wickedly toward women, seeking and achieving sexual intercourse with them. He also notes that certain demons, whom the Gauls call **Dusii** <note>original: "Dusios"; Celtic forest spirits or spirits of the dead often equated with incubi in medieval demonology.</note>, constantly attempt and carry out this same impurity. 

AP. Please, go on. 
PH. As far as the journey through the air is concerned: I think you have also heard—unless you have read it—that **Abaris** came from the Hyperborean temple of **Apollo** <note>original: "Phœbi"; Phoebus is another name for Apollo.</note> to **Pythagoras** in Italy, traveling while supported by an arrow. 

<margin>From the books of Iamblichus on the Pythagorean sect.</margin>
AP. This is not unknown to me, having been entrusted to writing by a certain Platonic philosopher. 
PH. If these things occur to you rightly, you will easily agree to the rest. For the mind ought to consider that all that famous **necromancy** <note>original: "necyomantia"; the practice of communicating with the dead to predict the future.</note> of **Ulysses** <note>Odysseus</note> took its beginning from the drawing of a circle. From this, you will easily perceive that these things are not new fictions regarding the drawing of the **compass** <note>original: "Circini"; likely referring to the ritual drawing of a magic circle.</note>, but are ancient deceptions or fables which the Latin poets also took care to imitate. For **Scipio** is also introduced hollowing out the dug-up earth with iron, and what follows is after the example of Ulysses. 

<margin>From Silius Italicus.</margin>
But that conversations with the shades are older than the beginning they took from **Homer**, those can easily know who have discovered the songs of **Orpheus** on this very subject. It is established that Homer not only imitated him in the summoning of **Tiresias**, but also followed the very rhythms themselves with great care and scrupulous observations. 

<margin>Justin Martyr.</margin>
**Justin Martyr** <note>An early Christian apologist (c. 100–165 AD) who sought to relate Greek philosophy to Christian truth.</note> writes that the first poem of the *Iliad* was composed after the model of the first Orphic poem, which was inscribed concerning **Ceres** <note>Demeter, the goddess of the harvest and the underworld mysteries.</note>. And though the rites varied, nevertheless everyone sought commerce with the departed, such that they were said to descend to the underworld. This was reported to have happened to **Pythagoras** a long time after Orpheus and Homer. It is said that he saw the souls of **Hesiod** and Homer being punished for the things they had claimed about the gods. He was held in great honor for that reason among the people of **Crotone** <note>A Greek colony in Southern Italy where Pythagoras established his school.</note>, especially when he reported that he had noticed those who were unwilling to lie with their wives being tortured in the underworld. Furthermore...

<meta>catchword: concerning the air</meta>

<summary>This page explores the diabolical and magical origins of witchcraft, citing St. Augustine's views on incubi and connecting ancient Greek legends of Pythagoras and Ulysses to the "modern" rituals of summoning spirits and magical flight.</summary>
<keywords>Satan, Augustine, Incubi, Dusii, Abaris, Pythagoras, Necromancy, Ulysses, Homer, Orpheus, Justin Martyr, Underworld, Mythology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Phronimus (PH) and Apistius (AP). Having just mentioned Pythagoras and the legend of Abaris flying on an arrow, Phronimus now expands on various mythological and historical accounts of aerial travel and the use of magical ointments for transformation.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

I do not know why you doubt the passage through the space of the air, since it seems to make little difference. Would you measure the wings of carriers by an arrow, or perhaps by a **leaping goat** <note>original: "scamnoran capra"; the OCR "scamnoran" is likely a corruption of "scandentem" (climbing) or a similar term referring to the leaping nature of the goat, a common animal in folklore for magical transport</note>? For it has not been handed down to memory how **Pythagoras** and **Empedocles** <note>Greek philosophers to whom various miracles and supernatural powers were attributed in antiquity</note> were carried: whether by a two-horse or four-horse chariot, or on the winged **Pegasus**, or by dragons, or by swans—so as to imitate either **Venus** or **Medea**. Or perhaps they traveled like **Circe** with a pair of yoked serpents, or on lions like **Cybele**, or on lynxes after the example of **Bacchus**, or rather in the manner of **Triptolemus**, 
<margin>From the Metamorphoses.</margin>
now high above the East, far from the ground, just as he carried grain, so these men might carry the seeds of philosophy. Both were deceived by **Pallas**, that is, by the cunning of a **Demon**. 

<margin>Simon Magus.</margin>
AP. Unless I am mistaken, I remember also hearing of **Simon Magus** <note>A biblical figure (Acts 8) who in later tradition was said to have attempted to fly in Rome to prove his divinity, only to fall at the prayer of St. Peter</note>, who attempted an aerial journey to his own destruction. 

PH. And perhaps you have heard of certain Ethiopians who were accustomed to bridle **dragons** and come into Europe sitting upon their backs. For so it is said to have been handed down by **Roger Bacon** <note>A 13th-century English philosopher and friar known for his interest in nature and experimental science</note>. 
<margin>Roger Bacon.</margin>
But if you allow this to be placed at the judgment of a skillful listener and a learned reader, do not think that we are inventing **Daedalean** flights <note>Referring to Daedalus, who escaped Crete with wings of wax and feathers</note>. If these stories are not lies, they can be credited to the mechanical devices of demons. I shall remain silent on how **Apollonius of Tyana** <note>A 1st-century Greek philosopher and reputed miracle-worker</note> is said to have vanished from the sight of the Emperor **Domitian**. 
<margin>From Philostratus.</margin>
Furthermore, if you admit that **succubi and incubi** spirits <note>Demons traditionally thought to take female or male forms to have sexual intercourse with humans</note> existed among the ancients, what reason is there why you should not admit them to our own age, when it is proven by so many and such great testimonies? I shall recount them if you wish. 

But regarding the **ointment**, I think you are not ignorant of what both **Lucian the Syrian** and **Apuleius the African** <note>Authors of "The Ass" and "The Golden Ass," respectively, which feature stories of magical transformation through salves</note> have committed to the memory of posterity. One wrote in the Greek tongue, the other in Latin, and the latter expressed from the former both the **small casket** <note>original: "κυβώτιον" (kybōtion)</note> and **very many jars** <note>original: "πάνυ πολλάς πυξίδας" (panu pollas pyxidas)</note> and the **oil** <note>original: "ἔλαιον" (elaion)</note>. 
<margin>From Lucian's *The Ass*.</margin>
Why then a small box and so many jars and oil were turned over and over by that woman, the author himself may declare: **"Taking from this, she rubs herself all over, beginning from her toes upward, and suddenly wings grew out of her"** <note>original: "ἐκ τούτου λαβοῦσα χρίεται ὅλη ἀπὸ τῶν ὀνύχων ἀρξαμένη τῶν κάτω καὶ ἄφνω πτερὰ ἐκφύεται αὐτῆ"</note>. He writes that once she was anointed from her toenails, she was immediately made a winged creature. He adds a little later that she became nothing other than a **"night-raven"** <note>original: "κκοραξ νυκτερινὸς" (korax nykterinos)</note>. For so she appeared to those who either watched or imagined they saw her. For I would not believe that anything is transformed from its own appearance into another by any ointment or magic song. Nevertheless, those **witches** <note>original: "striges"; literally 'screech owls,' used here for hags or witches believed to fly at night</note> wished to be seen as if they were smeared with certain ointments.

<summary>Phronimus and Apistius discuss historical and mythological accounts of magical flight and transformation, citing figures like Simon Magus and Apollonius of Tyana before focusing on the use of magic ointments described by Lucian and Apuleius.</summary>
<keywords>magical flight, Pythagoras, Simon Magus, Roger Bacon, Apollonius of Tyana, magic ointments, transformation, Lucian, Apuleius, witches, incubi, succubi</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Phronimus (PH) and Apistius (AP). Having discussed the aerial travels of ancient philosophers and the sorcery of Simon Magus, Phronimus now turns to the legends of metamorphosis, specifically focusing on the famous tales of men being transformed into donkeys through magical ointments in Thessaly.</meta>

-># ON WITCHES<-

<meta>catchword: wondering</meta>

To this end, so that they might appear either to themselves or to others in a different form, they were changed from their previous state into one quite unlike it. And although this false and insipid rhetorician <note>original: "rhetor hic falsus"; likely referring to the satirist Lucian of Samosata</note> pretended he had been changed, he did not claim to be a bird—even though he had used the same potion—but lied about being changed into a donkey. Accordingly, that woman 
<margin>From the *Ass* of Apuleius:</margin>
grieved, suspecting that through an error with the small jar <note>original: "Pyxidis"; a pyxis was a small lidded box or jar often used for cosmetics or medicine</note>, Lucian had been changed into a donkey. From this, he subtly indicated that the matter was not one of essence, but of image; and he confirmed this most clearly by the fact that he professes to have retained the mind and spirit of Lucius while in the body of the donkey. But it should not be thought that such a dream of changing form would have come to his mind unless the rumor was established that this was a specialty of the women of Thessaly <note>Thessaly, a region in Greece, was renowned in antiquity as the homeland of powerful witches</note>. Nor did he who later imitated him, the Platonist <note>referring to Apuleius, author of *The Golden Ass*</note>, fail to confirm this, pretending that he set out for Thessaly, and before his former form was abolished, he feigned having put on a new one by swallowing—if I report his words correctly—a little more ointment and with agreements which he committed to memory in many ways. So that in almost every detail he seems to have wanted to imitate that famous reputation. Since he made mention of the Thessalian murmuring <note>incantations or spells</note>, of the transforming magic oil, and of the rose-colored amulet that restores the form.

AP. Why do you think mention was made of that rose-colored antidote <term>Alexipharmaci</term> <note>original: "Alexipharmaci"; a Greek-derived term for a remedy that protects against poison or magic</note>?

<margin>Aristotle.</margin>
PH. If there is any truth in it, I believe it was drawn from **Aristotle**, in whose writings I read that it is counted among the marvels: that donkeys are known to die easily from the scent of roses. This is why both Lucian and Lucius, being well-informed, invented the idea that the donkey they wanted to be clothed in would perish <note>that is, the donkey-form would be shed or destroyed to reveal the human</note>; or perhaps some other magical secret lies hidden. For certainly the women of Thessaly and Thrace were once infamous for sorcery <term>veneficiis</term> <note>original: "veneficiis"; literally "poisonings," but in this context meaning magic or witchcraft</note>, of whom it was even fabled that they could draw down the moon with a song, 

<margin>From Horace.</margin>
and call down the stars fixed in the sky; a crime which was also shared with the **Sabines**, as that author <note>The Roman poet Horace</note> reveals. Furthermore, they were said to be inspired by **Bacchus**, 

<margin>From Plutarch</margin>
and they were called **Mimallones** and **Aedonides** <note>various names for the Maenads, the frenzied female followers of the god Bacchus/Dionysus</note>. And they would rage with snakes entwined among their **Thyrsoi** <note>ritual staffs topped with pine cones</note> through magical delusion, held in such veneration that **Olympias**, the mother of **Alexander the Great**, wished to be initiated into their rites. 

<margin>Where the origin of these fables began, against Synesius.</margin>
I think that things which appear to be lies might perhaps have been able to draw their origin from some likeness of the truth. But it seems more probable that from the very wonders of demons—not without some foundation in true history, though shadowed by many vanities—these stories drew some growth, rather than from dreams, as was recorded in writing by **Synesius** <note>Synesius of Cyrene, a philosopher-bishop who wrote a treatise on dreams</note>. For he would not have been so inclined to make the matter public, had he thought he had seen anything during rest, as if he had beheld it outside [with his own eyes]...

<summary>Phronimus discusses the literary and mythological origins of shape-shifting, specifically the "donkey" transformations in Lucian and Apuleius, attributing these tales to the historical reputation of Thessalian witches rather than mere dreams.</summary>

<sig>C ii</sig>
<header>BOOK ONE</header>
<meta>catchword: [unclear]</meta>

# BOOK ONE

...had been discussed with wonder. For so many sorceries have been sung of among the Greeks—<margin>From Virgil. From Theocritus. From the book to Pelianus and Eurydice.</margin> and so many among the Latins. From this source comes that priestess of the Massylian people <note>The Massylii were a North African tribe; the priestess appears in Virgil's Aeneid, Book 4</note>, who promised that she could release minds with her spells: to stop the water in the rivers, to turn back the stars, and to summon the spirits of the night. From this source also come the potions <note>original: "pharmaca"</note> of Circe, Medea, and Canidia, and the love-charms of Simaetha told of by Theocritus the Sicilian, whom Virgil <note>original: "Maro"</note> followed. Shall we think all these things were made up out of nothing? Indeed, I remember reading in Plutarch a story about "drawing down the Moon," a trick discovered by Aglaonice of Thessaly <note>original: "Aganice Thessala"</note>. Since she knew that an eclipse of the Moon was about to occur because of the intervening shadow of the earth, she persuaded the Thessalian women (who were ignorant of such things) that the Moon was being drawn down to earth by her at that time. Other myths can also be traced back to either actual events or to clever trickery. There was among the Greeks a certain man—unless the name escapes me—Palaephatus, who thought it worth the effort to show how a solid foundation of actual history existed for most myths, which was then often distorted into something greater—and always unstable—by the false rumors of the common people. From this, I think, comes that line of Virgil: "The papyrus testifies with the learned voice of Palaephatus." <note>Palaephatus was an author who wrote "On Incredible Tales," seeking rational explanations for myths</note>

Furthermore, it is very well known that it is customary for it to happen through sorceries that men either *became* different (as some idly joked) or *appeared* so. No one can rightly deny this, nor surely with any impudence, unless it seemed to them that the event itself was false. For you remember it was sung that the daughters of Proetus <note>original: "Prętidas"; these princesses were cursed with a madness that made them believe they were cows</note> filled the fields with false lowing, feared the plow, and searched for horns on their smooth brows. Various histories report that they were later healed—just as roses helped the donkey—<margin>From Virgil.</margin> either by the cleansing of Melampus <note>original: "Melampometis"; a legendary seer and healer</note>, as some relate, or (as others prefer) by the medicinal remedies of Aesculapius <note>The god of medicine</note>.

Accordingly, whether they were driven mad by the anger of demons or by a natural disease, antiquity applied various remedies to them. For the demons themselves—at the time when they almost possessed the world through impiety—had a diverse and frequent <margin>The frequent practice of demons to deceive.</margin> method of deceiving, not only through the priests of the temples and through oracles, but through young women agitated by the Pythian breath <note>The "Pythian breath" refers to the inspiration of the Oracle at Delphi</note>. For they drew men into wonder of themselves and plunged them into the precipices of crime under the pretext of religion. To this end, the demons themselves put on various forms. Thus, throughout almost all the poets, one may see them showing themselves in various phantom shapes. Likewise, Philostratus <note>A 3rd-century Greek writer</note> entrusted to posterity in his dialogues that those heroes who fell at Troy appeared visible to a vinedresser. <margin>In the *Heroica* of Philostratus.</margin> Thus it is said to Apollonius of Tyana <note>A famous Neopythagorean philosopher and reputed magician</note> that Empusae and Lamiae <note>Empusae and Lamiae were female supernatural beings or "bogeywomen" who shifted shapes to devour the young</note> [appeared]...

<summary>The author examines how classical myths of magic and transformation often stem from either natural phenomena (like eclipses) or demonic deception. He argues that while some "magic" is human trickery, demons also use illusions and shapeshifting to lead people into sin under the guise of religion.</summary>
<keywords>magic, Thessaly, Aglaonice, Palaephatus, Proetides, demons, illusions, Philostratus, Apollonius of Tyana, metamorphosis</keywords>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>ON WITCHES</header>
<meta>catchword: duties</meta>

...[most firmly] witches seem: when they report back about the "game" <note>original: "ludo" – This refers to the "Game of Diana" or the "Good Society," early terms for what would later be called the Witches' Sabbat</note>, it can indeed happen either through fear or a slip of memory. As they are for the most part uneducated and rustic women, it can also be attributed to the deceptions of Demons, who do not affect everyone in the same way. It is permitted

<margin>
Diverse rites of sorcery and oracles.
</margin>

to discover this in ancient illusions. For the type of sorcery <note>original: "veneficii genus"</note> was one thing in the Euxine <note>The Black Sea region</note>, another in Taurica <note>The Crimea</note>, and another in Italy. Nor is the "Sorceress" <note>original: "Pharmaceutria"</note> of Theocritus <note>A Greek poet</note> entirely similar to the one Virgil sang of. Furthermore, it happened that the same variety was observed in oracles. Some prophecies came from inspired women, others from a foul chasm in the earth, others through dreams sought by men in temples. For that reason, they used to sleep in the temple of Pasiphae. We also read that Calabrian and Daunian doctors once used to lie at the tomb of Podalyrius <note>A son of Aesculapius; this practice of sleeping in a sacred place to receive a vision is called "incubation"</note>. It is well known that many used to sleep in the temple of Aesculapius. This was practiced not only in heroic times, but even up to the times of the Emperor Antoninus, whom Herodian reports sought out the city of Pergamum for that very purpose. We also read that oracles used to be delivered through statues, both whole and halved. Also through doves—whether those were actual birds, or women called by that name who poured out prophecies. Also through trees in both Dodona and India; Alexander the Great reported this as a great marvel. In others, a frenzy was suddenly inspired: and other things of that kind were so varied, as were the auguries and the libations of those who performed sacred rites. For the expectation of an unspeakable rite and an accursed sacrifice is diverse. The incantations are also diverse; those of antiquity, and those which even in our own age swarm from the profane, are performed with a different rite than they used to be by the Romans. Cato the Elder relates some things in his *Books on Agriculture* <note>original: "Libris Rei rusticae"</note> so contemptible that almost no one can be found who can read them without laughter—and yet they were set forth by a man who was a Senator, a Censor, and who celebrated a military triumph. As for the movement by which they are carried by a Demon, 

<margin>
Truth is one; falsehood is manifold.
</margin>

or as for the place where they stop, it should not seem at all strange to you. For that which is deceptive is, by its very nature, often manifold and varied. That which is true relies on simplicity. One may see this in the things we have narrated. It can also be seen in the various and conflicting poetic fictions and often in histories, when they frequently mention double or triple—or even manifold—rumors. This is also found in the opinions of philosophers and in the legal responses which experts bring forth. But you will never observe this in theological records, except insofar as they treat things that are clearly common to both themselves and those whom we have cited.

<summary>The author argues that the inconsistent accounts of witches' activities and ancient oracles stem from the inherently deceptive and "manifold" nature of demons, contrasting this with the singular simplicity of truth. He cites various classical precedents for divination and superstition, including the "incubation" dreams in temples and the folk-charms of Cato the Elder.</summary>
<keywords>witches, striges, demons, oracles, incubation, Theocritus, Virgil, Cato the Elder, Aesculapius, deception, folklore</keywords>

# BOOK ONE
<meta>catchword: duties</meta>

<margin>
What a "Skin-shifter" <note>original: *Versipelle*</note> is.
</margin>
<margin>
Concerning the aerial journeys of the philosophers.
</margin>
<margin>
That Pythagoras and Empedocles departed life by a miserable end.
</margin>
<margin>
From Horace.
</margin>
<margin>
From Laertius <note>Diogenes Laertius, a 3rd-century biographer of Greek philosophers</note>.
</margin>

...duties. For in those matters which properly pertain to themselves, there is no disagreement. That is to say, those precepts or decrees which concern faith and the morals necessary for salvation are harmonious in every respect and delivered with a concordant voice. Therefore, the malignant Demon, a friend of dissension, just as he is lying and deceitful, so he is various and (as I might say) a **Skin-shifter** <note>original: *Versipellis*; a term used for shape-shifters or werewolves</note>. Scholars of the Latin language have used this word, drawn from those fables we have already mentioned, in which men were mockingly betrayed by being transformed into wolves. 

Just as he used to deceive the ancient philosophers under the appearance of doctrine—I speak of **Pythagoras**, **Empedocles**, **Apollonius**, and others of that kind (for he used the very snares by which they could be easily caught)—so in the past he drew away weak women with drinking bouts and lusts, and now he even entices lowly men. It is well known that many philosophers shrank away from such things. Therefore, he enticed those men in manifold ways to worship him under the veil of wisdom and under the pretext of a counterfeit religious superstition. Indeed, through degrees of learning, they devoted themselves to prayers, hymns, and oracles, through which they seemed to achieve a foreknowledge of the future, so that they might be carried through the air to different places. This was done by the service of **Demons**, though those men attributed it to human divinity. 

For in what other way could disciples have watched **Pythagoras** disputing at one moment in Taurominium in Sicily, and then in Metapontum after only a small interval of time? By what path did **Empedocles** travel through the air, or **Abaris**, from which he earned the name **Air-walker** <note>original Greek: *α’ιεροβάτης* (aierobates)</note>? As for **Apollonius** <note>Apollonius of Tyana, a famous Neopythagorean philosopher and reputed miracle-worker</note> having foreknown many things, whoever thinks that he commanded demons is especially deceived. The malignant Demon merely pretended to be troubled by him, so that once Apollonius was enticed by the appearance of a false divinity, he—and others as well—might cling to the Demon more strongly. You may observe this from his end, if you please. 

The Demon wished **Pythagoras** to be consumed first by sedition and then by fire; soon after, he saw to it that he was killed by the sword. He destroyed **Empedocles** with an infamous death, who had reached such a point of madness that he thought he had attained divinity, and would sing: *Farewell, I am to you an immortal god, no longer mortal* <note>original Greek: *Χαιρετ' ε'γω δ’υμμιν θεὸς α’́μβροτος ου’κ ε’́τι θνητὸς*</note>. By this, he indicated to his companions that they should rejoice, for he would not hereafter be a mortal man but an immortal god; and thus (as the poet sang): *While Empedocles desires to be held as an immortal god, he leapt cold-blooded into burning Aetna* <note>original Latin: *Deus immortalis haberi dum cupit Empedocles: ardentem frigidus aetnam insiluit*; a quote from Horace's *Ars Poetica*</note>. But whether he died by this death, or by that which Democritus of Troezen related when he wrote that he died with a noose cast around his neck from a tall...

# ON WITCHES

...[from the peak] having hung from the horn: it must be thought that he departed from life by the prompting of a Demon. For that delusion had not been enough for him, which claimed that his soul had passed into different bodies; whence that poem of his: *For I have already been both a girl and a boy.* <note>original Greek: ἤδη γαρ ποτ’ εγω γενομην κουρη τε κοροσ τε; a fragment from Empedocles’s "Purifications" regarding the transmigration of souls.</note> But he was also enticed to death by demonic voices and the glare of torches, as **Heraclides** <note>Heraclides Ponticus, a Greek philosopher</note> reported. Perhaps he snatched **Apollonius** <note>Apollonius of Tyana, a 1st-century Neopythagorean philosopher and reputed miracle-worker</note> away with his soul at the same time to eternal destruction; a death which seems not unworthy of the magical arts. For various things are told concerning his death. There are those who say he died in Ephesus, others in Crete, others in Rhodes. Neither a tomb nor a cenotaph <note>a monument for someone buried elsewhere</note> existed up to the time of **Philostratus**, even though a vain cult [worshipped him] as a God; a custom which, like other engines of the Demon, perished after a short interval of time.

<margin>
By the arrival of Christ, the princedom of the demon was cast down.
</margin>
Thus the oracles ceased after the arrival of **Christ**, by which almost the entire world was ensnared through the same deceits. But he who used to pour forth oracles openly now chatters in obscure corners, seeking sexual unions: [unions] now infamous among nations, though once glorious. Whence that story of the marriage of **Anchises** and **Venus**, whom she deigned to favor—and not only during that heroic age. But also in the time of **Alexander** and **Scipio**, to whom this glory was added: that many considered them sons of Jove. Indeed, it is better known from history than I should need to report diligently that the Demon Jove (whom they thought was a God) visited Scipio's mother and **Olympia**, the wife of Philip, in the likeness of a snake. Thus he drew those greedy for lust into crime, and even mixed in the disguise of superstition. Indeed, he baited those greedy for glory so that those who had foretold future events through his commerce while they lived would, after death, also pour forth prophecies.

<margin>
How the demon used to delude those greedy for glory.
</margin>
In this way they tell tales of **Orpheus**, who was considered a Prophet while he dwelled among men; after he departed from human affairs, he poured forth oracles, and that head of his, cut off by Thracian women, was washed up at Lesbos; it inhabited a rock and prophesied through the vents of the earth. They also spread oracles of the Prophet **Amphiaraus** and of **Amphilochus**, both while they lived and after they had ceased to remain among the living; which perhaps **Empedocles** strove for when he wished to be considered an immortal God. Likewise, they trifled that kings after death carried out warlike business, just as **Rhesus** in Rhodope was said to handle arms and take part in horses and hunting. To these things they said their souls were summoned forth, not only by those circles and Homeric libations <note>referring to the blood-offerings to the dead described in Homer's *Odyssey*</note>, but by their own will and by certain pacts.

He cites the deaths and myths of figures like Empedocles, Apollonius of Tyana, and Orpheus as examples of demonic manipulation.</summary>
