<meta>This page continues the argument that ancient demonic delusions have lost their power since the birth of Christ. Having discussed the death of the magician Apollonius of Tyana on the previous page, the author now contrasts the "wisdom" of ancient philosophers with the "madness" of modern witches, transitioning into a dialogue between a Judge and an unbeliever.</meta>

### BOOK ONE

...certain people. In what way **Philostratus** reports that **Apollonius of Tyana** <note>original: "Philostratus Tyaneo"; Philostratus was the biographer of the 1st-century wonder-worker Apollonius</note> showed himself as **Achilles**; and to the vine-dresser, showed **Protesilaus** <note>a Greek hero in the Trojan War</note> with the rest of the leaders who had brought war against **Priam**. Their appearances, habits, and deeds are different from those handed down by **Homer**, nor are they consistent with what either **Dares the Phrygian** or **Dictys of Crete** <note>reputed authors of alternative, non-Homeric accounts of the Trojan War</note> committed to history. Let these things warn you how great are the deceits of Demons and the lies introduced into knowledge, and also the harmful delusions imposed upon morals. Therefore, if the Demon deceived those who thought themselves wise, mocking them so that they believed things both contrary and clashing, and utterly abhorrent to reason itself, what cause is there for you to wonder so diligently that in the witches of our time we see many vain things, mostly delusional and self-contradictory?

<margin>On the wondrous power and wisdom of Christ</margin>
Rather, wonder at this: that such was the wisdom and power of **Christ**, that what the malignant Demon persuaded kings, orators, and the philosophers of the nations to believe as something supreme, admirable, and worthy of all wisdom before His arrival, he now scarcely persuades little men and lowly women to do. That is, to worship him and perform the things he has prescribed. What used to happen openly across the whole world among all nations as something glorious, now happens secretly among a few and in tiny corners—and add to this that it is full of disgrace and ignominy. Above all, look upon this as worthy of divine glory: the foundation of the Christian faith is so solid that the malignant Demon does not admit witches to his fellowship unless they have abjured the faith itself, scorned the Sacraments, and trampled upon the saving victim <note>original: "salutarem hostiam," referring to the consecrated bread of the Eucharist</note>. Thus that hater of God and men commands the deserters of our religion to be initiated into his sacrileges. Surely this is because falsehood cannot agree with truth, darkness with light, nor superstition with religion. But now, right now, I think you can be informed of that which we deferred while traveling. Behold, there is a witch talking with the **Judge** <note>Latin: "Dicaste"; from the Greek "dikastes" meaning judge</note> at the vestibule of the temple.

**APISTUS:** <note>A name meaning "Unbeliever" or "Faithless" in Greek</note> Greetings.

**DICASTES:** What news, **Apistus**?

**APISTUS:** We seek that from you, since **Phronimus** <note>A name meaning "Sensible" or "Prudent" in Greek</note> here and I have come hither so that we might hear the witch in your presence—if only it be no trouble—recounting the business of another world.

**WITCH:** <note>Latin: "Strix"</note> Woe is me!

**DICASTES:** Be of good cheer, speak without any fear; do not doubt that, according to these promises, you shall have no harm, provided you candidly bring forth all your crimes, which can in no way remain hidden: you have witnesses...

D

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the characters established on the previous page. The philosopher Phtonimus has been arguing that modern witchcraft is a demonic delusion. Now, the Judge (Dicast) brings forth a self-confessed witch (Strix) to testify, bridging the gap between theoretical debate and legal confession.</meta>

# ON WITCHES

<meta>The catchword "desideratissimi" (most longed-for) appears at the bottom of the page to guide the binder; there is visible ink show-through from the reverse side.</meta>

I am pleased with you, and you especially: for I have greatly desired this—a defendant who confesses. **Witch:** I have spoken. Why do you torture me again? **Judge:** It is necessary to repeat it, not only before two or three witnesses, but before many, and soon before the entire people, if you wish to escape the penalties decreed for you by the laws. You took it upon yourself to do whatever I might command; therefore I promised that I would not hand you over to the Governor <note>original: "praetori"; the Praetor was a high-ranking judicial official</note> who, according to the custom of our ancestors, would decree that you be burned with fire. I command nothing else except that you report what was done with the demons during the flight <note>original: "cursu"; referring to the "night-course" or flight to the Sabbath</note> or in the game <note>original: "ludo"; a common term for the Witches' Sabbath or gathering</note>. **Witch:** Oh, most ruinous game for me! Oh, unhappy woman that I am! **Judge:** There is no need for tears and wailing. **Witch:** I beg you, do not trouble me any more now; I pray for time to collect myself, and then I will report whatever I did. **Judge:** If it pleases you all, I will yield to her wishes. For she will speak with a better spirit and voice if we put off the questions until tomorrow. I most earnestly desire for you to be present then, if the journey does not weary you. **Apuleius:** It was no burden to travel from **Knossos** <note>a city on Crete</note> to the cave and temple of **Jove** for those intending to hear the vain laws of **Minos** and **Lycurgus** <note>famous legendary lawgivers of Crete and Sparta</note>; will it be troublesome for me to travel a single mile <note>original: "mille passus"; literally 'a thousand paces,' the standard Roman mile</note> so that I may learn more closely and certainly those things which now, through the discussion of **Phtonimus**, seem—if not true—at least plausible? **Phtonimus:** I am glad that you have granted so much not to me, but to the truth—or if the truth is not yet clear to you, then at least to the appearance of it. Certainly, for the sake of exercising my body, it will not be unpleasant for me to return here from the town.

->**Judge:** Tomorrow, then, you will come to us as<-
->most welcome guests.<-

<summary>The Judge secures a confession from a witch, promising her a reprieve from execution by fire if she provides a full account of the demonic "game" (Sabbath) the following day. The dialogue participants, including the skeptic Apuleius, agree to return to witness the testimony.</summary>

<image-desc>A large square woodcut decorative initial 'A' featuring classicizing scrolling acanthus foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>
**A**S YOU have arrived just in time, for the Witch herself will very soon be led out of custody. **APISTIUS:** Look, she is being dragged along in chains. **STRIX:** Is this how you keep your promises? Why do you apply torture to a woman who has already confessed? **APISTIUS:** No instruments of torture <note>original: "tormentorum"</note> have been brought here, good woman. Phronimus here and I have come only to see and to hear, and as much as is in our power, to help you. **PHRONIMUS:** That is so. **STRIX:** These handcuffs <note>original: "manicae"; iron hand-shackles</note> and the tight knots of the bonds twist me, and I fear that I may fall back into even harsher tortures. **PHRONIMUS:** You should order the bonds to be loosened. **DICASTES:** I indeed order them to be eased. **STRIX:** I begin to breathe a little. **DICASTES:** Be of good cheer; have confidence that I will not omit any of the things I promised you. Only stand by your promises, and do not break your word in any matter you are asked to explain to us. **STRIX:** I will stand by them, and I will not fail. **DICASTES:** Tell us those things which you confessed to me privately while a notary <note>original: "tabellione"; a legal official who records official testimony</note> was taking them down, both the day before yesterday and yesterday evening. **STRIX:** If you bring back to my memory through your questioning the things you most wish to know, I will answer in that order. **DICASTES:** You ask the questions, Apistius and Phronimus; I grant you leave. For today this spectacle is offered to you. I will stand by and advise her to return to the right path if she swerves from the track.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Judge (Dicastes), the skeptically-minded Apistius, the scholar Phronimus, and the accused Witch. Having just secured a confession, the men now debate the nature of the "assembly" or Sabbat she attends, trying to reconcile her testimony with classical mythology and Church law.</meta>

### ON WITCHES

<margin>The origin of the assembly of Diana and Herodias.</margin>

**APISTIUS:** Have you ever gone to the assembly <note>original: "ludum"; literally "the game," a common term for the nocturnal gathering or Sabbat of witches</note> of Diana or Herodias? **WITCH:** I certainly went to the assembly, but whether it belonged to Diana or Herodias, I never heard. **PHRONIMUS:** I told you yesterday, Apistius, that the Demon deceives in various ways. During the time when Diana was worshipped by the pagans throughout the world, her name was famous and celebrated; then it was seen as a glorious thing to be numbered among Diana’s companions. Although they were said to be virgins, they were also called nymphs. They were pleased with the title of "bride," but they preferred the reality of it, even though it was not performed through lawful ritual. For debauchery and adultery were frequent there. This is why that phrase is repeated so often in the poems of Homer: "mingled in love" <note>original: "ἐν φιλότητι μιγεῖσσα"; a recurring phrase in Homer used to describe sexual union, often between a god and a mortal.</note> when they told fables of how those deceptive beings—whether gods or heroes—overpowered either a companion of Diana or some other nymph, be it a Napead, an Oread, or a Dryad <note>Types of nature spirits: Napeads of the dells, Oreads of the mountains, and Dryads of the trees.</note>. It was also accepted by the pagans and confirmed by common consensus that sea and river nymphs were given to such loves. For you will often read of Cyrene, Leucothea, Cymodoce, and other false goddesses of the rivers or the sea. However, because it appeared more healthful to be carried up into the mountains than to be submerged in the waves, and more pleasing to spend time in Diana's hunts than in the waves of the Tritons <note>sea deities</note>, they fell in love with Diana’s assembly and dances as being more enticing. Later, the Demon enticed others under the guise of Herodias the Idumean <note>The biblical figure who danced for the head of John the Baptist; in medieval folklore, she and Diana were often leaders of the "Wild Hunt."</note>, who delighted in dances. **DICASTES:** Mention of this assembly of Diana or Herodias is also found in the pontifical decrees, where

<margin>Whether the "course" or flight of witches is the same as that mentioned in the *Canon Episcopi*, 26, question 5.</margin>

the ruling of a council is recited, by which it is ordained that sorceresses <note>original: "magas"</note> be cast out. **PHRONIMUS:** Do you think, Dicastes, that this is the same assembly? **DICASTES:** There are those who assert it is; there are others who prefer to call it a new heresy. **PHRONIMUS:** I would plainly believe that it partly reflects that ancient one, and is partly swarming with new superstitions—as if you were to say it is the ancient assembly in its "essence," but recent in its "accidents" <note>Scholastic philosophical terms: the "essence" is the fundamental nature of a thing, while "accidents" are its non-essential characteristics like time, place, or outward appearance.</note> (to speak in the manner of the modern scholars). **DICASTES:** You have devised an excellent distinction, which can resolve many things hanging on the same point. It is from this that some have seized upon a notable cause for error, thinking that these poor women are always carried to the assembly only in their minds and imagination, and not in their bodies. **APISTIUS:** So you think witches are always physically transported to the assembly? **DICASTES:** Not in every case, since sometimes they have been found upon a beam, weighed down by such a deep lethargy <note>original: "veterno"; a death-like stupor or trance</note> that they did not feel blows. At other times, they are found with broomsticks <note>original: "scopis"</note> pressed between their thighs with such force that they could not be pulled away from the sleepers—on which broomsticks they were carried...

<meta>catchword: ...carried [away]</meta>

<summary>The characters debate the origins of the "Witches' Sabbat," linking it to ancient myths of Diana and the nymphs, while discussing whether the witches' travel is a physical reality or a demonic illusion.</summary>
<keywords>Diana, Herodias, Nymphs, Sabbat, Canon Episcopi, scholasticism, essence and accidents, broomsticks, demonic illusion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the debate between the skeptically-minded Apistius and the scholar Phronimus. ### BOOK TWO

[...on a staff] <note>The fragment "pis ferri" likely refers to "stipite ferri," meaning to be carried on a staff or stick, a common motif in witchcraft confessions.</note> they think they are being carried. **APISTIUS:** What do you think the reason is that sometimes they are carried in the body, and yet at other times, while they claim they are being carried, they are present at the assembly <note>original: "ludo"; literally "the game," a term frequently used for the Sabbat.</note> by imagination alone? **PHRONIMUS:** Sometimes it is a trick of the Demon and a crafty delusion, and sometimes it is the actual gathering of the witches themselves. 
<margin>Heinrich and Jacob, German Inquisitors</margin>
Indeed, I remember it was recorded in writing by Heinrich and Jacob, the German theologians <note>Refers to Heinrich Kramer (Institoris) and Jacob Sprenger, authors of the infamous 1487 witch-hunting manual, the *Malleus Maleficarum*.</note>. They wrote that a certain witch said she could travel across those distances in whichever way she wished: that is, either in her waking body, or sometimes by imagination alone when she was weary of the journey. In that case, while lying in bed and reciting certain ready, accursed words, the images of the whole assembly were represented to her in a sort of **blue cloud**, as if they were being performed on a stage. **PHRONIMUS:** What would you answer to the opponents? **DICASTES:** 
<margin>On explaining the Canon *Episcopi*, Case 26, Question 5</margin>
I would wonder, first, why—based on a single method of travel observed at one time in one specific region of the world—they wish to judge every mode of sacrilege, superstition, and magical vanity practiced by any group of sacrilegious women, and extend that judgment to every corner of the earth. Furthermore, they believe themselves so wise that they would compress the vast power of the Demon, which he has possessed since his very creation, into a single old pattern. Next, they do not allow that text <note>The "Canon Episcopi," a medieval legal text which claimed that the "ride of Diana" was a delusion. Skeptics used it to argue that all witchcraft was imaginary.</note> to be explained in the manner of others who possess much greater authority, so that they might separate things belonging to nature from those concerning the Catholic faith. Finally, they openly wish to claim that something does not exist, even though they cannot deny its possibility without great ignorance, nor can they deny that it has actually happened without great impudence—not when they are contradicted by the testimony of **myriads of witnesses**. And perhaps someone bolder than I would say that he desires a more faithful copy of the Council's decree and greater authority for the person who reported it. For in the work of **Gratian** <note>Johannes Gratian, the 12th-century father of Canon Law. His "Decretum" was the standard legal textbook, but the author here questions its absolute authority.</note>, many things are inverted; perhaps for this reason among others, his compilation has never been so universally approved that it was received as a law which it would be a sin to protest. But (even if I grant everything), the opponent’s mouth seems closed by your distinction, which shows that the conduct of our local "little women" and "little men" <note>original: "muliercularū homūtionūq̃"; diminutive terms often used to disparage the uneducated common folk accused of witchcraft.</note> partly agrees with that old assembly, and is partly different from it. For Diana is not invoked here, nor is she believed to be a Goddess of the Pagans, nor are any of those other things seen that were condemned by the Council in that region. And nevertheless, many things happen here that are never recorded as happening there: things common both to the superstitions of other nations and to the games of wicked Demons...

The Judge (Dicastes) argues against skeptics who use the Canon Episcopi to dismiss all witchcraft as a delusion, suggesting the law is outdated or misapplied to modern cases.</summary>

# ON WITCHES

...among pernicious ointments, in the innocent blood of children, in the circus, in magic incantations <note>original: "catamine," likely a corruption of "cantamine," meaning a rhythmic chant or spell</note>, in many wicked deeds, and in bodily departure through the spaces of the air. These movements are beyond human strength; anyone who denies that such things can be done by a Demon would be subjected, I believe, to interrogation as one accused of a religious crime. For it is written in that sacred book by that most holy man from the land of Uz <note>Refers to the Book of Job; specifically Job 41:33, "Upon earth there is not his like, who is made without fear."</note> that there is no power on earth that can be compared to the demon. It is also written in the Gospel that Christ the Lord was placed upon a mountain and upon the pinnacle of the temple <note>Refers to the Temptation of Christ, where the Devil physically moved Jesus to high places.</note>; it is a settled matter among all theologians that bodies, as far as movement from place to place is concerned, obey the will of spirits separated from matter <note>i.e., demons and angels</note>. This is a dispute of law <term>iuris disceptatio</term>; the other is a dispute of fact <term>facti</term>, concerning whether these things actually happen or not. For when it is established that something *can* happen, whether it *did* happen is learned only through witnesses. Of these, we have countless numbers. 

**PHRONIMUS:** It is no wonder that they rave from the same context out of which others understand the truth. For just as God brings good out of evil, so perverted men attempt to bring evil out of good; thus all heresies arise from the malice of men and the Sacred Scriptures, though it is not the fault of the Scriptures. 

**APISTIUS:** I beg you, do not interrupt my questioning. For I had intended to ask about these things afterward. 

**DICASTES:** Ask her yourself.

**APISTIUS:** Did you set out for the **play** <note>original: "ludum"; a common term for the Sabbat, implying a festive but forbidden gathering</note> in spirit or in body? 

**WITCH:** Both in spirit and in body. 

**APISTIUS:** What name is given to this play? 

**WITCH:** It is called **The Game of the Lady** <note>original: "Dominae ludus"; likely referring to the "Lady of the Game," a figure often identified with Diana or Herodias in folklore</note> by our peers and companions. 

**APISTIUS:** In what manner did you go? 

**WITCH:** I was carried; I did not walk. 

**APISTIUS:** On what? 

**WITCH:** On the bench of a flax-dresser <note>A common household tool used to beat flax into fibers; folklore often claimed witches used everyday domestic items as mounts</note>. 

**APISTIUS:** How can it be that such a thing is carried when no one is carrying it? 

**WITCH:** My lover carried it. 

**APISTIUS:** And who was he? 

**WITCH:** Louis. 

**APISTIUS:** Perhaps a man who is strong in that name? 

**WITCH:** Not a man, but a malignant Demon who presented himself in the image of a man, whom I thought was a god. 

**APISTIUS:** I am amazed that a Demon, the hater of all men, would adopt this Christian name for himself. 

**PHRONIMUS:** Do you wonder that he, who even transforms himself into an angel of light, would take a name brought down to us from the Gentiles? 

**APISTIUS:** From the Gentiles, you say? 

**PHRONIMUS:** From the Gentiles, I say. For unless I am mistaken, you will never find that name derived from either the Greek or Latin language by example or origin. I only read of it as "Lithuanian" in the commentaries of Julius Caesar, from which "Luy-sius" is slightly distorted in the French tongue, and later "Ludovicus" <note>Louis</note> in Latin, to which he refers. 

**APISTIUS:** I will not argue the point, especially at this time, when my conversation is set with our witch...
<meta>catchword: set</meta>

<summary>This page argues that demons have the physical power to transport people, citing the Book of Job and the Temptation of Christ as evidence. # BOOK TWO

...established. **PHRONIMUS:** I have said what I think; I am ready to listen to one who is wiser. **APISTIUS:** But I beg you, good sorceress <note>original: "strix," a word for a screech-owl or a legendary flesh-eating bird, commonly used to mean a witch</note>, sincerely reveal <note>original: "padas," likely "pandas"</note> your loves to us. **STRIX:** What do you wish to find out? **APISTIUS:** Did he appear to be a man to you?

<margin>
Why the Demon is seen with goose feet, and many and varied reasons for it.
</margin>
**STRIX:** He was a man except for his feet, which always seemed to be borrowed from a goose; he always wore them backward <term>inuersos</term> so that he left his footprints behind him. **APISTIUS:** What do you think is the reason that he resembled a man in his face and the rest of his limbs, but imitated a goose in his feet? **DICASTES:** You will read this in all the books of legal inquiries <term>libellis quaestionum</term>: that whether you call him the Devil, a Demon, or Satan, he presents the image of a man except for the feet. I have often wondered at this and guessed the reason to be that he is not permitted to perfectly mimic the human form from top to bottom <note>literally "out of the whole," implying God limits his power to deceive</note>. As to why the feet, specifically, do not match the rest of the illusory body, it might be because "affections" <note>the desires or movements of the soul</note> are usually designated by "feet" in mystical language <term>mysticis eloquiis</term>. This is why he wears them reversed and backward. But as for why he chose to fashion his gait after a goose rather than other animals, I confess it is entirely unknown to me, unless there is some more hidden property in the goose that can be adapted to his malice. However, I do not

<margin>
Aristotle
</margin>
remember such a thing being observed by Aristotle; rather, he attributes a sense of shame <note>original: "verecundiam"</note> to that kind of bird, if I recall correctly. **PHRONIMUS:** It is possible that the public enemy of the human race wished to scatter certain other hidden superstitions from ancient peoples. In the past, under the image of Inachus and Inachis <note>Inachis refers to Io, the daughter of Inachus, who was often identified by the ancients with the Egyptian goddess Isis</note>, sacred rites were performed using a goose; hence those lines:

<margin>
From Ovid
</margin>
"Neither does the defended Capitol help the goose from giving its liver to your platters, elegant Inachis." <note>original: "Nec defensa iuuant capitolia quominus Anser, Det iecur in laces Inache laute tuas." This refers to the geese that saved the Roman Capitol from a night attack by Gauls, yet were still sacrificed to the gods.</note> Or as some prefer to read: "to the platters of your cow, Inachus" <note>referring to Io being transformed into a heifer</note>.

<margin>
Pliny
</margin>
Pliny is the authority for the fact that a goose's liver is usually given to Inachus, the god of the Argive river, for that bird delights in water. That he proves this regarding Inachis is because we learn from the history of Herodotus

<margin>
Herodotus
</margin>
that Egyptian priests were accustomed to eating goose meat. There, Isis—who was considered to be Diana—was worshipped with great superstition. Furthermore, the goose is "more watchful than a dog," as that author says, and it disturbs the silence of the night with remarkable ease. They used to think that Diana presided over that night. Perhaps the Demon took the image of that bird's feet to indicate that he is a wide-awake bird and goes without sleep when intent on guarding his work. Thus, one must hasten to that game <note>the witches' Sabbat</note>, spend the night in it, and devote oneself to its pleasures. It is also said that some part of this bird excites women to lust. It could also be a sign of some more hidden and monstrous love.

<summary>This page discusses the folkloric and demonological claim that the Devil appears with goose feet. The speakers analyze this through theological symbolism (feet representing the soul's desires) and classical mythology (linking the goose to Isis, Diana, and ancient sacrificial rites).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous page where Dicastes was explaining the symbolic and mystical reasons why demons are often depicted with goose feet. He continues to cite classical authorities to explain the unnatural behavior and appearances of these entities.</meta>

### ON WITCHES

<warning>Some marginalia on the left are extremely faded and nearly illegible.</warning>

<margin>Against Pliny</margin>
Since it has been handed down to memory that geese have sought out the lust of a different species—a fact well known in Pliny <note>original: "Pliniū"; referring to Pliny the Elder’s *Natural History*</note> regarding a boy named Olenus from Argos, and Glaucus, the cithara-player of King Ptolemy. It is recorded that geese were captured by love for them; in which passage I believe Pliny’s memory failed him,
<margin>Theophrastus</margin>
because Theophrastus <note>a Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle</note> reported in his book on love that the boy was not named Olenus, but was Amphilochus by name and Olenian by homeland. Nor is it entirely absurd that since goose-palms <note>the webbed part of the feet</note> were once considered delicacies of the table, it was meant to signify the most exquisite feasts of Delian Messae, to be preferred even over the "Table of the Sun" in Ethiopia—at which table one does not read of goose feet being served, which Messalinus Cotta <note>a famous Roman gourmet</note> had not yet thought to roast. These explanations please me more than the idea that geese should be celebrated for wisdom because they are reported to have consorted with the philosopher Lacydes even in the baths. For I would more willingly consider this kind of intimacy similar to that with which Ajax the Locrian consorted with a Dragon <note>or a large serpent</note>. Nor does it shrink from that familiar voice which buzzed around Socrates' ears <note>referring to Socrates' *daimonion* or inner spirit</note>, or from that voice which is recorded in memory in the poem as having prophesied to the sons of Atreus and Laomedon—the poem transmitted under the title *On Stones* attributed to Orpheus. Nor is it entirely contrary to reason that we think it indicates the speed of travel by which they are carried to the "game" <note>original: "ludū," referring to the Sabbat or witches' gathering</note>, since we read of no bird having completed such a journey on foot as the geese, of whom Pliny is the authority that they came on foot from the Morini <note>a people of Belgic Gaul</note> all the way to Rome.

**PHRONIMUS:** Hey you, did he ever display his feet in any other form than, so to speak, "goose-like," when he came to you?
**STRIX:** Never any other.
**APISTIUS:** In what manner did he approach?
**STRIX:** Both when called by me, and often of his own accord.
**APISTIUS:** Always in human form?
**STRIX:** Never except human while he sought my intercourse <term>concubitum</term>.
**APISTIUS:** Oh, what a coupling for a wrinkled old woman!
**STRIX:** Woe is me, woe is me!
**DICASTES:** Why are you terrified?
**STRIX:** Look, look!
**DICASTES:** Where?
**STRIX:** At the wall.
**DICASTES:** In what form?
**STRIX:** Of a sparrow.
**DICASTES:** Now he has taken the likeness of a most lustful bird <note>sparrows were symbols of lechery in the Renaissance</note>, not shrinking from the conversation of a lewd woman who surpasses all monsters of lust.
**APISTIUS:** I wonder why no one besides her sees this appearance of a sparrow.
**DICASTES:** Indeed, no one sees it.
**APISTIUS:** Truly a marvel.
**PHRONIMUS:** Why do you marvel, when you do not marvel at the ring of that Lydian shepherd named Gyges <note>a mythical ring that granted invisibility</note>, celebrated even by Plato and Cicero?
<meta>Catchword: DICASTES</meta>

<summary>Dicastes uses classical and mythological precedents to explain the demonic association with geese and sparrows, while the Strix claims to see a demon in bird-form on the wall, invisible to the interrogators.</summary>
<keywords>Pliny the Elder, Theophrastus, goose feet, animal lust, Socrates' daemon, Orpheus, Gyges' ring, demonic apparition, interrogation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Dicastes, Philodoxus, and Apotropaeus, who are now joined by the "Strix" (the accused witch). They are discussing the nature of spiritual visions—specifically why some entities are visible to certain individuals but remain hidden from others.</meta>

## BOOK TWO

E

<meta>catchword: Daily</meta>

<margin>That a spirit in an assumed body may be seen by some but not at all by others.</margin>
DICASTES. It happens not only in the sightings of malignant demons, but also in divine miracles, that things occurring openly are sometimes seen only by a few. To pass over other examples: that light standing at St. Martin’s <note>St. Martin of Tours (c. 336–397), a popular saint in the Middle Ages</note> head was perceived by very few, as Sulpicius Severus <note>a contemporary biographer of St. Martin</note> discusses. And only Paulinus <note>Paulinus of Milan, secretary and biographer of St. Ambrose</note> beheld that light shining upon Ambrose while he was writing a commentary. But as for why this present phantom of a Demon was seen by the witch <note>original: "strige"; meaning a witch or a malevolent supernatural being</note> alone: I can easily trace the cause to the friendship <note>referring to the "demonic pact" or intimate bond</note> she has established with him. It results in not only her eyes, but an imaginary power, being drawn by a certain habit toward the one she loves. Three days ago, trembling, she reported that she beheld her lover himself, a spirit wrapped in the form of a snake.

PHILODOXUS. Thus among the pagans they appeared in the form of a snake. Thus also in the species of birds. For you have read that ravens were the guides for the journey of Alexander when he sought the oracle of Ammon <note>referring to Alexander the Great’s trek to the Siwa Oasis</note>.

APOTROPAEUS. Certainly, and dragons too, if I remember what I read correctly.

PHILODOXUS. Now, do you suspect those were demons in the shape of ravens? What about those twin ravens—which Aristotle <note>referring to the *History of Animals* or pseudepigrapha</note> counts among the wonders—
<margin>Aristotle.</margin>
that flew around the temple of Jupiter in Caria? Why does it seem strange to you, when you have read in Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, *Natural History*</note> about the wandering soul of Hermotimus of Clazomenae, and that the soul of Aristeas of Proconnesus was accustomed to exit his mouth in the form of a raven? They babbled that this was his soul—though it was not visible to everyone, but only to certain people. You would wonder less if you knew what was handed down by Aristotle and repeated by others concerning that man of Thasos.

APOTROPAEUS. Tell me, I pray, what is said to have happened to him.

PHILODOXUS. His own image <note>original: "simulachrū"; a double or phantom</note> repeatedly appeared before his eyes, which was not at all apparent to the sight of other men.

APOTROPAEUS. Therefore, can it be believed without lightheadedness that the dying, as they say, sometimes see good and evil spirits in assumed bodies—spirits whom others do not perceive?

PHILODOXUS. Why not? Since it has been both believed by so many illustrious men and committed to writing for the memory of posterity.

APOTROPAEUS. Has the fear receded?

WITCH. It recedes because of your conversation and your presence.

APOTROPAEUS. Do you dread your lover so much?

WITCH. Once, I did not fear him. But after I was placed in custody and confessed our loves against his will, he terrifies me in a strange way. Standing sometimes at the small door of the prison and the tiny window, he rebukes me; he promises with his thumb <note>likely a gesture of favor or a sign of a secret oath</note> that he will be of help if I remain more stubbornly silent.

APOTROPAEUS. When you were heading to the game <note>original: "ludum"; a common term for the Sabbat or witches' gathering</note>, did he never strike any fear into you?

WITCH. Nothing at all.

APOTROPAEUS. Daily

The dialogue shifts to the witch's personal testimony about being harassed by this spirit while in prison.</summary>

# ON WITCHES

APOTROPAEUS. Were you going there? Or at fixed times?

STR. That night: which was the second after the Sabbath <note>original: "sabbatum"; in this context, it likely refers to the witches' Sabbath, though the speaker specifies it was two nights ago</note>, and today is the fourth day since then.

APOTROPAEUS. Did you ever go to the gathering <note>original: "ludum"; literally "the game" or "the play," a common contemporary term for the witches' Sabbath or ritual meeting</note> during the day?

STR. Never.

PHILODOXUS. From this you may recognize the remnants of ancient superstition, if you recall Hecate <note>A Greek goddess associated with magic, night, and crossroads</note> being wailed for at night at the crossroads throughout the cities—she who is also named Diana and the Moon. <margin>Pindar</margin> It is recorded by Pindar <note>An ancient Greek lyric poet</note> that women were accustomed to pour out prayers to her, while men used to pray to her alone concerning matters of love. For the night was dedicated to conversations of this kind, and as day approached, an end was imposed upon them. Hence that saying: *And the cruel rising sun has breathed upon me with his panting horses* <note>original: "Et me saevus equis oriens afflavit anhelis"; a quote from Virgil's Georgics describing the arrival of dawn</note>.

APOTROPAEUS. Perhaps there is another more hidden business beneath this.

PHILODOXUS. What?

APOTROPAEUS. That which Menander <margin>Menander</margin> mentions: <term>For in you, most of all, is Aphrodite</term> <note>original: "ὦ γὰρ ἐ σοὶ γὰρ δεί- πλεῖ τον ἀφροδίτης"; a Greek phrase suggesting that lust or desire is the primary motivator behind these nocturnal activities</note>.

DICASTES. Both of you argue skillfully, but from a human perspective. I shall bring forth an oracle proceeding from divinity—not some vain prophecy of Apollo, but one from Truth itself, and from God who became human. <margin>The Gospel</margin>

APOTROPAEUS. Bring it forth.

DICASTES. *He who does evil hates the light.* <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 3:20</note>

PHILODOXUS. That is indeed most true.

APOTROPAEUS. But you, good Witch, why did you not attend the dances of Diana or Herodias <note>In medieval and Renaissance folklore, Diana and Herodias were often cited as the mythological leaders of the "society of the night" or the witches' flight</note> on other nights, or the dances of her whom you call "the Mistress"? To speak more clearly: why, through the deception of demons—for I have learned from the Christian religion that Diana is no Goddess, nor do I believe Herodias or unclean spirits have enough power to rule over a human—why, I say, did you not attend, or why did it seem you were attending?

STR. I do not know.

APOTROPAEUS. Were you preparing yourself, or were you waiting for him who would carry you?

STR. Having made a circle, I would anoint myself and ride a stool. From there I was lifted up and carried through the air to the gathering. Sometimes I would tread upon a consecrated host <note>original: "hostiam consecratam"; the bread of the Eucharist, used here in an act of ritual desecration</note> within the circle, and immediately Ludovicus <note>The name of the witch's specific demonic familiar or lover</note> would be there, whom I used as I pleased.

APOTROPAEUS. What was that ointment?

STR. It was made for the most part from the blood of infants.

APOTROPAEUS. What part of you did you anoint?

STR. Ah, I am ashamed to speak of it.

APOTROPAEUS. O most shameless harlot of demons! You are ashamed to say what you were not ashamed to do.

STR. Is it any wonder?

APOTROPAEUS. Pour out your poison, you viper! What were you anointing?

STR. The parts I use for sitting.

APOTROPAEUS. You have reported that most modestly. But I long to know: how much time passed between leaving your home and arriving at the meeting of the gathering?

STR. A small amount.

APOTROPAEUS. How small?

STR. Less than half an hour.

APOTROPAEUS. How high were you from the earth when you were carried?

STR. The height of a proper tower.

APOTROPAEUS. But what happened...

# BOOK TWO
## E ii

...at the gathering: I would very much like to know why, good Witch <note>original: "Strix"; a term for a malevolent mythological bird or witch believed to fly at night</note>: if you wish for me to come to your aid, do not shrink from relating whatever was done there, and as if representing your game <note>original: "ludum"; the common term for the witches' Sabbath or ritual assembly</note> to me.

**STR.** When we had come to the Jordan <note>The River Jordan in the Middle East</note>.

**AP.** The Jordan? What do I hear?

<margin>Concerning the journey to the Jordan. A question.</margin>

**PH.** I believe this journey to the Jordan, completed in such a small interval of time, is a demonic lie; for so that he [the Devil] might impose greater delusions upon these poor little women, he keeps them entangled in a tighter bond by using the names of magnificent places sought out for this purpose. It is by no means to be admitted that a human body could be carried from Italy to Asia in half an hour. But perhaps Satan snatched a bit of local color for his story from the fact that Herodias <note>A biblical figure often associated in medieval folklore with the "Wild Hunt" or leading the witches' flight</note> lived there. But I wonder why he does not falsely claim they went into Scythia <note>An ancient region in Central Eurasia, often associated by the Greeks with wildness and human sacrifice</note> to the altar of Diana. Which perhaps would have been done by him if the name of Scythia were as familiar to them as the Jordan—which everyone knows who remembers hearing the Gospel recited in holy houses. Nor is that Tauric <note>Referring to the land of the Tauri (modern Crimea), where myths say Diana demanded the sacrifice of strangers</note> altar—not sacred, but a sacrilege of most monstrous slaughter—suitable for a "game." By some stroke of luck, he leads them to another neighboring river. Yet there are some who confess they are carried not to waters, but to the peaks of mountains.

**DICA.** It does not seem impossible to me to be carried to the Jordan at least within the space of two hours, as it generally seems to be agreed upon among the witches themselves, as heard from their own mouths.

**PH.** Do you think, then, that they traverse so much distance as lies between this country of ours and the land of Syria and Phoenicia?

**DICA.** A Demon can move bodies at will.

**PH.** But it does not follow that he moves them in such a little time, whether they are carried over the land toward Illyria and Thrace on the left, or on the right through the land of Africa, or straight across the great Ionian and Aegean seas—looking down upon Corfu, the Peloponnese, the Cyclades, Rhodes, and Cyprus—to settle on the bank of the Jordan.

**DICA.** What prevents it?

**PH.** Your own doctors <note>referring to doctors of theology or Church authorities</note>.

**DICA.** In what way do they prevent it?

**PH.** In the way that
<margin>St. Thomas</margin>
Thomas <note>Saint Thomas Aquinas (c. 1225–1274), the influential Dominican friar and philosopher whose "Summa Theologiae" set the standard for Scholastic thought</note> forbids that the whole mass of the earth could be moved from its place by a Demon, with nature itself resisting; by which it is forbidden that the entire order of things and elements be divided and destroyed. For it is against the nature of the human body that it be carried with such speed, whereby it would simultaneously be preserved and perish. For they live, though it would be necessary for them to die by the force of the air, since its nature is not changed and would be a great hindrance. If it [the air] were thinned out, it would easily turn into fire; if it were dense...

<summary>Philodoxus and Dicastes debate whether witches physically fly to the River Jordan, with Philodoxus arguing that such a journey is a demonic hallucination because physical travel at such speeds would violate the laws of nature as described by Thomas Aquinas.</summary>

# CONCERNING WITCHES
## DE STRIGIBVS

<margin>
Aristotle.
Philoponus.
John Scotus.
</margin>
...it would make it [the flight] more difficult: it would hinder the course with a greater effort of speed than if you moved the entire air with your thought. Even as Aristotle <note>The ancient Greek philosopher whose physics dominated medieval thought</note> established the heavens, so also Philoponus <note>John Philoponus, a 6th-century commentator on Aristotle</note> among the Greeks and John Scotus <note>Likely referring to the philosopher John Duns Scotus or John Scottus Eriugena</note> among his own followers would still stand against you. They would object based on the internal nature of quantity, by which a body must be carried through that great void <note>inane; a vacuum</note>, filled with no air, behind itself; and thus from here to Asia, with every obstacle of resisting wind excluded, it would take much more time than they say is spent.

**AP.** I beg you to put off these subtleties to be examined on another day; explain the game <note>original: "ludum"; the ritual gathering</note>.

**STR.** When we arrived there, we immediately saw the Mistress sitting with her lover.

**AP.** Who was he?

**STR.** I do not know. I only know that he was a beautiful man, dressed in a golden garment.

**AP.** Continue.

**STR.** There we brought consecrated hosts <note>original: "hostias consecratas"; the bread used in the Eucharist, believed by Catholics to be the literal body of Christ</note> to the Lady. Then, having received them with a grateful spirit and a cheerful face, she ordered them to be placed upon a bench, to be trampled underfoot, and to be drenched with urine.

**AP.** Who gave these to you to be carried to the game?

**STR.** Bernius the priest, who was born in this town.

**DICA.** The most wicked and worthless of all men whom I have ever known or read of being known by others! I judged this man—caught in a thousand shames—to be stripped of his holy orders and handed over to the prefect, who immediately carried out the punishment decreed by the laws.

**AP.** Do not stop the speech you have begun.

**STR.** We ate, we drank, we slept together. What more do you desire to know?

**AP.** I want you to explain in parts: what did you eat?

**STR.** Meats and other things that are usually sought for food.

**AP.** From where did you get them?

**STR.** We kill oxen, but they return to life. We also take wine from the vessels of those whom we hold in hatred for our carousing. Then each woman summons her own Demon to satisfy her lust, and any of the men among the demons place a female friend beneath them.

**DICA.** They see delusions regarding the oxen.

**PH.** These things are similar to those stories that man told.

**AP.** Which ones?

**PH.**

### BOOK TWO

<margin>From Ovid.</margin> "Bringing back the memorable spoil of the Libyan monster <note>original: "Libyci spolium referens memorabile monstri"; referring to Perseus carrying the head of Medusa</note>. He cleaved the thin air with his clanging wings." **AP.** But if you yourself accept the flying witch <term>Strix|A mythological owl-like creature or witch thought to fly at night and harm children</term>, why do you smirk when you read that "the Persean wings carried the Parrhasian <note>Arcadian; a reference to Ovid’s poetic description of the mythic flight</note>?" **PH.** I am not laughing—at least, not if you believe these things were done by the art of demons. But if you think it was by the power or ingenuity of humans, I do indeed laugh. Nor do I think it is a similar monstrosity for wings to be fashioned for a man or a horse to fly, or for a horse to have a tongue to shape its voice into words. <margin>From Ovid.</margin> <margin>Concerning the articulate voices of animals.</margin> Since it happens by no miracle that many small birds return articulate voices to which they have become accustomed, how much more easily can the tongue of a pack animal be so turned by a spiritual substance <note>meaning a demon or angel</note>, whether evil or good, so that speech is produced? **AP.** You say that it is possible? **PH.** Why not? Since the nature of the matter is the same. **AP.** Could you confirm that with an example? **PH.** It is stored in the unique holy book of Numbers, when it is written that the ass of the diviner Balaam <note>original: "Ariolobalaæ"; referring to the biblical story of Balaam’s ass in Numbers 22</note> spoke. And it is passed down by theologians that this was done by the help of an Angel. Although the beast herself did not know what she was saying, her tongue was guided to express that which was beneficial to the army of the Hebrews, over whom it is recorded that a good spirit presided. This is to say nothing of what the histories of the pagan nations report about speaking oxen. **AP.** We know that neither bones nor flesh are in demons; how do they eat and how do they mate? **STR.** They are clothed in parts that resemble flesh and bones, but they are thicker than anything belonging to mortals. **AP.** Could you compare it to anything? To give us a sign by which you might suggest a similarity for us to recognize the limbs? **STR.** I do not know, except that they are thicker than human limbs, and softer—like dense, compacted, and tightened hemp <note>original: "stuppæ"</note>, and clearly similar to *bombasio* <term>bombasio|A medieval term for cotton or padding, often used in garments</term>. **AP.** I understand hemp, Phronimus, but I do not understand *bombasio*. **PH.** I believe he meant a woody down <note>original: "Xylinam... lanuginem"; likely referring to the cotton plant</note>. **STR.** I want it to be understood as that material which is usually stuffed into quilts <note>original: "Lodicibus"</note>. **AP.** I have the idea, continue. **STR.** When the lust was satisfied, we were carried back home. **AP.** Did he ever visit you there? **STR.** Often; and sometimes he accompanied me when I went to and from the market. And I remember a day when I had left the town in the evening to return home; he coupled with me three times before we reached the threshold. **AP.** How far is your house from the walls? **STR.** About a thousand paces <note>approximately one mile</note>. **AP.** Therefore he took on the phantom appearance of a sparrow. But it truly cannot come into my mind what this coupling is intended for.

### ON WITCHES

**PH.** So that those "little wolves" <note>original: "lupulis"; likely a derogatory term for those driven by animalistic lust</note> might be satisfied. If indeed they can be satisfied; for they are said to be exhausted by their acts, but never sated. **AP.** Likewise, I cannot grasp in my mind where their pleasure comes from. **DI.** They report being flooded with such intense pleasure that they claim nothing on earth is like it. I suspect at first glance that this happens for several reasons. First, the form that those deserting spirits <note>fallen angels or demons</note> take for themselves is most pleasing. Next, they possess an extraordinary size of limbs; for these both soothe the eyes and fill the more hidden parts. Furthermore, they pretend to be captivated by love for the women themselves, and nothing is more pleasing to these wretched "marvels" <note>the accused witches</note> than to be loved. They are perhaps also able to stir something internally so that they are more vividly perceived than your human lovers. I believe it happens no differently for those who abuse <term>succubus|A demon in female form believed to have sexual intercourse with men</term> demons. Indeed, that most wicked priest whom I mentioned 

<margin>The crimes of a most wicked priest.</margin>
before used to say that he was more delighted by intercourse with a demon who had been given the name Armellina than by all the women he had ever slept with. And lest you think he had known only a few, he even had carnal knowledge of his own sister and was said to have fathered a son by her. That fact is certainly recorded in the legal depositions. That wretched man was of such a leaning heart toward this love, and burned with such great desire for Armellina, that he often had her as a companion in the marketplace while others could not see her. At her command, he even sent home "unwashed" <note>original: "illotos"; meaning he did not perform the actual water rite of baptism</note> infant boys whom he had received to be baptized according to the rite of the church. He would also lift an unconsecrated host for the people to worship, simulating the gestures and words of the sacrament to hide his impiety. If he ever did properly consecrate the bread, he would turn his feet in contempt against the image <note>the crucifix or holy image usually pressed into the wafer</note> printed there; then he would lift it high and put it away to be given to the witches, so it could be carried to the "game" <note>original: "ludum"; a common historical term for the Witches' Sabbat or gathering</note>. That demonic love was the cause of so many wicked crimes.

In the same madness, another man reports a demon named Florina. He loves her so ardently that he told me several times while I was questioning him that he would rather die than leave that "most beautiful woman" (as he calls her), in whose company and charms he has delighted for forty years. He reached such a point of insanity that he does not believe there is any other God. This man is still kept in prison, in case he might eventually wish to repent. But do not think they only commit crimes against 

<margin>How many crimes witches commit at once.</margin>
religion. Those who are captured by such love are in part atheists, in part despisers of God, and deserters of the faith they once professed. You should also learn that they sin extremely against the state. They steal the property of others through sorceries...

### BOOK TWO

[...with their evil] deeds they contaminate everything: they plunge themselves deeply into adulteries and debaucheries; they kill children and suck out their blood; they stir up the fiercest storms and devastate the fields with hail with such destruction that those who charmed away crops in ancient times <note>original: "fruges excantarunt"; a reference to the magical theft of crops by incantation</note> seem to have acted more mildly:

<margin>Law of the Twelve Tables</margin>
since a law was passed against them, which was later included in the Twelve Tables <note>The earliest code of Roman law (c. 450 BCE), which famously prescribed the death penalty for those who "charmed away" a neighbor's crops</note>.

**AP.** So, do they plot destruction not only against external goods produced by the earth, but even from the sky and the air surrounding us?

**DICASTES.** Ask her yourself. **AP.** Have you stirred up thunders? **WITCH.** Often. **AP.** Have you attacked the crops with hail? **WITCH.** More than once. **AP.** By what ritual? **WITCH.** I would trace a circle, and immediately Ludovicus <note>A specific name for a personal demon or familiar</note> would present himself there—not in human form, but in the form of fire. Then there would be lightning, thunder, and hail especially over those fields I wished to strike with disaster. **AP.** For what reason did you plot such destruction? **WITCH.** Out of hatred, not for favor.

**PH.** I have read of demons somehow resounding within the very incantations themselves:

<margin>From Ovid</margin>
when that ingenious poet <note>Ovid, in his Metamorphoses</note> summoned those sorceries under the name of the gods or through their personas: "By whose power, when I have willed it, rivers have returned to their sources while the banks marveled; I shake what is still, and I still what is shaken; by song I drive away the clouds from the sea and I bring clouds on; I drive away the winds and I call them forth," <note>original: "Quorum ope cum volui ripis mirantibus amnes. In fontes rediere suos..." quoting Medea's speech from Ovid’s Metamorphoses, Book VII</note> and so on. But our own witch here is more powerful than Medea; she has even summoned hail and brought it upon the crops. They also lure the minds of men with enticements, soothing their senses to entice them into crime. For as it is said, "the old men burned with illicit flames"; and not only do harmful potions work, nor only when they steal the *hippomanes* <note>original: "pignora foetae"; a reference to a growth on a foal's forehead used in love magic</note> from the brow of a mother animal. The mind, though polluted by no draft of poison, perishes by being "enchanted away," <note>original: "excantata"</note> which we have seen renewed, in a way, in

<margin>From Lucan</margin>
this very town. That priest was seventy-two years old when we drove out his passions with flames rising from a heap of brushwood <note>This likely refers to the priest's execution by burning at the stake</note>—passions by which he was being carried toward a <term>succubus|A demon in female form believed to have sexual intercourse with men</term>. Another man had passed his seventy-fifth year; another, who had seen eighty solstices, frequented the same "game" <note>likely referring to the witches' sabbath</note> eight times a month. This has been established by the testimony of many: for we found not just one witch, or two or three, but many; and not just three or four who use <term>succubus|A demon in female form</term> demons, but they have been caught in much greater numbers, and they themselves report...

# ON WITCHES

...that up to two thousand people frequent the gathering <note>original: "ludum"; literally "the game," a common term for the witches' Sabbath</note>. **AP.** Antiquity handed down to posterity the names of three or four famous female sorcerers. Therefore, in our time, there are many Medeas, many Canidias, and more than one Erichtho <note>Medea, Canidia, and Erichtho were famous witches from classical Greek and Roman literature, known for poison, necromancy, and cruelty</note>. **PH.** Do you wonder that six hundred Medeas are kept, when you know without surprise that twelve thousand Circes <note>Circe was the mythical sorceress who turned Odysseus's men into swine</note> (for they were considered sisters) are found in one city? **AP.** I understand the point; there is no need to seek the meaning of the riddle through hidden paths or twists. **PH.** Therefore, in this time, when all things seem to be rushing toward the worse, I believe it has happened by a wonderful providence: that God himself wished the faith to be strengthened in the minds of faithful people in many ways, so that religion might be propagated far and wide. **AP.** In what ways? **PH.** In three ways especially:

<margin>Faith is strengthened in three primary ways, both in itself and accidentally.</margin>By the fulfillment of predicted events; by miracles divinely performed; and by uncovering the crime of such a wicked ritual. For we have found that wars, famine, and pestilence have occurred exactly as they were divinely foretold through the passage of so many years. To those without faith, these might be seen as overwhelming calamities, easily suspected as the result of chance or fate, unless faith were vigorous—recently stirred up in this town by so many miracles proceeding from the Virgin Mother of God <term>Deipara; literally "the God-bearer"</term>. While these confirm the Christian faith in themselves, so too does the confession of the witches <term>strigum; from "strix," a mythical screech-owl or blood-sucking witch</term> corroborate it incidentally. Through a great multitude of witnesses of every sex, we learn that there are malignant demons opposed to Christian truth. The more they strive with every effort to destroy and obscure it, the more it happens that the truth is lifted up and shines far and wide. **AP.** You have deduced this correctly. But you, "good" witch, have you also killed children? **WITCH.** Very many. **AP.** With a sword or a club? **WITCH.** With a needle and lips. **AP.** In what way? **WITCH.** We used to enter the houses of enemies and even friends at night; the doors would sometimes open for us. While the parents slept, we would seize the infants. After placing them by the fire, we would pierce them with a needle under their little nails and apply our lips, drawing out as much blood as would fill the mouth. Part of the swallowed blood was gulped down; part was kept in a small jar <term>Pixide; a container often used for medicines or ointments</term> to make the ointment with which they wash their buttocks before we are carried off to the gathering. **DICASTES.** And lest you believe they have either lied or were dwelling in the houses of parents through imagination and dreams: nursing infants have been found with pierced fingers...

<meta>catchword: and covered</meta>

<summary>Phronimus argues that the rise of witchcraft and divine miracles both serve to strengthen the Christian faith during troubled times. The witch then provides a gruesome confession of infanticide and blood-drinking used to create flying ointments.</summary>

<keywords>infanticide, blood-drinking, Sabbath, Medea, Circe, miracles, providence, witches' ointment, Strix</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous discussion where the witch (Striga) was describing how she harms children. Here, she explains how she heals some for profit, leading to a debate between Apistius the skeptic, Phronimus the scholar, and Diodorus on the nature of demonic knowledge and the reality of supernatural phenomena.</meta>

# BOOK TWO

...and with a scar drawn over the fingernails. **AP.** I wonder that the boys did not cry out when they were pricked. **STR.** They are put to sleep <note>The witch describes a magical or drug-induced slumber used to silence victims</note> in such a way that they do not feel it. But when awakened, they let out strange wails; they fall sick, and sometimes they die. **AP.** Why do they not all perish? **STR.** We care for them, having been taught remedies from which profit then comes to us. **AP.** By whom were you taught? **STR.** By demons. **AP.** 

<margin>On the knowledge of demons and their manifold deceit</margin>
That does not seem probable to me. **PH.** The Demon is not ignorant of the powers of herbs, which humans also know. Nor should you be ignorant that many rules of healing were once inscribed in the temple of Aesculapius <note>The Greek god of medicine; his temples were centers of healing where patients practiced "incubation" or temple-sleep</note>; it is handed down to memory that Hippocrates <note>The famous Greek physician of the 5th century BCE</note> took his knowledge from there and collected it into his books. Many remedies for wounds and poisons have also been committed to history as discovered through sleep. Indeed, in the temples of Pasiphae <note>A goddess associated with oracles and prophetic dreams</note> and others—who were thought to be Gods, as was previously revealed—we read that those who sought help for diseases used to sleep so that a cure might be revealed to them through rest. **AP.** What do your lovers <note>original: "amatores"; referring to the incubi or demons with whom the witches were said to have relationships</note> promise you? What do they bid you hope for? **STR.** Life, riches, and continuous allurements which we enjoy. **AP.** Has he ever bestowed coins upon you? **STR.** He gave some once, but they vanished; I have reserved a few farthings <note>original: "quadrantes"; the smallest Roman coin, used here to show the "devil's gold" is worthless</note> for myself. **AP.** 

<margin>On the greatness of the wealth which Alexander carried with him</margin>
O, what vast riches! What if the Treasury of Croesus <note>A king of Lydia legendary for his immense wealth</note> were at stake in these wagers? And much more the wealth of Alexander, which was carried by forty thousand mules if we believe Curtius <note>Quintus Curtius Rufus, a Roman historian of Alexander the Great</note>, or if we believe Plutarch, by "ten thousand pairs of mules and five thousand camels" <note>original: "Μυρίοις ὀρικοῖσ ζεύγεσιν καὶ πεντάκισ χιλίαις καμήλοις"</note>. **PH.** It seems enough has been done for this man <note>referring to the demon</note> if he provides as many pleasures as neither Sardanapalus, nor Smyndirides, nor Straton <note>Historical figures famous in antiquity for their extreme luxury and decadence</note> possessed in abundance. **AP.** Those pleasures were human, though obscene; these are ridiculous and vain. **PH.** You should by no means call them "vain" if by that word you meant they were fictitious or imaginary. **DI.** I indeed consider them partly true—that is, consistent in actual fact—and partly empty and founded on no basis of reality, especially those things handed down by some concerning transformation, and concerning oxen already eaten and recalled to life by casting their bones back into the reserved and stretched hide <note>A common folklore motif where a demon or god resurrects an animal from its skin and bones</note>. But that they are carried through the air, and sometimes—even frequently—they eat, drink, and lie with one another <note>original: "incumbant, succumbant"; technical terms for the sexual acts of incubi and succubi</note>, that is by no means to be cast aside as false or shrinking from the truth. I could tell you many things asserted by the most famous witnesses, if I did not fear that you would complain of being cheated of the time reserved for listening to the witch...

<summary>The dialogue explores the source of a witch's healing power, attributing it to demonic knowledge of nature, while debating whether demonic gifts and physical experiences are real or mere illusions.</summary>
<keywords>demonic medicine, Aesculapius, Hippocrates, Alexander the Great, incubation, temple sleep, devil's gold, incubus, succubus, illusion vs reality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the trial and interrogation of a witch (Strix). In the previous page, Phronimus discussed the historical precedents of demonic healing and the deceitful nature of the devil. Here, the conversation shifts to the specific pacts and rituals used to summon these entities, specifically focusing on the significance of numbers.</meta>

# ON WITCHES

...I might have snatched away your opportunity. **AP.** I pray you, postpone it until tomorrow. **DI.** He has already been assigned to other questions. But if you have a mind to dine with me, though it be in the country, there will nonetheless be enough provisions at least to keep hunger at bay. **PH.** A friend's banquet is not to be refused; it is all the more worthy of the name when it sets out less food and more conversation. **AP.** Both please me: the body is fed by one, the mind by the other. **DI.** Ask the witch whatever you prefer. I will leave this deputy <note>Likely a guard or a legal official</note> here for a while until I return; for in the meantime, I will instruct the steward what needs to be done. **AP.** Did you have some signal by which he <note>The demon or "lover"</note> would present himself when called into the circle? **STR.** If I had withdrawn and called twice. **AP.** For what reason not three or four times? **STR.** I do not know, for that is how he instructed me; and he earnestly warned me not to call three times. **AP.** What do you think about this

<margin>On the pacts of demons and duality.</margin>

matter, Phronimus? **PH.** These pacts of demons depend on the demon himself; they are not only open pacts, such as these are considered, but also hidden ones, about which our Augustine and others have spoken. However, I would by no means believe that there is a natural cause in this "binary" <note>The number two</note>, nor do I think he wished to indicate the "service of the Dyad" <note>The Pythagorean concept of the number two, representing division or the material world</note> passed down from the Chaldean Magus through Pythagoras to the Platonists—

<margin>Zaretas the Chaldean.</margin>

whether he should be called Zaretas <note>original: "Zaretas"</note> according to the custom of Origen in the book *Philosophumena* <note>original: "φιλοσοφουμένων"; a 3rd-century treatise "Refutation of All Heresies"</note>, or whether it should be written Zaratas, which word Plutarch of Chaeronea uses in designating the teacher of Pythagoras while interpreting a small part of the *Timaeus* Dialogue; or whether he should rather be called Zaradas, since the "laws of Zaradas" <note>original: "ζαράδου νόμοι"</note> are cited by the theologian Theodoret in his book *On Laws*. For why would a Demon philosophize on such a matter with this Thestylis <note>A traditional name for a simple country woman or witch in classical poetry</note>? Instead, I think some trick of the most deceitful enemy lies hidden here: either so that he may not agree even in voice with the Most Holy Trinity <note>The number three</note>, which is God; or so that he may turn her further away from the customs of our religion; or by some other fallacy unknown to us, handed down to ancient peoples under an even number, which they wanted dedicated to the gods of the underworld, just as the odd number was for the gods above. **AP.** That is very pleasing, but was it clear to you, Witch, that you were being deluded by your lover? **STR.** Never. **AP.** But when you looked at the coins that vanished? **STR.** In whatever way—but I did not pay attention. For he returned again and again, and bound me with many enticements. **AP.** What did he seek from you when he promised you such great things, when he had business with you with such ardor, and pretended to be seized by a wondrous love for you? **STR.** Nothing else than that I should reject the faith of Christ and place no hope in Him, but rather love him...

<meta>The text cuts off at "amato[rem]" (lover), continuing the witch's confession regarding the demon's ultimate goal: the renunciation of the Christian faith.</meta>

<summary>Diodorus invites the group to dinner while Apistius questions the witch about summoning rituals. Phronimus provides a scholarly explanation for why demons prefer the number two over the number three, linking it to ancient philosophy and a rejection of the Holy Trinity.</summary>
<keywords>summoning rituals, numerology, Pythagoras, Holy Trinity, Zaradas, Origen, demonic pacts, duality, Thestylis</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Phronimus (the philosopher), Apotropaeus (the defender), and Dicastes (the judge), who are interrogating a woman accused of being a "Strix" or witch. The previous page discussed the devil's use of numbers and signals in pacts.</meta>

->## BOOK TWO<-
->F ii<-

...that I might worship him in His place, bow my knee to him, and regard him as my God. **PH.** Oh, truly a most wicked spirit! He is called "Satan" <note>original: "Sathana"</note> among the Hebrews, meaning an adversary; among the Greeks, the "Devil" <note>original: "diabolum"</note>; and among the Latins, the "Slanderer" <note>original: "calumniatorem"</note>. 
<margin>Various names of the Demon explained.</margin>
No greater slander against God could be devised than for him to snatch away divinity for himself by using God’s own words, and most arrogantly and falsely attribute it to himself. Perhaps for this reason he grew to love the name of "Demon," whether because it indicated knowledge or fear; but his primary specialty is to construct slanders. In this way, he first deceived man under the name of the gods. Justin, the philosopher and martyr, asserted that the very name of "slanderer" arose from this. **AP.** But by what means were you distinguished from others who devote themselves to the Christian religion? **STR.** There was no distinction. I would gather at the sacred temple. During the season of Lent, I would confess to the priest; yet, with this one thing <note>the pact with the demon</note> removed, I confessed all my other sins. I would approach the Altar; when the sacred host was administered, there was no difference between me and the other women. Nor had my lover <note>the demon</note> forbidden this to me. He only advised me to say certain things silently and to do some things in secret; once these were performed, he required nothing more. **AP.** Tell us everything. **STR.** On feast days when I was in the church (as is common), and the priest read in a loud voice, he commanded that I say in a low voice: "It is not true, you are lying." Truly, when the priest raised the host above his head, he ordered me to turn my eyes away, and to fold my fingers behind my back beneath my clothing (just as you see me doing now). Furthermore, he strictly commanded that I report nothing at all to the priest about our love and dealings, nor anything concerning the "game" <note>original: "ludum"; a common term for the witches' Sabbat or gathering</note>. Whether I spoke of other things or not, he thought it mattered little. Presently, when I was brought to the altar and the consecrated body of Christ was placed in my mouth, he wanted me to slowly draw it out, feigning a wiping of my cheek, and to wrap it in a linen handkerchief to be carried back to the "game," so that he might mock it in those ways you have heard. I also wore two hosts sewn into my clothing. He told me that by these, I would be prevented from being able to confess what I was asked by the judge. When the judge pressed me and threatened tortures, that wicked one ordered me to throw them into the basin <note>original: "trullam"; likely a waste vessel or chamber pot in the cell</note> which the jailer had prepared for me. **AP.** Did you obey? **STR.** I obeyed, poor wretch that I am. And a thing most horrific: when I had broken them with a stick, I noticed that blood flowed out. **PH.** My ears shall never drink in such things again. **AP.** Nor mine. **PH.** Let us say that as he returns to us, if it please you, let us meet him. **AP.** It pleases me very much. **DI.** Does anything else remain? **PH.** We are so provoked to anger...

<summary>The accused witch describes her behavior in church, including her mockery of the Mass and her desecration of the consecrated Host at the demon's command. Phronimus and Apotropaeus express their horror at her confession of these sacrilegious acts.</summary>
<keywords>Satan, names of the devil, Justin Martyr, confession, Lent, Eucharist, Host desecration, Sabbat, witchcraft trials, sacrilege</keywords>
