<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Dicastes, Phronimus, and Apotropaeus. Previously, a woman accused of witchcraft (the Strix) confessed to making a pact with a demon and leading a double life within the church. Now, the men debate whether the "witches' sabbath" is a physical reality or a mental delusion.</meta>

-># ON WITCHES<-
<meta>catchword: men</meta>

...[let us conclude], so that we do not miss lunch. DICASTES: Let us withdraw into the garden for a little while: and perhaps while walking you will sharpen your appetite; let that woman be led back to the prison. APOTROPAEUS: I never could have imagined in my mind that so many crimes could be devised. Indeed, I would have easily forgiven this type of person before, when I thought that levity <note>frivolity or lack of judgment</note> was the cause: I believed they were led into this error mostly and deluded by the illusions of the <term>strix: a legendary bird-like witch believed to fly at night and harm children</term>. Nor would I swear that they themselves are not deluded. But just as I have never failed to assent to the truth of the Christian religion, so I could not agree that such impious violators of it should be spared. DICASTES: If I bring it about that it is clear this pertains to the Christian religion, and if I bring witnesses such that you almost cannot help but believe—that many things in that very game <note>referring to the 'ludus' or witches' sabbath</note> are performed truly and, as we are accustomed to say (forgive me this word, if you please), *really* <note>original: "realiter", a technical term in philosophy meaning physical reality as opposed to an illusion or dream</note>—I think you will not resist so stubbornly. APOTROPAEUS: My mind does not yet lean toward either side. DICASTES: Tell me, I pray: have you ever seen any dead person raised? APOTROPAEUS: I have not seen it. DICASTES: Do you believe the dead can be raised?

<margin>Plato, Book 10 of the Republic.</margin>
PHRONIMUS: He will not deny it, since it is celebrated among the poets, written also among the philosophers, and especially in Plato, that the dead have risen from the underworld. APOTROPAEUS: In a matter so serious and of such consequence, I do not rest upon poets or philosophers, but upon the Gospel.

<margin>On the Spanish navigation.</margin>
DICASTES: I will put forward examples of another matter which is not contained in Holy Scripture: Do you believe that ships set out from Cádiz <note>original: "Gades"</note> and from the port of Lisbon <note>original: "Vlyssiponis"</note> in Portugal, and that, having now turned toward the West Wind <note>original: "Zephyrum"</note>, after traveling twenty myriads of stades <note>a "myriad of stades" is about 1,200 miles; a poetic way to describe vast distances</note> more or less, they are carried to such a vast land whose circumference is still unknown, and that now, with the West Wind driving them through the Atlantic Sea, they arrive at the Indian Gulf? APOTROPAEUS: Why should I not believe it? DICASTES: Whom do you believe? APOTROPAEUS: So many merchants who say they have completed such a journey "upon the broad back of the sea" <note>original Greek: "ἐπ’ εὐρεῖα νῶτα θαλάσσης", a phrase from Homer’s Iliad</note>. DICASTES: Have you ever spoken to them? APOTROPAEUS: I myself never have; but I have spoken with someone who affirmed he heard these things with his own ears from those who sailed. DICASTES: Could they not delude you? APOTROPAEUS: But who would doubt that men of weight <note>serious or reliable men</note> take delight in lies? DICASTES: If I produce for you no fewer in number, and witnesses of no less weight, who have confirmed even by oath that witches are carried to the game, and that demons in female form lie down...

<summary>Dicastes argues that the witches' sabbath is a physical reality, using the contemporary voyages to the New World and the Indies as a rhetorical comparison for believing in things one has not personally seen.</summary>
<keywords>witchcraft, Strix, reality vs illusion, Plato, Age of Discovery, Lisbon, Cádiz, Atlantic navigation, demons</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Phronimus and Apotropaeus, following Dicastes' question about the resurrection of the dead. They are debating whether the confessions of witches regarding supernatural acts should be believed based on common consensus, comparing the "discovery" of witchcraft to the recent discovery of the New World.</meta>

### BOOK TWO

...to men, who have confessed under oath that demons lie upon them just as men lie upon women <note>This refers to the belief in incubi and succubi, demons who were thought to have sexual relations with humans</note>. Will you not agree then?

PHRONIMUS: One would be judged as either an unwise or a stubborn man if you did not do so.

APOTROPAEUS: Why is that?

PHRONIMUS: Because when many people agree in their minds on a single matter, and consent with one voice to confirm it, it should not seem likely that anyone could rightfully object—unless perhaps he were led by some reasoning of such great power that it could demolish an opinion strengthened by that common consensus. I hardly believe such a reason will be available to you.

APOTROPAEUS: This <term>epichereme <note>original: "Epicherema"; a complex rhetorical argument or syllogism where the premises are supported by evidence as they are presented</note></term> of yours would have some strength if it were not applied to things that seem beyond nature, but rather to things that are common in human experience. For this reason, I did not dispute the navigation of the Spanish fleet, yet I do dispute this Game of Diana <note>original: "Dianæ ludo"; a folkloric belief in a nocturnal procession led by the goddess Diana, which authorities increasingly identified with the witches' sabbath</note>.

PHRONIMUS: One could oppose those describing the route to India far more than those describing the night-game of Hecate <note>original: "Hecates"; Hecate was the Greek goddess of magic and the crossroads, often synonymous with Diana in this context</note>. For that route was in no way known to antiquity; only certain signs were found by which they recall that some ship or other once drifted from India to the Spanish coast. Now, one sails from Europe to India through the Ethiopian Sea <note>original: "pelagus Aethyopicũ"; a historical term for the South Atlantic and Indian Oceans</note>; and the ports and shores are already marked on painted maps. Furthermore, islands of wondrous size have been found that were not known to the ancients. Indeed, no voice ever spoke of that land of vast extent discovered in our own age <note>A reference to the Americas, used here to argue that just because the ancients didn't know something, it doesn't mean it isn't real</note>. If the philosophers who imagined many worlds existing in the nature of things had known of it, they might perhaps have seemed to be "insane" with greater reason, led by that argument. Certainly neither Strabo nor Ptolemy <note>The most authoritative geographers of the ancient world</note> said a word about it, nor did those who are considered more prone to fables mention it. But regarding witches <note>original: "strigibus"; refers to the *strix*, a legendary bird-like witch</note>, there is open mention in both ancient and modern books.

APOTROPAEUS: I feel my mind beginning to lean, I know not how, toward your opinion. But I would gladly hear the witnesses whom Dicastes promises to bring forward, and whatever reasons he might have besides those he has already used.

PHRONIMUS: It is a sign of an inconstant mind to waver, leaning now this way and now that. For previously, what we were saying seemed true enough to you—or at least likely. Then you resisted, contending that you had a right to resist. Now you confess that you are being carried toward our opinion by a certain inclination of the mind, by which...

<summary>The speakers debate whether the sheer number of confessions about demonic activity constitutes proof, comparing the "discovery" of witchcraft's reality to the recent geographical discovery of the Americas and the sea route to India.</summary>
<keywords>consensus, demons, Game of Diana, Hecate, New World, navigation, Strabo, Ptolemy, witches, evidence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philomathes and Apotropaeus. On the previous page, they were debating the reality of the "Night-Game of Diana" (the witches' sabbath) compared to tangible discoveries like the route to India. Apotropaeus remains skeptical of the supernatural claims despite common consensus.</meta>

# ON WITCHES

...regarding these matters, I gather an unstable opinion. Unless perhaps I should think you have used irony, being accustomed as you are to the playful trifles of the poets, or having spent time in Socratic dialogues <note>The "Socratic dialogues" refers to the method of inquiry used by Socrates, often characterized by "Socratic irony," where the speaker pretends ignorance to lead others to discover the truth</note>. From such things, a certain and fixed truth can either never be gathered, or only with great difficulty.

**AP.** I am concealing nothing: nor do I judge that there is any need for irony with you. But in a matter of such great prejudice, I would not wish to give assent rashly. It seems more fitting to me—provided it is done with modesty—to doubt slightly. And thus from time to time, now to you, now to Dicastes <note>Dicastes is the third member of the dialogue, whose name implies a judge or arbiter</note>, I shall reveal the weakness of my mind, as if showing an abscess to a surgeon.

¶ For indeed, if I remember correctly, it was stated by a man of great name that one must walk step by step in matters of this kind, which seem easily to surpass our understanding. For if they are despised, an impious deception might immediately creep upon us; yet if they are accepted too hastily, an old wives' superstition <note>original: "superstitio anilis"; a common Renaissance term for folkloric beliefs dismissed by the learned as irrational or feminine gossip</note> might ensnare us. For my part, even if I were doubtful in my mind and it seemed I should hesitate, I have never resisted with a stubborn spirit.

**PH.** If you are so disposed that you wish to use your intellect rather than your will in this matter, then one should certainly hope well for you. But always observe this—not only in every matter that carries a risk, but especially in this one of which we are debating—that emotion should never precede the intellect. For there are those who walk in a backwards order in the pursuit of learning, deciding by their own will what is true before they have considered the truth itself with their mind.

**AP.** I am possessed by a great thirst to know what Dicastes will contribute to this matter, for I see him returning to us. Indeed, his words cannot be anything but excellent, if he is willing to stand by his promises.

**PH.** But first it is necessary to satisfy our hunger, and then soon your thirst <note>The speakers use a metaphor here: "hunger" refers to the actual meal (lunch) they are about to eat, while "thirst" refers to Apotropaeus's desire for knowledge and Dicastes's legal/philosophical judgment</note>.

**DICA.** Lunch is ready now, and we have dragged out the dispute deep into the day. When we have given to the body what is sufficient to restore it from its daily decay,

->whatever remains of<-
->the dispute, we<-
->will approach.<-

<summary>Apotropaeus expresses his cautious skepticism regarding the supernatural, advocating for a middle path between total disbelief and blind superstition. The dialogue pauses as Dicastes returns to call the group to lunch, promising to conclude the debate afterward.</summary>
<keywords>skepticism, Socratic irony, superstition, intellect vs. will, Dicastes, logic, Renaissance dialogue</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book Three of Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola’s dialogue. While the previous page featured a conversation between Philomathes and Apotropaeus, we now transition to a new set of interlocutors: Apistius (the skeptic), Strix (the witch), Dicastes (the judge), and Phronimus (the wise man). The setting has moved from a general discussion to a specific inquiry following a meal.</meta>

# THE THIRD BOOK OF GIANFRANCESCO PICO DELLA MIRANDOLA,
# LORD AND COUNT OF CONCORDIA,
# ETC., ON THE DECEPTION OF DEMONS.
# INTERLOCUTORS
### Apistius : Strix : Dicastes : Phronimus.

->APISTIUS.<-

<image-desc>A large decorative drop-cap "P" inside a square frame. The letter "P" is intricately entwined with foliate vine patterns and floral motifs, set against a background featuring light architectural detailing.</image-desc>

**P**ost-hunger has been removed by the feast <note>original: "Postquam exempta fames epulis"; a classical allusion to Virgil’s Aeneid, signaling the end of a meal and the start of serious conversation</note>, Dicastes the Inquisitor, let me be permitted to ask you this before all else: something that has brought not just a doubt <note>original: "scrupulum," literally a small sharp stone in a shoe causing unease</note> but a spear into my soul, if it were granted to be true; namely, that which was thrust into our ears by that Witch <note>original: "Strige," referring to the character Strix</note>. **DICASTES.** Let it be permitted, as you please. **APISTIUS.** It does not satisfy me, what is said by some, while these monsters and vices are tolerated: that a judgment is sometimes carried out in subterranean places, where those who have immersed themselves in crimes pay the due punishments <note>Apistius is questioning the justice of allowing crimes to happen if the only resolution is punishment in the afterlife or secret courts</note>. Would it not have been better if they were prohibited from committing them, rather than being punished once they were admitted? **DICASTES.** Better indeed, if you refer to the one who committed the crime: for if he had abstained from it, he would certainly have looked out for himself excellently. **APISTIUS.** Why was it permitted to be committed? Do we not think it would have been more divine if it were divinely forbidden? **DICASTES.** It was forbidden indeed by law, but not by the action itself. **APISTIUS.** Why was that action permitted? **DICASTES.** Because it is free and placed in the human will <term>arbitrio: the capacity for free choice or judgment</term>. **APISTIUS.** Would it not have been better for him whom God knew would fall into these depths of impiety never to have been born? **DICASTES.** Better altogether, as far as it concerns the one who will persevere in crime until the end of life, if he had been extinguished in his mother’s womb. **APISTIUS.** But if he had never existed, do you think it would be better for him? **DICASTES.** For whom? **APISTIUS.** For himself. **DICASTES.** An empty question: since indeed both "he" and "nothing" are so opposed to each other that one is destroyed by the other, and to that "nothing" which we conceive...

<summary>This page opens Book Three of Pico's dialogue, where the skeptic Apistius questions Dicastes the Judge about the problem of evil and divine permission, specifically regarding the crimes of witches.</summary>
<keywords>Apistius, Dicastes, Strix, free will, divine law, punishment, Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola, theodicy</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from Book Three. Having just finished their meal, Apistius (the skeptic) has begun questioning Dicastes (the inquisitor) about the problem of evil—specifically, why a good God would allow such wickedness as witchcraft to exist, or create souls He knows will be damned.</meta>

# DE STRIGIBVS
### (ON WITCHES)

...whatever happens, whether prosperous or adverse. **APISTIUS.** Why then did God, in His supreme goodness, create a man whom He knew was destined for eternal torments? **DICASTES.** On account of His own supreme goodness. **APISTIUS.** How can this be? **DICASTES.** It is possible for this reason: so that God's infinite goodness might not be conquered by human malice. Indeed, the Apostle Peter is said to have replied in this very vein to Simon Magus <note>Simon Magus was a sorcerer mentioned in the Acts of the Apostles, often considered the first heretic</note> when he asked almost the same question—if Clement has correctly recorded the dispute held between them in his literary monuments.
<margin>From Clement.</margin>
It would surely seem that such a sublime benefit as the creation of a soul—certainly a work of infinite power—should not cease simply because a man would abuse that benefit. Add to this that if you turn your mind to all the virtues God has shown the world, Justice exerts itself by embracing those who chose to flee the duties of goodness and mercy rather than follow them; nor is mercy extinguished or diminished because of this, for it strikes less harshly than the rigor of justice might demand. And so, whatever often arises from the crimes of the wicked is drawn out by God Himself, whom
<margin>Augustine.</margin>
Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo, a foundational Church Father</note> proclaims to be so good that He would not permit evil to happen unless He willed to elicit some greater good from it. This result is frequently, if not always, seen by learned men, though not by the common people. Let these few points serve as examples for you among many. The righteous Joseph was sold by his brothers—an extraordinary crime on the part of the brothers. The unskilled multitude requires nothing further <note>they see only the crime</note>, but men who are both learned and imbued with piety recognize that from such an impious trade,
<margin>On Joseph.</margin>
Joseph was appointed almost as a king of Egypt, and delivered his father, brothers, and his whole family from destruction; and from that, many great mysteries followed which we celebrate. From the
<margin>On Tyrants.</margin>
tortures and murders committed by tyrants, the virtue and glory of the Martyrs shone forth. Finally—why say more?—from the death of Christ, the exceptional goodness of God was made clear: the redemption of the entire human race from eternal death, and the opening of the gate to piety and justice. **APISTIUS.** You have removed the doubt <note>original: "scrupulum," literally a small sharp stone in a shoe causing unease</note> that plagued me, if indeed what we heard was to be placed among the truths. Now, as you promised to do, discuss that which followed: how this account <note>referring to the "play" or story of the witch</note> is a factual history and not a fictional invention.
<margin>On the condition of history.</margin>
**PHRONIMUS.** Are you going to accept every history? **APISTIUS.** Not at all. For that Samosatenian tale <note>A reference to Lucian of Samosata’s *True History*, a famous ancient satire that mocks tall tales by claiming to be true</note>, though it is a most unadulterated fable, is nevertheless circulated under the title of a "True Narrative." But many histories are so uncertain, so of double—and often multiple—repute, that they differ little [from fiction].

<summary>Dicastes defends theodicy, arguing that God permits evil (like the crimes of witches or Joseph's brothers) only to bring about a greater good. Apistius accepts this theological point but demands proof that the stories of witchcraft they are discussing are historical facts rather than satirical fictions like those of Lucian.</summary>
<keywords>theodicy, problem of evil, St. Augustine, Joseph (biblical), Simon Magus, Clement of Rome, Lucian of Samosata, historiography, martyrdom</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue in Book Three. Previously, Dicastes justified God's permission of evil. Now, the conversation shifts from theological justifications to the historical evidence for witchcraft and the importance of writing style and authority in conveying truth.</meta>

# BOOK THREE
### G

...to be seen as fables. **PHRONIMUS.** You judge correctly. For just as some light of truth sometimes shines through the shadows of fables, so among historical narratives that conflict with one another, you will perhaps find one that is true. The others, inevitably slipping into falsehood, must be counted as fables. For truth cannot quarrel with truth. Thus, I think I understand, Dicastes, what Apistius means. **DICASTES.** What is that? **PHRONIMUS.** A history proven by many witnesses, against which no other history of equal or greater authority can be placed in opposition. **APISTIUS.** You have expressed my meaning exactly. **DICASTES.** I promise to show that this pertains to the Christian religion—so that we may consider this matter seriously rather than as a game, and take care to uproot [evil]. I will bring forward several historical accounts that do not conflict but are in great harmony, and I will bring the witch <term>strigem<gloss>A 'strix' or 'striga' was a mythological screech-owl or witch said to prey on children; here used for a practitioner of harmful magic</gloss></term> back here to you. Guard of the prison, bring her forth as quickly as possible! She must be bound by oath to tell the truth. Many of the testimonies I will present to you were taken from men bound by sacred oaths and committed to the memory of posterity for the sake of proving the truth. **APISTIUS.** Explain further. **DICASTES.** I could refer you to books composed with great care on this matter, and although that would not be unwelcome to Phronimus—who, as his discourse showed, is well-versed in every kind of writing—it might not satisfy Apistius. He seems to have drunk deeply of more "polished" literature <note>This refers to the 'studia humanitatis' or Renaissance humanism, which favored classical style over medieval scholasticism</note> and might recoil from any books that lack luster and refinement. **APISTIUS.** Are you perhaps condemning, Dicastes, by that rhetorical figure of yours, speeches elegantly composed in either verse or prose? **DICASTES.** Not at all.

<margin>On the elegance of writing.</margin>
**APISTIUS.** Yet some people seem to be like that—those who only know "Parisian letters," <note>original: "Parisienses literas." This refers to the Scholastic method of education common at the University of Paris, often criticized by humanists for being overly technical and dry</note> that is, little questions scratched out on paper (for I have seen volumes written in Paris by the natives themselves in an elegant and precise style); while they are experts in those, they loathe a continuous speech that is aptly, distinctly, and ornately composed. **DICASTES.** I should not be placed among those, for it is clearly evident to me that John Chrysostom, Basil, the three Gregorys <note>Likely referring to Gregory of Nazianzus, Gregory of Nyssa, and Gregory the Great</note> in Greek, and Jerome, Augustine, Cyprian, Ambrose, and others in Latin did exactly that. **APISTIUS.** Did they also write poems? **DICASTES.** Most of them certainly wrote poems, so that people would stop saying that such a style of writing was unsuitable for those times. For they resisted the enemies of the faith even with poems. Nor are there lacking in our own time those who are more easily drawn to our sacred rites by eloquence.

<summary>Dicastes and his companions discuss how historical truth is distinguished from fables through consistent testimony and authority. They also debate the merits of elegant writing versus scholastic "Parisian" style, noting that the great Church Fathers used both refined prose and poetry to defend the faith.</summary>
<keywords>witchcraft, historical evidence, fables, rhetoric, humanism vs scholasticism, Church Fathers, John Chrysostom, Augustine, poetry in theology</keywords>

<header># ON WITCHES</header>

<margin>Concerning the *Hammer of Witches* <note>original: *De malleo maleficarum*. This refers to the *Malleus Maleficarum*, first published in 1487, the most famous treatise on the prosecution of witches.</note></margin>
...who is now chaste: she certainly cannot be condemned, just as an excellent good among men cannot be condemned, for which both reason and the authority of our ancestors speak in favor. **APISTIUS.** What books are these? And at what time were they published? **DICASTES.** Many are held [to be authoritative]. But some were published sixty years ago; one, however, in our own time. **APISTIUS.** Who were the authors? **DICASTES.** I believe they were Belgians or Germans. As for the one I mentioned last, there are two authors, both German. They saw to it that witches were "pounded with a single hammer"—and more justly than Nicocreon the Cypriot pounded Anaxarchus of Abdera. <note>Anaxarchus was a philosopher reportedly pounded to death in a mortar with iron pestles by the tyrant Nicocreon; Dicastes uses this violent image to describe how the authors of the *Malleus* "crushed" the arguments and lives of witches.</note> **APISTIUS.** In what style were they written? **DICASTES.** In that which is commonly called the "Parisian" style <note>The Scholastic style of the University of Paris, characterized by rigorous, technical logic rather than elegant prose.</note>—using small questions, but with great subtlety, as much as the subject allows. And as it seems to me, it rests upon the testimony of the truth of holy men; nor does it seem so to me alone, but also to many theologians. The beginning of that last volume started with the Supreme Pontiff <note>Pope Innocent VIII, whose bull *Summis desiderantes affectibus* (1484) is often prefaced to the *Malleus*.</note>, and its conclusion was approved by the authority of the Caesar <note>The Holy Roman Emperor.</note>. We know the book was publicly praised by those who profess the sacred letters in Cologne. <note>original: *Agrypina Colonia*. Cologne was a major center for theological study and the Inquisition.</note> **APISTIUS.** I would prefer, Dicastes, for the things that pertain to our matter to be narrated by you—whether you wish to draw them from there or elsewhere—so that you may pour them back to me in a more familiar language. For since Phronimus here is present for the discussion, just as he has discussed many other things which I do not think are contained in those books, so perhaps he will not be burdened to add many more; and even the Witch, who is now hastening here, will perhaps add other things to what she has already reported that are not yet contained in any books. **DICASTES.** I will comply, though I use this preface as a plea for indulgence: if I happen to bring forth anything unusual to your ears, you must completely forgive me. For although I have loved Latin and Greek literature most dearly, I have nonetheless spent no less study among the theologians, who, having set aside the cultivation of words, are free to discern the reality of things. **PHRONIMUS.** The 
<margin>On the loss of things and words</margin>
loss of words is less than the loss of things. I have always thought that he who can provide both excels those who rely only on one. But if one had to be without the other, I would never think that the knowledge of things should be placed after vocabulary—although you, as I have gathered from your speech, could have 
<margin>How impious and how ignorant is he who would deny that demons exist.</margin>
abstained from this plea of guilt. **DICASTES.** I will speak as I can. First of all, it is sufficiently established in Latin that he who would deny that demons exist should be cast out from the threshold of the Catholic Church, as one who contradicts the sacred scriptures in many ways and especially the Gospel itself. **APISTIUS.** This I admit as most true, beyond all doubt. **PHRONIMUS.** That same person should also be cast out from the Academy and from the Lyceum <note>The "Academy" refers to Plato’s school and the "Lyceum" to Aristotle’s; Phronimus argues that even secular philosophy recognizes the existence of spiritual beings.</note>, since according to Plato...

<meta>This page continues the discussion in Book Three regarding the reality of witchcraft. ### BOOK THREE
<sig>G ii</sig>

...and among all the Platonists there is much mention of demons, which Aristotle did not shrink from, since indeed in his *Ethics*, *Politics*, and *Rhetoric* he is mindful of that name, to say nothing of other places. **DICASTES.** But they differ from our views in this: they believed in both good and evil demons. We assert that they are all malignant; though we mark them with the name of Satan and Devil, it is established that they are also designated by the nomenclature of "Demon." From this come those words of the Prophet: "All the gods of the gentiles are demons." <note>original: *omnes dii gentium daemonia*. A common medieval citation of Psalm 96:5, used to argue that pagan gods were actually fallen angels.</note> And the Apostle says, "I would not have you become associates of demons" <note>1 Corinthians 10:20</note>; and that which was also brought forth by the Apostle: "The demons believe and tremble." <note>James 2:19</note> Furthermore, when they perform witchcraft <term>maleficia; harmful magic or sorcery intended to cause physical or social damage</term> in enchanting crops, in dissolving or binding marital chambers <note>A reference to "ligature," a type of magic believed to cause impotence or infertility in newlyweds</note>, and in torments that occur against nature, no sane man has doubted that these are done by a pact with demons. And for this reason, many things are prescribed among theologians both ancient and recent, in the Holy Scriptures, the canons of the Roman Church, and the imperial laws.

<margin>By all laws, it is necessary that sorcerers pay the penalty.</margin>
In Deuteronomy, sorcerers and enchanters are ordered to be killed. In the book of Leviticus, the law provides that soothsayers and those possessed by a Pythian spirit <note>original: *Pythonico spū*. This refers to a "spirit of divination," named after the Pythia of Delphi.</note> must be overwhelmed with stones. In the 24th and 26th questions of the *Decretals* which Gratian collected, one may examine many such things. <note>The *Decretum Gratiani* was the foundational collection of Canon Law for the medieval Church.</note> **APISTIUS.** In Augustine's books *On the City of God* and *On Christian Doctrine*, so much can be read that little else is required, to say nothing of the younger theologians who have discussed witchcraft in many places. Moreover, in civil law within the Code of Justinian, many laws are set against sorcerers and astrologers <note>original: *mathematicos*. While we think of "mathematicians," in late Roman and medieval law this term specifically referred to those who cast horoscopes or practiced divination.</note>. **PHILOMATHES.** And among the younger philosophers, both Platonists and Peripatetics, many things are seen that may pertain to this—I mean Iamblichus, Proclus, and Porphyry. **APISTIUS.** Far be it from me to deny that demons exist and that many things can be perpetrated by their malice. Thus, I would wish for those things which properly pertain to this matter to be made clear to me. Do they go? Are they carried to the "game" <note>original: *ludum*. An early term used to describe the Witches' Sabbath or nocturnal gathering.</note> in body, or only in will and by an empty phantom?

<margin>Concerning the chapter *Episcopi* <note>The *Canon Episcopi*, a famous 10th-century legal text that initially suggested the "night-flight" of witches was a delusion.</note>, 26th question, 5th part: by what agreement they understand what is prohibited from being believed there.</margin>
**DICASTES.** This question has long tormented many, taking its occasion from the words of the Council which was mentioned: those words are read in the fifth question of the twenty-sixth cause, from which some have believed that little women and poor fellows <note>original: *mulierculas & homunciones*. Diminutives used here to suggest the vulnerability or low status of those accused.</note> are by no means present at such "games" in body, but only in mind. Wherefore, some have said that this "game" is a new species of heresy, different from that ancient superstition. Others say it is exactly the same, but that in this case, a complaint is merely made and a penalty decreed for those who...

It introduces the central debate regarding the 'Canon Episcopi' and whether the witches' Sabbath is a physical reality or a demonic delusion.</summary>

# ON WITCHES
<term>Striges; originally a classical term for "screech-owls" believed to suck the blood of infants, it was used by Renaissance writers to describe witches who allegedly flew at night and transformed into animals.</term>

<margin>
How the activity of witches is partly ancient and partly new.
</margin>
...they used to think that it was Diana the Goddess, or Herodias <note>original: *Herodiade*; a biblical figure often paired with Diana in medieval folklore (such as the "Canon Episcopi") as the leaders of a mythical night-procession of women</note>, and that the human body—just like the bodies of Demons—could be changed into the bodies of various animals. They asserted that these things were a rapture of the mind, and were to be discerned in such a way that it could be properly distinguished whether the body is actually carried to the place where the mind ascends. Since indeed Paul the Apostle said he did not know <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 12:2, where St. Paul describes a vision and says, "whether in the body or out of the body, I do not know; God knows."</note>. But when witches of this sort are carried, they are not snatched away in spirit; and if they were snatched away, the nature of that rapture would be different, having proceeded from a different principle. For they are carried, willing and awake, to dances, to banquets, and to nightly intercourses. Concerning this matter, Phronimus, I approved of your distinction in yesterday's dispute, by which you determined this sport of the witches <term>maleficarum; women who practice harmful magic</term> to be ancient in its essence, but new in its accidental details.

**PH.** If I have found in the ancient superstitions of the Demons: the ritual circle, ointments, incantations, the passage of human bodies through the spans of the air, prepared feasts, and sexual intercourse under the appearance of men and women—what remains? Should we not think this is an ancient commerce between wicked spirits and lost human beings? However, some things are found in this spectacle of Demons—which is presented to many now—that were not read of as being exhibited in antiquity. I have also revealed the causes, clearly attributed to the skin-changing <note>original: *versipellem*; literally "turn-skin," a term for a shapeshifter or a deceptive, crafty person</note> cunning of the malignant demon, the hater of mankind. He has adapted multiple arts of deception for different times and different people, so that he might entice men of various characters toward the precipice with various allurements.

**DI.** For that reason, what you have brought forward is pleasing.

**AP.** Do you think, then, that they are carried in the body?

**DI.** I think they are sometimes carried in the body; at other times, I would easily believe they can be so deluded that, with their imaginative power being diseased, they think they are carried in the body across Gargara, across the sounding Ascanius, and across the Caucasus echoed by Amazonian arms <note>A poetic reference to Virgil's *Georgics* and Statius's *Thebaid*, using these remote locations to represent the impossible distances witches claimed to travel</note>. Like what happens in dreams, they think they fly with Daedalean wings <note>original: *pennis daedaleis*; referring to the mythical waxen wings created by Daedalus</note>. But they are not subjected to questioning because of that—whether they walk about at home or outdoors in body or in mind—

<margin>
From Virgil.
From P. Statius.
</margin>

<margin>
Why the inquisitors hold examinations regarding witches.
</margin>
—but rather because they have denied the faith which they professed, mocked the sacraments, and, having despised Christ, have worshiped a Demon and perpetrated many acts of witchcraft <term>maleficia</term>. For this reason we inquire into them; and once convicted of the crime, we hand them over to be punished by kings, or petty rulers, or their governors. We rely not only on the ancient laws of the church, but also on the new ones repeatedly issued, such as those of Innocent VIII... <note>Pope Innocent VIII, whose 1484 bull *Summis desiderantes affectibus* provided the papal authority for the systematic persecution of witches.</note>

<summary>This page explores the debate over whether witches physically fly to their gatherings or experience a demonic delusion, concluding that their prosecution is justified by their apostasy and pacts with the devil regardless of the flight's physical reality.</summary>

### BOOK THREE

...of [Innocent] the Eighth first, then Julius the Second, the Great Pontiffs. <note>Refers to Pope Innocent VIII, who issued the bull *Summis desiderantes affectibus* against witchcraft in 1484, and Pope Julius II.</note> But nevertheless, take care lest you think that the majority of them are not transported in the body. **PHRONIMUS:** I warned this Apistius yesterday, Dicastes, that he should not reject as incredible what all or most people think is probable. It is a praise of Aristotle <margin>Aristotle.</margin>—which everyone quotes—that a widely held belief is not entirely false. Indeed, persuaded by this, Thomas Aquinas <margin>St. Thomas.</margin>—numbered among the heavenly citizens for his piety and among the most distinguished doctors for his knowledge—considered, especially in the second commentary on the *Theological Sentences*, that there are incubus and succubus demons <term>incubi and succubi; demons believed to take male or female forms respectively to have sexual intercourse with humans</term>, because many and distinguished men had come to that opinion. Therefore, Apistius, do not abhor what is accepted by such public report and by such a consensus of those who are well-regarded in letters. **DICASTES:** You have warned him very well. And so that greater faith may be held in this: come here, Witch <note>original: *Strix*</note>; swear upon this most sacred book which I have placed in your hands. Know that you are bound by such an oath that if you break your word, or if you lie even in the smallest detail, you should not think you will find mercy with us, nor in the world to come. **WITCH:** I have sworn. **DICASTES:** Were you carried to the game <note>original: *ludum*; a common term for the Sabbath or witches' gathering</note> in body or in spirit? **WITCH:** In both body and spirit at the same time. **DICASTES:** How do you know you were lifted through the air with your very body? **WITCH:** Because with these very hands I touched the demon, who was called Ludovicus. **DICASTES:** What did you touch? **WITCH:** His body. **DICASTES:** Was it like the body of any one of us? **WITCH:** It was softer. **DICASTES:** Were others there with their bodies? **WITCH:** A very great crowd. **DICASTES:** Thus all those whom I have ever subjected to questioning—even without torture—have asserted; thus all the other inquisitors record that those they questioned responded with one voice. Although they know this is not the reason they ought to be tortured or punished, but rather because of the broken faith they promised <note>Referring to the witch's perceived betrayal of their Christian baptismal vows</note>. Nevertheless, with one mouth, both males and females, whether in this town or elsewhere in the world, speak in this way. I will tell a history, not a fable: A few months ago, in the citadel of that town—which you see is most fortified, surrounded by such vast moats and girt with such high walls <margin>Note whether they go in body or in spirit only.</margin>—an infant boy was being carried by his mother in her arms, as is customary. When that most wicked priest—upon whom punishment has been visited—happened to see him. For while he was talking with his kinsman, the guardian of the citadel, the cunning man was present for the purpose of sucking <note>The Latin *suctum* refers to the belief that witches, like legendary screech-owls, sucked the blood of infants</note>...

# ON WITCHES
<note>original: DE STRIGIBVS</note>

...the day seemed longer to him than to those who have work to do. When night approached, he was lifted up by a Demon and carried through the air to the house where the boy himself lay in his cradle. He sucked the boy's blood in such a great quantity that the child appeared to be a vanishing shadow rather than a human boy; the cause of his paleness and illness remained entirely undiscovered until, having been condemned to the fire, the culprit sought pardon for the crime committed from the boy’s own father—whose tender blood he had sucked out—by pacing back and forth upon those high walls. Let antiquity go now and boast of its Maneaters <note>original: *Anthropophagos*; a legendary race of cannibals mentioned by Herodotus</note>, and let our own age marvel that men are found in the islands of the Eastern sea who feed on human flesh. For here in the middle of Italy, in a most civilized region far removed from all wildness, a numerous society has been discovered, fed on human blood by the instigation of malignant demons. 

But I return to you, Witch: what was the intercourse like with an aerial body? 

**WITCH:** I do not know what kind of body it was, but I knew it to be such that it brought more delight than intercourse with my husband. 

**DICASTES:** Did fear and horror not strike you, knowing it was a Demon? 

**WITCH:** I saw nothing except something human, except for the feet, which did not present themselves to my sight in the same way as the face, the chest, and the other members. 

**APISTIUS:** O, what a sight! 

<margin>Demons adapt various deceptions to the various conditions of men.</margin>

**PHRONIMUS:** Such a sight that the cruelty was hidden, while the smoothness, the softness, and the rest of the charms by which one is enticed were displayed. In seeking out the bed of Adonis in the woods, Venus is depicted as performing the duties of a huntress so that she might entice the hunter. Whence the ingenious poet <note>Referring to Ovid</note> says: *With knee bare, and her robe tucked up after the manner of Diana, she encourages the hounds and the animals of safe prey.* 

<margin>From the *Metamorphoses*. From the longer hymn to Venus.</margin>

Likewise, she deceived the shepherd Anchises in no other way than was suitable for a man living in the countryside. Thus in a certain hymn by Homer it is sung how she presented herself in the likeness of the maiden Admeta, both in size and appearance, whence it is written: <note>original Greek: Τη̃ δ’ αὖ του προπαροιθε Διὸς θυγάτηρ ἀφροδίτη παρθεvω ἀδμήτη μέγετοσ τέ καὶ εἶδοσ ὁμοίη.</note> "But before him stood Aphrodite, daughter of Zeus, like a maiden unwed in height and appearance."

**DICASTES:** Say it in Latin. 

**APISTIUS:** Say it in the modern way. 

**PHRONIMUS:** Success does not always smile upon boldness when translating Greek on the spot. 

**DICASTES:** Translate it; if you can do it correctly, fine; if not, do it anyway. 

**PHRONIMUS:** "Venus, born of thundering Jove, stood before Anchises, like the maiden Admeta in form and figure." 

**DICASTES:** What do you think the poet intended by that comparison? 

**PHRONIMUS:** What precedes and follows shows enough. For she sought him out as he was walking apart from his cattle...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Phronimus and Apistius regarding the physical nature of demons. Having just heard the Witch’s testimony about the pleasures of demonic intercourse, the speakers now turn to classical mythology and theology to explain how spirits without flesh can engage in such acts and even produce offspring.</meta>

# BOOK THREE

...and solitary, and by its splendor and grace it summoned him; and it drew him into admiration, pretending to be a mortal, and having narrated a genealogy with long-winded detours. Then it enticed him to intercourse. **APISTIUS.** I have certainly read that Anchises <note>A Trojan hero and father of Aeneas; in myth, he was struck by lightning for bragging about his affair with the goddess Aphrodite.</note> also paid the penalty with a thunderbolt because it was predicted to him that it would be so, whence that poem: *Zeus, having become angry at you, will strike you with a flaming thunderbolt* <note>original: "Ζεύς σέ χολωσάμενος βαλέει φλογόεντι κεραυνῷ"</note> and whatever the cause of the publicized crime may be that he had to be punished with that penalty, it nevertheless signifies beforehand that whoever of the gods experienced intercourse, they were treated badly by the heavens. 
<margin>From the same source.</margin>
Thus they fabled about Semele <note>Mother of Dionysus, who died when she saw Zeus in his true, divine form of a thunderbolt.</note> with the thunderbolt, nor am I far from that opinion: that I should think Callimachus <note>A Greek poet and scholar from Alexandria.</note> was followed in this matter, even though it is told otherwise by Ovid. That **Tiresias** was deprived of sight <note>original: *lumine captum*</note> because of an encounter with **Pallas** <note>Athena.</note>, either had or sought: although Callimachus fashioned it with a more honorable explanation as having happened to him because he had seen Pallas naked. **PHRONIMUS.** What then? **APISTIUS.** Since all these things are simulated, I think they are either entirely fabricated, or I am in doubt. **PHRONIMUS.** Do you think that in those ancient and heroic times **demons** <term>dæmonas: in this context, the pagan gods are reinterpreted as malevolent spirits or fallen angels</term> appeared—whom by our religion you do not doubt to be a race of malignant spirits? **APISTIUS.** Why not? Why do you not believe they sought the intercourse of women under the appearance of a man, or of men under the appearance of a woman? **APISTIUS.** <note>The speaker remains Apistius here, raising an objection to himself.</note> Because they are not made of flesh: so that they cannot be delighted by sexual pleasures. **PHRONIMUS.** It has been said quite often that it happened not for the sake of that thing, but for the sake of deceiving. **APISTIUS.** Yet they say they are the sons of gods and goddesses; and I remember it was said by you yesterday that some foundation remains under the fables. Where therefore is the seed itself in males? The birth in females? From what things do infant boys come forth? **PHRONIMUS.** You do not doubt without reason. Since even from a certain place of sacred **Genesis** narrated by **Moses**: 
<margin>Whose sons they ought to be considered who are born from the intercourse of demons.</margin>
while mention was made of the "sons of God and daughters of men," <note>Referring to Genesis 6:1-4, a passage often used to explain the origin of giants or demons.</note> some have thought such advances were signified; some preferred it to be referred to a just and unjust progeny. But it should not be thought for that reason, if any are called sons of **Jove** and **Apollo**, that they should be thought to have arisen from the seeds of demons—which they themselves lack—but rather they are the sons of that man from whom the seed which the demons use was taken. For the **Demon** who submits to a man (if I may be allowed to say so) acts as a female—that is, under the likeness of a female—and the same Demon, while he lies upon a woman under the appearance of a man, implants that seed in the genital parts. Therefore the woman who conceives from that seed must be thought to have conceived from that man from whom that very biological seed <note>original: *genitura*</note> flowed.

<summary>The dialogue explores how demons use the biological material of humans to produce offspring, explaining mythological "sons of gods" through the theory of the Succubus and Incubus. It argues that demons do not have their own seed but transport it from men to women to deceive and manipulate humanity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Phronimus and Apistius. Phronimus is explaining how demons use deception to create the illusion of "divine" children and how they introduced unnatural lusts into human history. The "DI." in the text likely refers to the "Disciple" (Apistius), responding to Phronimus's previous points.</meta>

# ON WITCHES

If any were truly believed to be the sons of Goddesses, one must think that the Demon stole true offspring from other women and presented them to those whom he was mocking; thus he deceived them once again so that they might nourish these supposititious <note>Children substituted for others; essentially "changelings."</note> infants as their own. In this way, if any wished to argue in favor of those whom antiquity claimed were the children of Gods and Goddesses—maintaining that there was no lie involved (though I certainly do not think so, as I have no doubt most of these tales are fables)—they would defend the birth of Aeneas in Asia and Achilles in Greece, who were famous in the heroic ages. Likewise, they would claim that Queen Olympias <note>The mother of Alexander the Great, who was rumored to have been fathered by Zeus in the form of a serpent.</note> in Macedonia and the mother of Africanus the Elder <note>Scipio Africanus; legends claimed a serpent was seen in his mother's bed before his birth.</note> in Italy conceived in that same manner. **APISTIUS.** These things you have narrated, Phronimus, are seen in the writings of Augustine. **PHRONIMUS.** Truly, he did not only entice women to intercourse, but even attempted masculine Venus <note>A classical euphemism for male-male sexual acts.</note>.

<margin>¶ The nefarious love of boys should be attributed to demons.</margin>

Whence it can easily come to mind that the obscene love of boys was attempted—following the example of demons—by those who said they were "plucking the flower" of youth. This practice first took its origin in Asia; from there it was led into Greece and Italy, and even as far as the Celts in a short space of time. For the abduction of Ganymede <note>A Trojan prince of great beauty whom Zeus, in the form of an eagle, abducted to serve as cupbearer to the gods.</note> in Troy is considered by the nations to be a most ancient event. The burning of Sodom and Gomorrah and three other Asian cities is most well known, not only among our people but among the heathens. Orpheus, having set out from Asia into Thrace and Greece, was the first author among the Thracians of that accursed vice, just as he was of the worship of many gods—unless one prefers the view that Thamyris <note>A mythical Thracian singer who was said to have been the first man to love another male.</note> was earlier. In Greece, it was once so well known that it was believed to be permitted by the wicked; among the Celts, Aristotle was the authority for the belief that it was once entirely without fault. But just as it is believed to have been extinguished in those regions by the benefit of Christian law, so it flourished greatly among the Persians, both through ancient crime and the absence of Christian law; for through that law we discern what is holy in morals, what is profane, and what is altogether wicked. Therefore, the Demon was not only so malignant as to delight women prone to lust, with nature stimulating them toward conception, but he devised a filthy and foul lust contrary to nature, and promised rewards to those who would endure it. To some, he promised eternal life, as to Ganymede, whom a vain antiquity recorded as having been taken into heaven no less impiously than impudently. To others, he promised divination, as to the shepherd Branchus <note>A youth loved by Apollo who received the gift of prophecy and founded the oracle at Didyma.</note>, to whom they fabled that a prophecy was inspired by Apollo. **APISTIUS.** Please, do not relate more...

<summary>Phronimus argues that "divine" heroes of antiquity were actually "changelings" provided by demons and asserts that unnatural lusts were demonic inventions introduced through figures like Orpheus and the myth of Ganymede.</summary>

### BOOK THREE

H

...many things to me, as well known as they are wonderful, which have not occurred continuously throughout the course of the centuries. If one can sound the retreat <note>To "sound the retreat" is a classical idiom meaning to end a discussion or stop a particular line of inquiry; Phronimus suggests they have finished discussing the ancient "heroic" era.</note> once we have moved from the heroic ages to the time of Scipio. **PHRONIMUS.** Speak of better things: in every time, in every age, such things occur. **APISTIUS.** Why then is it unknown? **PHRONIMUS.** Most things are known, though not all. **APISTIUS.** Why are they not celebrated <note>In the sense of being widely discussed or recorded in literature.</note>?

<margin>How the Demon lays snares differently in ancient times and in our own, and for what reasons.</margin>

**PHRONIMUS.** Two reasons occur to me. One is that since the wicked demon was cast out from his lordship over the world through the blood and death of Christ, he does not mock the crowds so frequently or so openly. For whereas he was once worshipped under the guise of divinity, he now dwells in deserted and hidden places like a deserter or a fugitive. The second reason is that he used to spread his nets of love for the whole human race. Now, however, he is most busy laying Venus’s snares <note>Lustful or sexual temptations.</note> for two types of people: the best and the worst. I call "the best" those who have devoted themselves entirely to God with all their strength. Having trampled upon and despised all allurements and pleasures—even the honorable ones—these are the ones he frequently attacks. But because these things happen in secret, they are not revealed, except sometimes for the example and salvation of others. I call "the worst" this class of witches <term>strigum: derived from 'strix,' a mythical screech-owl or hag believed to suck the blood of infants; here used for witches.</term> of whom we speak. You know, however, that their "loves" must be wrenched from them by threats and torments; for they do not speak freely of such things except among their fellow initiates. **APISTIUS.** So he sets his snares of love for holy men, and especially for those who have dedicated themselves to God? **PHRONIMUS.** If you knew their lives and deeds as recorded in writing, you would have no doubt.

<margin>Regarding St. Martin.</margin>

Sulpicius Severus <note>A 4th-century chronicler and biographer of St. Martin of Tours.</note> records that the demon attempted to mock that most holy man, Martin, under the persona of Jupiter, Mercury, Pallas, and Venus. Do you not think that when he falsely took the form of Jupiter, he promised kingdoms? When Mercury, eloquence and all the noble arts? When Pallas, wisdom and military discipline—to which Martin had already bid farewell? Under the image of Venus, what delights do you suppose he promised? With what white lead <note>Ceruse, a toxic lead-based white cosmetic used for a pale complexion.</note> or what purple dye <note>Purpurissum, a red pigment used for rouge.</note> did he paint her cheeks and lips?

<margin>Regarding St. Anthony.</margin>

Do you think he offered a many-colored garment, playful eyes, and seductive words? You may learn from Athanasius <note>St. Athanasius of Alexandria, who wrote the influential 'Life of Saint Anthony,' detailing his battles with demons in the desert.</note>, who recorded his deeds and character in literary monuments, in what ways the Demon tempted Anthony.

<margin>Regarding St. Francis.</margin>

Truly, let the snows be witnesses to how much heat the Demon cast upon Francis; he threw himself into them to extinguish those fires.

<margin>Regarding St. Benedict.</margin>

As for how much "softness" <note>Meaning carnal pleasure or luxury.</note> he presented to Benedict, let the thorns teach you, which as a remedy for the plague...

<summary>Phronimus explains that demons now work in secret rather than openly as gods, focusing their temptations on saints like Martin, Anthony, Francis, and Benedict, as well as on the "worst" class of humans, the witches.</summary>

While he was in Etruria <note>Modern-day Tuscany.</note> and was heading to Pisa on business, he set out from a certain town in the Pisan territory where he had lost what money he had in a game of dice. He was eventually carried to the city by his companions, who were traveling the same road but were a long way behind him. He was sick for six months and lost all his hair; he even asserted, under oath, that his leather boots were scorched as if they had been burned by a fire. He remembered afterward that he had been urged by her not to hold a spear in his hand that had an iron tip shaped like a cross; she had promised she would give him a more beautiful one. **APISTIUS.** Regarding intercourse, it is enough for me now to know that the Demon has provided such things from the very

<margin>About Jerome.</margin>
...than the rest of the philosophers. In this way, he drove more men, and more frequently, toward lust than he stirred them toward vain glory. Nor did he only trouble them from without, but he often provoked them within. If you think it matters little whether the demon attacked the internal senses or whether he walked petulantly before the eyes under the specter of Venus <note>The Roman goddess of love and sexual desire, here used to represent the personification of lustful temptation.</note>, ask Jerome. He is a man who committed the lives and deeds of great men to writing, and he will teach you sincerely about what they endured. Furthermore, he entrusted his own experiences to the memory of posterity, making it known that in his almost dead flesh, only the fires of lust still boiled <note>original: "in carne fere mortua sola libidinũ incendia bulliisse." Jerome famously described his ascetic life in the desert where, despite his fasting, his mind was still filled with visions of Roman dancing girls.</note>. **APISTIUS.** So Venus even attempted to join with Jerome? **PHRONIMUS.** Sulpicius Severus <note>A Christian writer and biographer of St. Martin of Tours.</note> certainly uses the name of Venus, and it was clearly seen how she laid snares for Martin under her persona. Whether she revealed herself to Jerome

<margin>Demons harm both externally and internally.</margin>
as a visible form or worked some mischief within him, we do not know for certain. However, I do not think it has escaped your notice that the most ancient authors among the pagans were accustomed to saying that Venus glides in both internally and externally. When she makes herself present to the eyes, the way to detect her is easy. But when she creeps into the imaginary power <note>A term in medieval psychology referring to the "phantasia," the faculty of the mind that produces mental images.</note> and stirs the senses, not everyone is able to recognize her secret traps. Thus, in the Orphic hymns <note>A collection of ancient Greek poems attributed to the mythical singer Orpheus.</note>, you may see Venus called both "visible and invisible" <note>original Greek: "φανoμένη καὶ ἀφανής" (phanomenē kai aphanēs).</note>. Likewise, those "Loves" who proceed from her were said to wound souls with "intellectual arrows" <note>The text suggests that these are not physical arrows, but mental impulses or thoughts that pierce the mind. Original Greek: "νοεροῖσιν... βελέμνοις" (noeroisin... belemnois).</note>. Hence, in another of those hymns, Orpheus sings that she both appears and does not appear; and in another, he shows that souls are sought by intellectual arrows. In the hymn to the Lycian Venus by Proclus <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist philosopher.</note> and the Platonists, there is that song: "having the symbols of an intellectual marriage and intellectual wedding songs" <note>original Greek: "συμβόλ’ ἔχων νοεροῦ γάμου νοερῶν ὑμεναίων" (symbol’ echōn noerou gamou noerōn hymenaiōn). This refers to a spiritual or philosophical union rather than a physical one.</note>, so that they might indicate these mental unions. **APISTIUS.** But 

<margin>About Socrates's demon and its varying familiarity with others.</margin>
Apuleius <note>A Roman philosopher and author of "The Golden Ass" who wrote "On the God of Socrates."</note> judged that it was a God, not a demon, that appeared to Socrates. **PHRONIMUS.** Yet Plutarch and Maximus of Tyre call it a demon; the former discussed it in one book, the latter in two. But why is it said that one demon took up the patronage of Plato, another of Zeno, and another of Diogenes? Why did one haunt Plotinus? Truly, it was to deceive. For we should not listen to those who have preached that demons have various natures—some thinking that they delight in city affairs, others in the country, some being earthly, and others maritime. These are the dreams of foolish nations, clearly related to those who babbled that some demons practice medicine, others look after naval matters, some preside over divination, some like laws, and others wield arms. In this way, they claimed that Aesculapius and Podalirius <note>Legendary Greek figures associated with healing; Aesculapius was the god of medicine.</note> sent healing dreams...

...slippery. Thus they fabled that the Dioscuri <note>Castor and Pollux, twin heroes in Greek mythology often associated with St. Elmo's fire and the protection of sailors.</note> presided over sea storms. Thus Rhesus, Achilles, and Theseus—even before the Trojan times—were said to engage in military affairs after their lives had ended. But those deeds were done in secret, while this was in the open field. For fame reports that during the battle against the Medes <note>The Persians.</note> at Marathon, a phantom of Theseus fought on behalf of the Athenians, a story which Plutarch also committed to the memory of posterity. For they used to think that demons were nothing other than souls stripped of their bodies; because of this, they believed Aesculapius <note>The god of medicine.</note> could heal, Minos and Rhadamanthus <note>Judges of the underworld.</note> could pass judgment, the Dioscuri could ward off storms, and Amphilochus, Mopsus, Orpheus, and Trophonius could prophesy. They believed Rhesus, Achilles, and Theseus handled the business of war. The author of all these things was a demon, intended to make them believe, so that men might be more easily enticed; and being imbued with a vain hope, they would perform sacred rites to them as if to the spirits of heroes. It is clear that neither Plato nor Aristotle shrank from this superstition while they were prescribing laws for public affairs and debating the institutions and arts of citizens. Indeed, even in our own times, it is clearly accepted that demons are carried about in vials and rings, and that responses are given from the belly and the hip as if by a Pythian spirit <note>A reference to the Delphic Oracle; here it describes ventriloquism or "gastromancy" attributed to demonic possession.</note>. Thus we may recognize that the enemy of the human race has, at various times, devised various ways of deceiving under the appearance of familiarity. **APISTIUS.** I certainly think so. But I do not yet recognize the "game" <note>The "ludus" or "game" refers to the gathering later known as the Witches' Sabbath.</note>. **DICAEARCHUS.** Gradually you are being drawn toward our opinion. **APISTIUS.** Not indeed "drawn," but led by reasons and testimonies. **DICAEARCHUS.** Know that you are bound by the same oath, O Witch. And unless you produce the truth about the "game," you shall be punished by this fire before us—which we see is eternal—and removed from our eyes. **STRIX.** I know it well. **DICAEARCHUS.** Do you eat and drink at that game? <meta>This page continues the discussion on demonic apparitions and the historical precedents for supernatural gatherings. The previous page discussed how demons impersonated ancient heroes like Theseus and the Dioscuri to foster pagan worship. This page moves into specific accounts of miraculous "tables" or banquets described by ancient geographers, linking them to the activities of modern witches.</meta>

### ON WITCHES

-># BOOK THREE<-

Though Solinus differs from Herodotus, and Pomponius Mela varies from him, we recognize a multifaceted superstition in their accounts. Herodotus records that in a suburban meadow, the magistrates set out meat by night, which the people eat by day; yet it is claimed by the voices of the locals that the earth itself provides these things. Solinus, however, relates that this table is in the place of the harmful <note>original: "noxiorum." This likely refers to the "Table of the Sun" in Ethiopia mentioned by Herodotus and Solinus, where food was said to miraculously appear on a table in a meadow.</note>, always filled with sumptuous feasts where everyone eats indiscriminately, and they are said to be increased by divine power. But Pomponius Mela, whether it be in a suburb or a prison, does not say a word except to affirm that the feasts are born suddenly by divine power. And although these accounts do not agree in every detail, they share this common and undisputed point: that the feast is provided in a miraculous way by the Sun alone. This matter corresponds to the feast of this Diana—who, as it was held, was the sister of Phoebus, who is also called the Sun. Should we not also think that this relates to what Pomponius Mela also reported in his description of the world: that there is a place where many fires flash at night, and what look like camps lying far and wide are shown; cymbals and drums clash, and flutes are heard sounding louder than those of humans? The magical banquets of Ulysses also displayed this appearance, with blood poured all around, to which demons betook themselves under various likenesses. That hunter <note>likely referring to the Valesian or a similar figure in Philostratus’s "Heroicus."</note> related this custom—of the soul of Ulysses being drawn out by Homer—he who was said to converse with the shadows of Protesilaus and other heroes in the works of Philostratus. The witches <term>striges<gloss>A term for malicious female spirits or witches, often associated with owls and the consumption of blood.</gloss></term> of our own time now drink blood, which they mostly take from children and keep in vessels to prepare that profane and wicked ointment. And while these things might seem sufficient regarding the banquet, I will nonetheless add the table of Achilles.

<margin>On the table of Achilles.</margin>
**AP.** What will this be? **PH.** I am making nothing up; ask Maximus of Tyre <note>A Greek rhetorician and philosopher of the 2nd century AD.</note>. If you do so, he reports—or rather, he will show it recorded in his own papers written several centuries ago, that is, over a thousand years—that in his time the table of Achilles was well known, very similar to the one where they say the witches recline. **AP.** I have faith in your words. **PH.** Unless you have the book, the author’s work is with me, both in the Greek and the Latin translation by Donatus. In it, he writes that in an island of the Euxine Sea <note>The Black Sea.</note> there is a temple of Achilles, in which he was frequently seen, and he even invited men who traveled there to a banquet. And there were Patroclus, Thetis, and other demons, or as he says in his own words, a chorus of demons <note>original: "δαιμόνων χορόν"</note>. To pass over what he wrote about Hector being customarily seen in Troy, and that the Dioscuri were seen by him in a time of danger at sea...

<meta>The catchword "na/" at the bottom of the page suggests the next page continues with "navigantes" (sailing/sailors) or "navagio" (shipwreck), likely completing the sentence about the Dioscuri appearing to those in peril at sea.</meta>

<meta>This page continues a dialogue concerning the deceptive nature of demonic banquets. The previous page ended while describing the magical feasts of Ulysses and the use of blood in such rituals. This page recounts a specific contemporary anecdote from the Alps to illustrate how demons mimic holy rites to deceive the faithful.</meta>

<header>ON WITCHES</header>

...had brought help by means of blood. For these things do not pertain to the banquet of spirits <note>original: "lemurum," referring to the lemures, which in Roman mythology were the restless or malevolent spirits of the dead.</note>. **AP.** They do not seem foreign to the banquet of Nereus and Oceanus <note>Sea deities in Greek and Roman mythology.</note>, which various poets have mentioned. **PH.** So that the Demon might snatch away from men the banquet of Christ prepared upon His table in His own kingdom, he devised these deadly banquets.

<margin>Note a marvelous thing.</margin>
But I will not now recount to you a banquet celebrated by any poets; instead, I will bring forward what was told to me a few years ago by a great man, endowed with the most distinguished dignity and power, and very great wealth, supported by the most reliable testimony. About twelve years ago, a certain priest in the Rhaetian Alps <note>A region in the central Alps, covering parts of modern-day Switzerland, Italy, and Austria.</note>, while he was about to provide the Viaticum <term>Viaticum: the Eucharist or "saving host" given to a person near death to sustain them on their final journey.</term> to a sick man, and since the man was too far away for him to reach quickly and easily on foot, he mounted a horse. With a wooden pyx <term>Pyx: a small, portable container used by priests to carry the consecrated bread (the Host) to those unable to attend church.</term> hanging from his neck—in which the sacred host was kept as the provision for the dying man—he set out on his journey. Along the way, he met someone who invited him to see a marvelous thing. He unthinkingly agreed, and when he dismounted from his horse, he both saw and felt himself being carried immediately through the air with his companion in a short space of time. They were both placed on a very high mountain peak, where there was a wide expanse of meadows surrounded by very tall trees and enclosed by threatening cliffs. In the middle, dances and all kinds of games were seen; feasts were prepared, playful spectacles, and whatever is usually done on earth to delight the souls of mortals. The priest was amazed, and not daring to open his mouth for astonishment, he asked his companion of the aerial journey if he also should worship the Lady and offer her a gift according to custom. For there was a most beautiful appearance of a certain queen seated on a golden throne, towards whom everyone was proceeding in pairs or in fours, in various orders, both to greet her and to worship her with gifts. Hearing the mention of a "Lady," and seeing her equipped with such splendor and so many attendants, he thought she was the Virgin Mary, the Mother of Christ, the Queen of Heaven and Earth. For he was one who did not recognize the illusions <note>original: "præstigia," meaning demonic trickery or sensory deceptions.</note>, nor had he, as far as he knew, admitted any superstition that would have led him there. Therefore, thinking that no gift could be more pleasing to the Mother than the consecrated body of her only-begotten Son, he approached the throne to worship. Falling at her knees, he took the pyx which hung from his neck in his hands and placed it in her lap. Immediately, both the throne and the image that was upon it vanished. The multitude of dancers disappeared from sight...
<meta>The catchword "dentiū" indicates the next page begins with "ludentium" (of the dancers/players).</meta>

<summary>This page describes a priest in the Rhaetian Alps who is lured by a demon to a supernatural gathering on a mountain. Thinking he is witnessing a heavenly court, he offers the consecrated Host to a seated "Queen," only for the demonic illusion to instantly vanish upon contact with the sacred object.</summary>

### BOOK THREE

...descending: and the prepared feasts nowhere appeared, even in the slightest. And the count himself, under whose guidance he had seen those very things, was neither found nor discovered. Realizing what had happened, he turned to God with prayers, begging that he not be deserted in such a desolate place. Soon, when he could find no trace of either humans or wild beasts there, and not knowing which way to turn, he decided to press on. Thus, he made his way through rugged rocks to find out where on earth he had been placed.

After a great distance had been covered, he learned from a certain shepherd that he was nearly a hundred miles away from the place where he had begun his journey. Returning home, he met the magistrate of the Emperor Maximilian <note>Maximilian I (1459–1519), Holy Roman Emperor.</note> and explained the matter to him in order, just as I have told it to you. That this could be done by a Demon, the consensus of theologians should reveal to you; they assert that corporeal nature obeys the command of substances separated from matter <note>Spiritual beings or spirits.</note> at a mere nod, insofar as it pertains to local motion, by which a thing is moved from one place to another. 

Henry and James <note>Henricus Institoris (Heinrich Kramer) and Jacobus Sprenger, the Dominican inquisitors who authored the infamous witch-hunting manual *Malleus Maleficarum*.</note>, German theologians, can provide many examples of human bodies carried through the air, both from the records of learned men and by the testimony of their own eyes. You will also be in possession of their "Hammer" <note>original: "malleo," referring to the *Malleus Maleficarum* (The Hammer of Witches), first published in 1487.</note>, if you wish to use that very hammer against those who resist.

**AP.** I have heard a wonderful thing with my own ears, which neither the shadows of night can obscure with darkness, nor dreams counteract. Nor is it the result of any force from a painful interrogation, in fear of which a man might confess to things he never did. But indeed, the only thing remaining for me to ask in this matter is why, at the touch of the sacred 

<margin>A doubt with a manifold solution.</margin>

host, everything vanished completely, since demons not only do not fear its touch, but even command and see to it that many of them are thrown down and trampled upon while the mockery is performed.

**DI.** It should not seem strange to you; for although we know that demons tremble at the sign of the cross, sometimes they nevertheless sneak in under the image of Christ to deceive. If you had read the deeds of Martin, Francis, and many others of our heavenly saints, no wonder would have held you. Add to this that Christ himself, while he lived in mortal flesh as the driver away of demons, permitted himself to be tempted by a demon, and to be placed here upon the pinnacle of the temple, and there upon the peak of a mountain. 

Thus in Matthew <note>Gospel of Matthew 4:1–11, describing the Temptation of Christ.</note> it says "he was led up" <note>original Greek: *anēchthē* (ἀνήχθη), the passive form meaning 'was led up' into the wilderness by the Spirit.</note> which can be translated as "taken up," and then "he takes him" <note>original Greek: *paralambanei* (παραλαμβάνει).</note>. This word signifies to take along, and "he set him" <note>original Greek: *estēsen* (ἔστησεν).</note>, which means he positioned him, clearly declares what was done. Finally, He permitted himself to be crucified by the ministers of the Devil. Furthermore, you suppose, as told by witches <term>Striges: originally bird-like monsters of Roman folklore that fed on flesh; in this period, the term was commonly used to mean "witches."</term>, that the Demon trampled upon sacred...

It cites the Malleus Maleficarum and the Temptation of Christ as evidence that demons can physically move people and holy objects for deceptive purposes.</summary>
