### ON WITCHES

...sacrificial hosts <note>original: "hostias"; referring to the consecrated bread the priest was carrying on the previous page.</note>, the matter is not as it seems: the malice of humans carries it out, which the Demons procure; they do not commit these acts themselves. Furthermore, the priest's simplicity, although imprudent, and especially the power of his faith, was the reason why he was not only saved from being deluded to his own destruction, but was also able to warn others and reveal a matter that seemed controversial: namely, that the "game" <note>An illusion or demonic play.</note> we are discussing can occur in the body and not only in the imagination. Finally, the power of divine providence, which can never be praised enough, sometimes permits this to happen and sometimes prohibits it. Why it happens differently can always be assigned a just cause in a general sense, but it cannot always be explained in detail; the sharp edge of the human mind is so dull when it comes to scrutinizing divine secrets.

AP. These things resonate with my mind very much.

PH. If anything remains to be asked of Dicastus, do not allow time to be wasted in vain, and take into account that the sun is already sinking toward its setting. You should fear lest, once the town gates are closed, we be forced to spend the night here—where we shall have neither a bed nor anything comfortable to ward off the harms of a winter night—in this monastery <note>original: "coenobio"; a communal religious house.</note> which has barely even begun to be built.

AP. Almost nothing seems left to ask except about sorceries <note>original: "veneficiis"; specifically poisonings or magical crimes.</note>. <margin>On sorceries</margin>

DI. What specifically?

AP. Whether they perform these things in reality <note>original: "Re... ipsa"; in the thing itself.</note>, or whether they only seem so in the mind. For why God sometimes permits those things to happen (if they do happen) and sometimes prohibits them, the power of divine providence—always just, though never fully understood—has already been sufficiently revealed.

PH. Are you mindful of both Lucian of Samosata and Lucius of Madaura? <note>Lucian (c. 125–180 AD) wrote "Lucius, or the Ass"; Lucius of Madaura refers to Apuleius (c. 125–170 AD), author of "The Golden Ass." Both stories involve a man transformed into a donkey.</note>

AP. Certainly. For I have read them occasionally, and I heard you discussing that matter the day before yesterday. But I doubt if the things contained in that Greek and Latin work about the Ass are facts rather than fictions. <margin><unclear>Concerning transformations or beasts...</unclear></margin>

PH. Just as I do not doubt that many things are fictions—even most, or if you prefer, all of them—I still contend they were not invented from nothing. For even our Augustine thought that the narrated <term>transformations</term> <note>The changing of one's shape or nature into another.</note> of the birds of Diomedes, the beasts of Circe, and the Arcadian wolves found in Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BC), a prolific Roman scholar.</note> did not take their occasion from nothing at all. He even related in the eighteenth book of *The City of God* <note>St. Augustine’s "De Civitate Dei," a foundational work of Christian philosophy.</note> that in his own time, many things were accustomed to happen in Italy that were similar to those which Apuleius either pointed out or invented. He asserted this, however: that Demons can achieve nothing through the power of nature unless Almighty God permits it—whose judgments are many and hidden, but never unjust. Therefore, if Demons do anything of this sort, they are said to change only in appearance <note>original: "specie tenus"; only to the extent of the visual form.</note> those things which were created by the one and true God, so that...

<summary>The speakers discuss whether demonic activity and transformations (like those in Apuleius's "Golden Ass") are physical or imaginary, concluding that while often fictional, they reflect a reality permitted by God's providence as discussed by St. Augustine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Dicastus and Phronimus. ### BOOK THREE

->I<-

...by that very change they seem to be what they are not: and he refers everything either to an **imaginary spirit**, or to a **demon** substituting one thing for another. Indeed, he <note>Referring to St. Augustine, whom the speakers are discussing.</note> thinks that the imaginary spirit of a man, as if embodied in the likeness of some animal, appears to the senses of others. And in this way a man seems such even to himself. He believes this explains the deeds and acts of those who seemed to be **donkeys** <note>original: "asinorum"</note> carrying loads in the form of a pack-horse, as well as the **disputation of the philosopher** who argued Platonist theories without a body <note>A reference to a story where a philosopher appeared to a student in a dream or vision to explain a difficult passage of Plato.</note>, and the matter of the **Arcadian werewolves** <note>original: "versipelle"; literally "skin-changers," a term used for those who transform into wolves.</note>, and the **songs of Circe** <note>A sorceress from Greek mythology who turned Odysseus's men into swine.</note> transforming the companions of **Ulysses**—all these should be credited to the imaginary spirit. However, he believes the **hind** <note>A female deer.</note> substituted for **Iphigenia**, and the **birds** substituted for the companions of **Diomedes**, were actually placed there by a Demon.

DI. Phronimus has recounted this from **Augustine** rightly and most briefly, and no less truly, I believe. For it is recognized by the full consensus of **Theologians** that the human sense and the imaginary spirit are subject to demonic power by their very nature. This is because a demon is a "separate substance"—an intellect set apart from all bodily mass—and is therefore superior to our physical senses. It is also recorded in histories that narrate the deeds of those who chose to lead their lives through vast wildernesses and deserted wastes <note>original: "tesqua"; rough, wild terrain.</note>, that through **witchcraft** it was brought about that a woman appeared to be a **mare**. That illusion <note>original: "praestigium"; a demonic trick that deceives the eyes.</note> was removed by the prayers and oration of the most holy man **Macarius** <note>St. Macarius of Egypt, a 4th-century desert hermit.</note>. The malignant demon was able to stir the internal sense in many people, which is why she appeared to them as a beast by thrusting that image into their inner perception where the mockery took place; but he could not stir the sense of Macarius, who was uniquely supported by divine help.

<margin>
Note an illusion very similar to those of the ancients.
</margin>

In the same way, the sight of others was deceived by the phantom <note>original: "simulachrum"</note> of that man who seemed to be a **donkey in Salamis, Cyprus**. He remained under the power of that sorceress whose drug he had felt disturbing his mind, and the appearance of a beast surrounded him. For three years he was subjected to carrying wood like a beast of burden, until he was helped by the wisdom of **Genoese merchants**. When they noticed him prostrating himself in worship at the posts of a sacred church, they realized he was not a beast. They took care to see him freed from the evil spell <note>original: "maleficio"</note> and ensured the witch paid the penalty. Truly, many things can be feigned; many things may seem different than they are through the **illusion** <term>prestigium<gloss>A deceptive trick or "glamour" created by a demon to make a person or object look like something else.</gloss></term> of demons. While both the soul and the body remain the same in the man, many things can be troubled. Furthermore, it happens that the eyes of spectators are deluded, and the sharp edge of the mind is disturbed when the imaginary power is stirred. But also the body, as we have said, can be carried to different places. Thus it happens that those who have not examined these things separately are easily deceived, provided they duly follow the various contexts...

<summary>This page discusses whether magical transformations (like men turning into donkeys or wolves) are physical or mental illusions. Citing St. Augustine and the miracle of St. Macarius, the text argues that demons manipulate human "internal senses" to create these appearances while the actual body and soul remain unchanged.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the dialogue between the three speakers. Having discussed various supernatural phenomena, the skeptic Apistus is finally convinced of the reality of demonic influence, leading to a symbolic name change as they conclude their journey and return to town.</meta>

# ON WITCHES, BOOK I

They do not notice, nor do they judge how great a difference there is between that which is **created** and that which is **permitted** by some natural order of things; what is made anew, what is made in part, what is true, what is similar to the truth, and what presents its own image versus what presents a borrowed one. They do not weigh with a just scale the force of universal nature, nor the power of **demons**; nor, finally, do they consider the judgments of God, which are often most hidden but always most just. 

**PH.** But now, Dicastus, the twilight of night approaches, and it urges us to return home. For if this discussion is not enough for you, Apistus, I clearly do not see what would be enough, since you have been able to perceive that this play—drawn both from antiquity and from the events of our own time—is no empty fable. In its substance, it is most ancient; in many of its conditions, it is more recent; changed from time to time by the will of the **Demon**, and perhaps to be changed again, so great is that ancient hater of men in his proficiency for deceiving. 

<margin>Epilogue</margin> I have shown you the circles, the ointments, the magical words, the journeys through the expanses of the air, and the couplings of demons—found both in our own times and in the heroic ages. I have shown that malicious demons from the earliest antiquity devised slanders against the human race, deluded them with oracular responses, deceived them through familiarity, and attempted to mock every age and sex with various phantasms and images. They have feigned divinity, provided harmful banquets to mortals, and carried them like **winged beasts of burden**, and sought out most wicked couplings with them. 

But since I see that in your mind you have set out abroad, to the various shores of **Italy** and **Sicily**, across the **Ionian Sea**, across the **Euxine** <note>The Black Sea.</note>, and I myself have led you in my words now into **Africa**, now into **Asiam**, to the **Hyperboreans** <note>A mythical people of the far north.</note>—and where have I not led you?—will a return home with me never be granted to you? 

**AP.** It will. For enough has been done for me. 

**DI.** Do you agree then? 

**AP.** I agree. 

**DI.** In truth or in jest? 

**AP.** Do you think it a jest that I should judge this matter: in which all antiquity and all posterity agree? A matter at which Poets, Rhetoricians, writers of history, lawmakers, <term>philosophers</term> <note>original shorthand: "phi"</note>, theologians, prudent men, soldiers, and peasants all aim their mark, even when personal experiments are set aside—even if some refuse to acknowledge it, who consider themselves alone to be wise. 

**DI.** And so you have changed your opinion. 

**AP.** Far beyond any doubt; and since I am now of a different state of mind, I wish hereafter to be called by a different name. 

**DI.** As you please; and you shall be called <term>Pisticus</term> <note>Pisticus: From the Greek word for "faithful" or "believer." This contrasts with his original name, Apistus, which means "unbeliever."</note> henceforth. 

**AP.** It pleases me well. 

**PH.** Therefore, if there is absolutely nothing left that you desire to discuss: let us depart, and with the good leave of the investigator, let us return to the town. 

->The End.<-

The speakers conclude their debate on the nature of witches and demonic illusions as night falls, agreeing to return to the city.</summary>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>I ii</sig>
<meta>visible ghosting from the verso page</meta>

### TO POPE LEO X AND THE LATERAN COUNCIL.
By the same Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola,
Lord, on the Reformation of Morals
## AN ORATION.

<image-desc>A large decorative historiated drop cap 'S' at the beginning of the text, featuring ornate floral scrollwork and foliage within a square frame.</image-desc>

If anyone, perhaps, should desire that words be spoken in this most sacred assembly and gathering of our <term>Christian Commonwealth</term> <note>original: "Christianæ Rei.P." (Res publica), referring to the collective body of Christendom.</note> concerning the passing of laws to restrain the audacity of wicked men, let him first consider within himself how it is necessary to punish those who have held in contempt—and now especially hold in contempt—those laws which were rightly established by our ancestors and held in the highest esteem. He will not doubt that an oration ought to be held, before all else, concerning the restoration of the morals of men, which have lain collapsed for so long, and their reduction to the standard <note>literally "the norm" or "the rule"</note> of laws already established. For even though I do not believe it should be brought into dispute that this most holy <term>synod</term> <note>A council of church leaders gathered to decide on points of doctrine or administration.</note> ought to pass certain laws, I nevertheless think it better that the leading men, and the **Cardinals** themselves—upon whom the whole weight of our Christian Commonwealth turns and is governed—should guard the most holy decrees of the ancient Fathers and the most honorable institutions.

If this were done, the people would easily follow the **Bishops** <note>original: "Antistites," a term for high-ranking prelates or overseers.</note> themselves as if they were animated and living laws, and they would be recalled to the standard of piety and true discipline. I do not by any means spurn new consultations and decrees, but I consider the guarding of ancient sanctions to be necessary above all else, so that those things which have already fallen away and decayed may first be restored by pious morals, and then bound by severe laws. Indeed, I would not deny that there is more need for censors, for punishers of the laws themselves, and for defenders, than for legislators. 

And although I am not unaware that the burden of discussing this matter is greater than I can easily bear (for it would require stronger shoulders and demand more robust strength), nevertheless, because by its very nature it pertains to everyone, I do not think it is foreign to me. And if up to this day, in such a great crowd of most learned men, I had seen even one person who had exactly taken this same task upon himself, I would have thought that I should cease altogether. But since I know that talk has often been held regarding the establishing of laws, but regarding the guarding of laws either none or [very little]...

<summary>In this opening of an oration to Pope Leo X and the Fifth Lateran Council, Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola argues that the Church needs the enforcement of existing ancient laws and moral reform among its leaders more than it needs the creation of new legislation.</summary>
<keywords>Pope Leo X, Lateran Council, Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola, Church reform, ecclesiastical law, Cardinals, moral discipline, Christian Commonwealth</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal oration by Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola to the Fifth Lateran Council. The previous page introduced the necessity of restoring ancient laws and moral standards within the Church hierarchy to prevent the collapse of the Christian world.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>ON THE REFORM OF MORALS</header>

...continually, as the judgments of men are varied, moved by various circumstances of things. It seemed to me not out of place if I were to bring forward some points, so that the most holy institutions of our ancestors might be more easily recalled to memory—institutions by which, when observed, our <term>Christian Commonwealth</term> <note>original: "Christianā Res. P." (Res publica). Pico uses this to describe the collective political and spiritual body of Christendom.</note> has always both been increased and preserved. Yet, once these were neglected and held in contempt, it has suffered countless losses; and had it not been divinely preserved, it would have fallen into such misery and calamity that it would be in vain for you now, **Most Holy Father Leo X**, and you **Cardinals** and **Bishops** of the Church, to hold this assembly so that it might be restored to a better state.

I certainly wished that someone from the number of most learned men had taken up this task for himself, so that the speech might have greater authority and power in the minds of the listeners; but perhaps the naked truth and pure simplicity will not lack strength. That laws were established by the ancient fathers regarding the avoidance of luxury, lust, and greed—there is no one who does not know this. Even the unlearned know that norms were handed down regarding Piety, Justice, and the rest of the virtues. We experience more than we would wish that these are partly neglected and partly lie dormant.

<margin>
Pay attention to this most true point.
</margin>

Many leaders of our Commonwealth turn ancient simplicity into **Cunning**, chastity into **Wickedness**, and liberality and thrift into **Luxury and Greed**. Among most of the leaders of our religion—by whose example the ignorant common people ought to have been fashioned and formed—there is either no worship of God, or certainly a meager one; no method or institution for living well, no shame, no modesty. Justice has declined either into hatred or into favoritism. Piety has almost collapsed into superstition, and sin is committed openly among all ranks of men. It has reached such a point that, frequently, virtue is turned into a vice by the wicked, and vices are accustomed to be honored in place of virtues by those who have come to believe that the walls and ramparts of their crimes are their unheard-of insolence and long-lasting impunity. 

These are the diseases, these are the wounds you must heal, **Supreme Pontiff**; otherwise, if you refuse to apply a remedy (since this task falls chiefly to you for many reasons), I fear that—not with ointments, but with fire and iron—He whose place you hold on earth will cut away and destroy the affected members themselves. I truly believe He has already given signs of that future medicine. 

<margin>
See this most unspeakable matter.
</margin>

Did the girls dedicated to God in the plundering of **Brescia** <note>original: "Brixiana". Referring to the 1512 Sack of Brescia by the French, where contemporary accounts reported horrific violence against nuns in their convents.</note>, dragged from the most religious temples, fall to the soldiers as plunder? Were the priests slaughtered in the destruction of **Ravenna**? <note>original: "Rauennati". The Battle of Ravenna (1512) was one of the bloodiest of the Italian Wars, involving the brutal sacking of the city.</note> Or in the [sack] of **Prato**... <note>original: "Pratensi". The Sack of Prato (1512) by Spanish troops was another contemporary example of extreme wartime atrocity used here as a warning of divine judgment.</note>

<summary>Pico della Mirandola delivers a blistering critique of the moral decay among the Church's leadership, warning Pope Leo X that divine punishment—already glimpsed in the violent sacks of Italian cities—will follow if reform is not enacted.</summary>
<keywords>Fifth Lateran Council, Pope Leo X, moral reform, Sack of Brescia, Sack of Ravenna, corruption, divine judgment, Christian Commonwealth</keywords>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola’s oration to Pope Leo X and the Fifth Lateran Council. The previous page lamented the neglect of ancient moral laws, which has led the Christian Commonwealth to the brink of ruin. Here, Pico describes the specific horrors and divine warnings currently facing Italy and the Church.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>ORATION.</header>

...in the slaughter of <term>Prato</term> <note>referring to the brutal Sack of Prato in 1512 by Spanish troops, which led to the return of the Medici to power in Florence.</note>, were virgins once celebrated for their holiness not prostituted? What else, **Most Holy Father**? What else have they given us to understand, but that sacred dwellings and temples were previously entrusted to pimps and <term>catamites</term> <note>original: "Catamycis"; young boys kept for sexual purposes.</note>, that the sheepfolds of the Best Shepherd were handed over to nefarious wolves, and that enclosures in many cities once dedicated to virgins have been converted into brothels and obscene dens? 

But I fear those were only the beginnings of evils, and foretastes of a bitter and mournful potion about to be served to us by the treacherous deserters of our Religion, unless we turn it away with good morals, and in prayers and supplications—not from the tips of our lips, but from the innermost depths of our souls—we appease an angry God. At His warnings and these celestial thunders, we have seemed not to prick up our ears so that we might recover our senses, but rather to lower them, going deaf like the <term>Ethiopians</term> <note>A common classical trope; ancient writers like Cicero claimed people living near the cataracts of the Nile were deafened by the constant roar of the water.</note> at the roar of the falling Nile. 

<margin>Attend to the calamities of our time.</margin>

We are affected by depraved custom in such a way that we listen to the persuasion of vices, but ignore the warnings of God, Most Good and Most Great, the most wise, who, I believe, has judged that He will no longer tolerate being despised by our age: a judgment shown by many signs and portents—pestilence, famine, and the most bloody battles a short while ago. 

For what else—or if something else, why does this not sink clearly into our minds?—is meant by so many thousands of slain men, whose corpses have both covered the fields and blocked the courses of rivers, and whose blood has stained the **Upper and Lower Seas** <note>The Adriatic and Tyrrhenian seas surrounding Italy.</note> as if with a kind of purple dye, if not to bring to our memory that the countless benefits bestowed upon us should not be a mockery? Namely, that Christ Himself, the Savior of the human race, shed so much blood so that those who were not only brought back to life by that blood but also enriched by it, should now fail under these rains of blood? 

Why have I mentioned luxury? I passed over the crowds of harlots and the flocks of <term>prostitutes</term> <note>original: "exoletoꝝ"; specifically male prostitutes or those grown old in vice.</note>, and the priesthoods bought and sold. I was able to hope, **Most Holy Father**, after you were raised to the highest peak of the priesthood—not by evil arts, nor by shameful pacts and trades as many were in the past (who sought the highest honor through the highest disgrace), but by your most gracious humanity (as it seemed to me and to many others)—that at some point the **Christian Commonwealth** would be reformed to a better state. 

But I do not despair entirely: if you are willing to strive, and to that humanity, leniency, and approachability of yours, you take care to mix in some gravity, some rigor, and some measure of punishment, **Greatest Pontiff**, so that our Commonwealth may suffer no harm. For you have a war with many, I say, an internal war: dangerous and grave, which must be turned away by the severity of discipline; luxury...

<header>ON CHANGING MORALS</header>

...restrain [men] of every rank, place a limit on ambition, and curb the untamed and unbridled furies of obscene lust. Set reins upon the suspicious cohabitation of priests (as is also provided for by the laws of the church <note>original: "legibus ecclesiae cautum est"; Pico refers to Canon Law regarding clerical celibacy and the prohibition of "housekeepers" who were effectively common-law wives.</note>). Fix an end to greed and the wicked desire for possession. Reclaim the patrimonies of pious men, which were not so much entrusted and commended to the faith of priests as they were truly consumed and devoured by them; ensure that they are distributed for good uses, so that they are no longer swallowed up by impure gluttons—by whirlpools of all crimes. Let those who have kept watch over the auctions of sacred things and shameful biddings <note>A reference to simony, the illegal selling of church offices or services.</note> pay the penalties for their reckless trade. Let those who have not looked after the flock committed to them, and who have devoted themselves to countless vanities, be corrected. Those who delight in superstitions, revelries, shameful exercises, and inappropriate familiarities should either be entirely corrected or removed from sacred duties. 

Since indeed they defile everything by their example, and by the depraved habit of their lives they plainly corrupt those who are well-mannered and excellently instructed, the result is that not only the common people, but every rank of society falls into crime. Nor is it truly a wonder, since John Chrysostom <note>An influential 4th-century Church Father and Archbishop of Constantinople known for his eloquent preaching.</note> judges that every evil comes forth from the temple,

You are able, **Supreme Pontiff**, and no other on earth is able; and since you are able, you ought to. ->ORATION<-

...who ought to keep themselves immaculate from every bodily and even permitted pleasure, are later promoted to the ranks of the priesthood <term>sacerdotium; the office or status of a priest</term> after the flower of their youth has passed and they are already worn out. Let us not wonder, then, if they swear falsely and both falsely profess themselves to be instruments of the sacred scriptures, since they do not even thoroughly know what that name signifies. Indeed, I remember a certain boy admitted into the position of a bishop who, because he was utterly ignorant of letters, when asked in jest whether he had made great progress in his studies, replied naively that he had not yet learned the beginning of that oration which priests are accustomed to say by custom when about to perform divine rites. I know also those who boast that they spend—and have spent—the annual church funds entrusted to their faith on the filthiest pleasures. Will you, Leo the Tenth, tolerate these monsters? Will you both see and endure these things? Will you endure, I say, that those ignorant of letters—who are more fit for anything else than for handling sacred things—should through ignominy and insult abuse divine matters? And that wealth prepared for either maintaining or restoring temples, destined by pious men who have now departed this life for the relief of the misery of the poor, should be converted to foreign 
<margin>Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), a Church Father known for translating the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate).</note></margin> and profane uses? Jerome writes that those who abuse the property of the Church are like the scribes and the Pharisees and the priests who bought the blood of Christ the Savior with a lie. Indeed, I do not demand from all priests Dionysius’s <note>Refers to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian theologian and philosopher of the late 5th to early 6th century.</note> mystical theology and the dogmas of divine names, nor the subtleties of hypostases <term>hypostases; from the Greek 'hypostasis,' referring here to the distinct persons of the Trinity (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit) within one essence</term>, nor the notions of origins and other disputes which, having been debated in Paris <note>original: "Lutetia Parisiorum"</note>, have flowed down to us. If they would nevertheless give themselves to sacred letters and diligently apply themselves to the doctrines of the excellent leaders of our religion, they could certainly attain many advantages for living well and happily. This is not only because they would learn many things that are great in themselves and useful to them, but because through studies of this kind, it would be granted to them that they might more easily bid farewell to the allurements of the senses. For they would be occupied in the most honorable duty and warned by the studies themselves that we are born for a better life, and that this life which we live is full of the darkness of ignorance—if we wish to follow only the senses and human whims—full of sorrow, and full of calamities, if we wish to trust in human desires. We are even invited by the discomforts of this life, as if by spurs, to hasten more gladly toward that life which is to come. Who, I ask, with a slightly more vigorous mind, does not notice that in this first life humans are born more for pain than for pleasure? For in such a span of time, no one has ever drunk, or eaten, or had time for progeny...

<meta>This page continues the oration by Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola to Pope Leo X. Pico is currently contrasting the ignorance and greed of the clergy with the profound spiritual insights they could gain through the study of sacred letters and the contemplation of eternity.</meta>

# ON THE REFORM OF MORALS

...[they] could have [desired knowledge], as much as those suffering from fevers thirst, or as the impoverished and the besieged hunger, or as those accused of crimes are tormented. They could easily obtain this benefit: to bridle ambition, the mother and nurse of all evils. For indeed, all things that appear great to those looking only with bodily and human eyes, <margin>Human things seem small to spiritual eyes.</margin> seem small to spiritual eyes and to those contemplating eternal things. And this is for the best of reasons, since the mass of the earth, compared to heaven, is like a mere point; inhabited in few places, a desert of sterile sand, noxious serpents, and thirst; impassable with vast swamps, intersected by the highest mountains, and interrupted by the deepest waves. In this tiny portion of nature, the human race bustles about, with a life brief and uncertain—even if it should seem long-lasting, even very long, or fixed upon a stable foundation. For what is long-lasting for mortals? If this very thing is compared to the eternity to come and to immortal life, what is certain and stable in man? In whom all things waver; in whom—to say nothing of external and surrounding masses of troubles—the very conflict of the fighting qualities of the bodily temperament <note>This refers to the "humors" or physical elements (hot, cold, moist, dry) that Renaissance medicine believed had to be in balance for health.</note> will repeatedly become an internal war for him; and once the war is finished, destruction threatens. Add the darkness with which the human mind, left to itself, is surrounded; from which arise battles of various opinions and of desires succeeding one another, and then clearly continuous surges of fluctuating passions. Add hostile attacks, deceits, frauds, injuries. Add also the assaults and actions of higher minds <note>Pico likely refers here to the influence of celestial bodies or spiritual entities/demons on the human mind.</note>: <margin>Truly the life of man is a struggle upon the earth.</margin> so that that saying is most true: "the life of man is a struggle <note>original: "militia"; this is a reference to Job 7:1, "Is there not a warfare to man upon earth?"</note> upon the earth." It is a struggle, without a doubt, but one by which we can prepare for ourselves a victory, a crown, and a kingdom in heaven, of such a kind that we are clearly unable to grasp it in our minds or even to desire it in our prayers. For the happiness of both soul and body which is stored there for the friends of God—and preserved for the keepers of His law after the course of this life—entirely exceeds the grasp of the human mind, nor is it revealed to men unless God is the teacher. And these things would be nothing at all if compared to that happiness which the poets and philosophers—those illustrators of empty antiquity—have devised: concerning the Blessed Isles <note>original: "fortunatis insulis"; a legendary paradise in Greek mythology.</note>, the rivers of nectar, the Milky Way, the return to the companion stars, and other things which they, hallucinating about the truth, inserted into their late-night writings. These things and similar ones they <note>the clergy</note> could easily obtain from the sacred leisure of letters <note>original: "litterarum ocio"; a humanist term for the time dedicated to scholarly and theological study.</note>; but I do not demand these things from all priests, as I wished it to be indicated; yet let them not be entirely ignorant of letters, lest they bear the mark of tasteless salt or an extinguished light, and [so that they might] know...

<summary>Pico della Mirandola argues that the clergy’s worldly ambitions would vanish if they viewed human life through a spiritual lens, comparing the hardships of earthly "warfare" to the incomprehensible rewards of heaven. He contrasts the true Christian afterlife with the "hallucinations" of classical poets and philosophers, urging the priesthood to at least possess a basic education.</summary>

### ORATION
K

<margin>If only what is described here would happen!</margin>
...[that they might] know the things that pertain to their office. This I demand; this I require; and if they do not provide it, I plainly detest their conduct. Above all, I ask for innocence of life and a discipline of character—not necessarily that most exacting and superlative kind, but at least a moderate one. I do not ask that they repeatedly beat their breasts with a stone in the manner of Saint Jerome, <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420) is often depicted in art as a hermit in the desert striking his chest with a rock as an act of penance.</note> but neither should they adorn themselves with pearl necklaces like the breasts of harlots, nor decorate their sandals with Hydaspean gems. <note>original: "hydaspeis gēmis"; Hydaspean refers to the Jhelum River in India, a source of precious stones in antiquity.</note> Nor would I demand that they grow accustomed to the fasting of Hilarion, <note>Saint Hilarion (291–371) was an anchorite who practiced extreme asceticism and lived on very little food.</note> but let them not emulate or surpass the banquets of the Sybarites. <note>The inhabitants of the Greek city Sybaris were legendary for their luxury and pursuit of pleasure.</note> Even less would I insist that they roll in thorns and snow after the manner of Francis and Benedict. <note>Saint Francis of Assisi and Saint Benedict of Nursia are both famously said to have thrown themselves into briars or snow to overcome carnal temptations.</note> But when they purchase soft swan’s down with vast sums of money—money that by the highest right was dedicated either to redeeming captives or providing dowries for poor virgins—
<margin>He warns well and holily.</margin>
that I plainly condemn. I advise moderation; that excellence of virtue is easier for many to admire than it is for them to imitate. If they choose to follow that higher path, I will both praise and proclaim it. However, I do not "cut to the quick" <note>original: "rē ad uiuū reseco"; an idiom meaning to be overly harsh or exacting.</note> so much that, when I advise them to be generous and ready to give, I mean that I want them to entirely imitate that excellent and most celebrated deed of Martin cutting his cloak <note>Saint Martin of Tours famously cut his military cloak in half to share it with a beggar shivering in the cold.</note> so that a poor man, trembling with cold, might be clothed. Rather, I wish those who possess heaps of money to be warned by Martin’s example: clothes should be given to the naked, rather than covering their beasts of burden with scarlet and purple cloths. Nor, likewise, do I demand that they distribute their entire income on the costs of sacred buildings, emulating either the Sibylline shrines once built at Cumae, or the massive, towering walls of Simandius <note>original: "Simandii"; likely a reference to Osymandias, the Greek name for Pharaoh Ramses II, known for his monumental building projects.</note> and Artemisia, <note>Queen Artemisia II of Caria, who built the Mausoleum at Halicarnassus, one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World.</note> or the temples of Solomon, or even the **Julian buildings** <note>original: "Iulia ædificia"; a reference to the massive, expensive architectural projects of the previous Pope, Julius II, including the rebuilding of St. Peter's Basilica.</note> of our own time.
<margin>The Julian buildings.</margin>
Instead, I would gladly ask that they do not permit the temples to fall into ruin which were built by those very people from whom the clergy obtained such great wealth—wealth with which they are said to buy "Salmacian nights" <note>original: "Salmacidas noctes"; an allusion to the nymph Salmacis. It implies expensive nights of luxury, effeminacy, or debauchery.</note> with much gold. I would ask that they do not permit the chapels, once frequented by the ancient fathers, to become dens for porcupines, kept so filthily and neglectfully that one often sees the lairs of wild beasts and the stables of horses kept cleaner and more honorably. Meanwhile, their own bedrooms shine with gold, their floor coverings are of the most precious purple, and their table and kitchen tools glitter with engraved work.

<summary>Pico della Mirandola calls for clerical reform, arguing for a "middle way" of moderation where priests avoid both extreme asceticism and the scandalous luxury of the Renaissance papacy. He specifically critiques the neglect of ancient churches while churchmen spend vast sums on personal finery and contemporary building projects.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Pico della Mirandola's address to Pope Leo X regarding church reform. The previous page concluded with a critique of the clergy’s excessive luxury, contrasting their lifestyle with the poverty and asceticism of the saints. Here, Pico moves to the specific legal and moral issue of "pluralism"—the practice of one person holding multiple church offices—and the need for the Pope to act as a moral exemplar to convert the world.</meta>

# ON THE REFORM OF MORALS

<margin>Bernard.</margin>
You should address these disadvantages, Greatest Pontiff, <note>original: "Maxime Pontifex"; a title for the Pope.</note> through the benefit of other decrees, but especially by guarding that law which most holily provides that many priesthoods <note>original: "sacerdotia"; here referring to "benefices" or church offices that provided an income.</note> to which the care of souls is attached should not be held by a single person. That which is usually called a "dispensation" <note>original: "dispensatio"; a legal exemption from church law.</note> has brought it about that many now hold not just "many" or "several" offices, but "innumerable" ones—men who would not even deserve to perform the duty of a deacon. I am not the one who calls such a thing a "dissipation" <note>original: "dissipationem"; Pico is making a pun on "dispensation." Instead of a legal exception, it is the "wasting away" or destruction of the church's order.</note> rather than a dispensation; it was Bernard <note>Saint Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian abbot and influential reformer who famously criticized the papacy's worldly corruption in his work *De Consideratione*.</note> who called it that so many centuries ago. Oppose yourself to this insolence, Most Blessed Father, and among your other tasks, take up this distinguished duty: ensure that those who are overflowing with luxury, who are burning with ambition, and who behave wantonly with priesthoods obtained either by purchase or through such "dissipations"—those, in short, who do not observe the laws properly established—may feel the force of your justice and your fearless virtue. Thus, even if they are held by some fault of human error, they may nonetheless be freed from the enormity of such crimes. Let it be permitted for the good and the modest to live, for they are currently not even allowed to be at peace within our common peace. This is because those others snatch everything for themselves and impose the greatest servitude upon those who need their help simply to endure hunger. 

If you do not do this, Highest Prelate; if you loosen the reins for wicked men; or if you do not restrain those reins which have already been loosened for a long time, I fear that under your Pontificate our Commonwealth <note>original: "Res.P." (Res Publica); in this context, it refers to the "Christian Commonwealth" or Christendom.</note> may fall so far that it could truly be said: shame has been utterly defeated by lust, fear by audacity, and reason by madness. You may hear that a war is being waged by the enemies of our religion before you even hear it is being prepared. 

But if you do act, you will acquire both benefit for everyone and glory for yourself—not merely this momentary and fleeting glory which is confined within the narrow limits of a short time, but a stable and perpetual glory, not only on earth but in the heavenly seat. You will not only have nothing to fear from foreigners, but you may fearlessly hope that the coming enemies of our religion will be invited to the discipline of our faith more easily by good examples than by force and arms. 

<margin>Emperor Constantine.</margin>
The most illustrious defenders of our faith once judged that the world was brought back to the institutions of our piety through the Apostles more effectively by the most holy character of their lives than by the laws, battles, and triumphs of Caesar Constantine. <note>Constantine the Great (r. 306–337 AD), the first Roman emperor to convert to Christianity.</note> Indeed, they judged it was achieved by the very ministry of the Apostles, through miracles beyond the power of nature, and by their agreements. And who, I ask, would agree with us when we urge good morals if we ourselves were wicked and abusive? Who would desire to be initiated into the religion we worship, whom they see...

<meta>catchword: ...see [dere]</meta>

<summary>Pico della Mirandola urges Pope Leo X to end the corrupt practice of "pluralism" (holding multiple church offices) and warns that without moral reform, the Christian world will fall to its enemies. He argues that the holy example of the clergy is a more powerful tool for conversion than military force or imperial law.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Pico della Mirandola's appeal to Pope Leo X for internal reform. Having previously criticized the accumulation of church offices (pluralism), Pico now argues that moral restoration is a prerequisite for any external military action (like a Crusade) and calls upon the Pope to cleanse Rome of its notorious corruption, drawing parallels to Moses and Christ.</meta>

# ORATION

...[shameful] <note>The word "deret" completes "puderet" from the previous page.</note> would it not be to deny with our deeds what we profess with our mouths? Therefore, in bringing back enemies and deserters to the piety of our faith, it is better that we restore collapsed morals to the standard of ancient virtue than to do what many desire: to seek the Euxine <note>The Black Sea.</note> with a fleet, and having landed soldiers on the continent and shaken Trapezus <note>Trebizond, a city on the Black Sea coast.</note> in Colchis after finishing great battles, to carry bloodied banners into Armenia. Come then, Most Holy Father, excited by the unwritten law of nature—which was born, not written, and by a discipline revealed from heaven—and by the sanctions and decrees of your ancestors, and by both utility and glory: turn that most holy and most healthy fury against the deserters and the impious, so they might learn to act through fear of punishment, since they ought to have acted through love of virtue.

<margin>Moses, the most gentle lawgiver</margin>
That famous lawgiver Moses, though he was the most gentle of men, was nevertheless moved by terrifying anger when he recognized that God was being neglected, and he exercised severe punishment upon the deserters. Christ Jesus, God and man, the exemplar of all virtues, bore most patiently the insults directed at himself, but he did not tolerate the mark against his Father's honor when he drove the sacrilegious buyers and sellers from the temple and scattered them with a whip. And while that man [Moses] pursued the worshipers of a single golden calf with a great slaughter, and this man [Christ] willed that the saleable doves and turtledoves be ejected from the temple; will you not at least restrain with exile and the fine of all honors the worshipers of those "calves" <note>Pico is likely referring to the famous and wealthy courtesans (prostitutes) of Rome, comparing their luxury to the idolatry of the Golden Calf.</note> who not only stable themselves in the city in great numbers, but nearly rule it?

They walk adorned with gold, purple, and pearls in the royal fashion, so that at the sight of them—let alone meeting them—many are both brutalized and perish. Will you permit the sacred houses to be profaned in every way by crimes and so many monsters to grunt around them? Will you endure so many sirens—or rather, so many thousands of sirens <note>In Greek mythology, Sirens lured sailors to their deaths; here used to describe the tempting and destructive nature of Roman vice.</note>—to bring about, as much as in them lies, the shipwreck of the small boat <note>The "Navicula Petri" or the Barque of Peter, a common metaphor for the Church.</note> entrusted to you by God? Will you tolerate the trade of the temples of the Best and Greatest God and of the blood of Christ? You who attained the supreme priesthood by no commerce <note>Pico is complimenting Leo X for not buying the papacy (simony), unlike some of his predecessors.</note>: if you seem merciful, people will easily ascribe it to piety; if too mild, to your nature, habit, or the temperament of your body. But if you allow God and his law to be neglected, and monstrous crimes to be performed everywhere and with absolutely no shame: no judge of affairs will be so unjust as to fear attributing it to either malice or negligence. Wherefore, if you wish to attend diligently and weigh this whole matter about which we have discussed...

K ii

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page concludes Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola’s address to Pope Leo X. Building on the previous page's comparison of the Pope to Moses and Christ, Pico moves from the necessity of punishment to a broader program of humanist reform, including the correction of biblical texts and the stabilization of liturgy, before ending with a plea for peace among European rulers.</meta>

# ON THE REFORM OF MORALS

<warning>Foxing and ghosting from the reverse page are visible throughout the sheet.</warning>

<margin>Conclusion with an exhortation.</margin>
Most Holy Father: thus, if I am not mistaken, you will clearly ordain that the laws already duly established be guarded at least by the fear of punishment. You will apply yourself not only by your own example and the innocence of your life, but by the use of discipline against others, so that the wicked do not dare to do whatever they please with impunity. In this way, you will provide for both yourself and for everyone at once, and you will rescue the Christian Commonwealth <note>original: "Christianam rem.P." or *Res publica Christiana*, referring to the collective body of Christendom.</note>—which is now collapsing and nearly dying—from destruction. Every rank of society and the consensus of all good men demands and implores this of you. 

When these things have been acted upon by you, and once you have either tamed or extinguished the monsters of the most wicked lives so that they do not sprout up again later, the caution of laws and a somewhat more severe discipline must be applied. This should be done not only regarding clothing and expenses <note>Referring to "sumptuary laws" that regulated luxury and excess.</note>, but in studies. The sacred letters of both "instruments" <note>original: "utriusque instrumenti," a common term for the Old and New Testaments.</note> must be revised and compared with ancient and corrected copies of their first origin, so that they may be entirely purged of the errors which have crept into them through the vice of the times and the negligence of copyists. 

The solemn ceremonies—concerning which there were formerly some difficulties presented to previous councils—must be established and strengthened. Daily prayers <note>The "Divine Office" or Breviary recited by clergy.</note> must be restored to a stable and approved order, and true history must be separated from apocryphal trifles <note>Pico was critical of legendary and unverified stories of the saints often included in medieval prayer books.</note>. Duties must be prescribed to leading men, so that the wicked opinion—so destructive to our Commonwealth—that "whatever pleases is permitted" may be entirely removed and banished from the minds of the common people. Let there be peace and an end to discord among the princes themselves, so that we may finally rest from long-standing dissensions. We shall then give our efforts to those duties which elevate mortals to immortal life and to a happiness exceeding the wishes of all living things.

->THE END.<-

<summary>In this concluding passage, Pico della Mirandola urges Pope Leo X to enforce existing laws, correct the corrupted text of the Bible using original sources, and reform the liturgy to restore the "collapsing" Christian world.</summary>
<keywords>moral reform, Leo X, humanist scholarship, biblical correction, liturgy, Christian Commonwealth, peace among princes</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general conclusion of Gianfrancesco Pico’s address to Pope Leo X to a new, specific epistle addressed to the Bishop of Chieti (Teate). This new section shifts focus from moral and ecclesiastical reform to a scholarly defense of the authenticity of the writings of Dionysius the Areopagite, a cornerstone of Christian mystical theology.</meta>

# BY THE SAME GIOVANNI FRANCESCO PICO DELLA MIRANDOLA,
## Lord of [Mirandola], etc., an Epistle to the Most Reverend Bishop of Chieti,
## for the defense of the books of Dionysius the Areopagite against calumny.

When you had recently returned from Britain and the Spanish lands, and having turned aside to visit us, you were speaking with me about our shared studies, you revealed that you did not approve of the opinion of those who have written that the works <note>original: "lucubrationes," literally "studies by lamplight."</note> of Dionysius—which are titled *On the Hierarchies* and *On the Divine Names*—are not by that Dionysius who was first an Areopagite <note>A member of the Areopagus, the high court of Athens.</note> and then a disciple of the Apostle Paul. In this controversy, since you understood that I was far from agreeing with them—indeed, seeing that I was placed in the opposing camp and clearly eager to reject the objections raised against the Areopagite’s books—you began to urge me to express in writing what I had conceived in my mind. This indeed I promised I would do, and so willingly that I do not know whether I should seem to be doing a favor to you or to myself in this matter: since our desire is equal, and our cause no less so. 

For I fully agreed with you that it should be considered quite intolerable that a man so great in both letters and our religion—such that after the apostles we have no one greater—should have calumny inflicted upon him under the pretext of truth. Nor did it seem beneficial to the theological enterprise that he whom we had for so many centuries venerated as both a disciple of the Apostle and a martyr of Christ—and whom we had honored as the prince of later theologians—should now be split apart, as it were, by certain frivolous suspicions, such that we would establish two or three Dionysiuses, or rather, that we should claim those books were circulated under a false title, as if they ought to be credited to Apollinaris <note>original: "Apollinario," likely referring to Apollinaris of Laodicea, a 4th-century writer often suspected by Renaissance humanists of being the true author.</note> or perhaps to some other unknown ancient author. 

Accept, therefore, the reasons that compel me to believe—and to think others should believe—that this same Dionysius is the Areopagite, both writer and martyr. Who would not affirm that this first point is well enough established: that from the earliest antiquity, the fame of Dionysius the Areopagite was so celebrated that it has in no way come into controversy that the greatest faith ought to be placed in his writings? It is certainly established that Bishop Timothy, to whom he writes, was a disciple of the Apostle Paul; from which you can easily gather both the time and the authority of the published work from both sides; and to this add,

<summary>This page begins a scholarly defense by Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola addressed to Bishop Gian Pietro Carafa, arguing for the authenticity of the works of Dionysius the Areopagite. Pico dismisses the humanistic criticisms that these influential mystical texts were later forgeries, asserting their apostolic authority.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Pico della Mirandola’s defense of the authenticity of the writings attributed to Dionysius the Areopagite. He moves from general introductory remarks to a list of historical authorities—ranging from early martyrs to medieval Scholastic giants—who accepted these works as genuine, arguing that the weight of tradition outweighs modern skepticism.</meta>

# FOR DEFENDING AGAINST CALUMNY
<meta>catchword: of man</meta>

<margin>Polycarp the Martyr and Dionysius of Corinth.</margin>
There are also the very clear praises of the martyr Polycarp <note>Polycarp of Smyrna (c. 69–155 AD), a prominent early Christian bishop and disciple of the Apostle John.</note> and of Dionysius of Corinth <note>A 2nd-century bishop known for his letters to various Christian communities.</note> regarding the Areopagite himself. Furthermore, it cannot be called into doubt that the most holy Popes Gregory, Martin, and Agatho made honorable mention of the writings of Dionysius the Areopagite in their sermons, in councils, and in their letters. In the seventh and eighth synods <note>The Second Council of Nicaea (787) and the Fourth Council of Constantinople (869).</note> as well, testimonies were cited from these same volumes by both Greek and Latin writers. And I need not even begin from the times of Charles, surnamed the Great <note>Charlemagne (r. 768–814).</note>, who saw to it that the works of Dionysius the Areopagite were translated into the Latin language by the labor of John the Monk <note>Likely referring to the 9th-century scholar Johannes Scotus Eriugena, though Pico attributes the timing to Charlemagne's reign.</note>. Certainly, if you were to employ the testimony of the greatest monk, or the Scythopolitan <note>John of Scythopolis (6th century), the first known commentator on the Dionysian corpus.</note>, or Anastasius the Librarian <note>A 9th-century Roman scholar and translator who revised earlier Latin versions of Dionysius.</note>, and if you added both the testimonies and the interpretations of the most famous men—interpretations of the Areopagite's volumes that are elaborate and celebrated far and wide—I mean the interpretations of Hugh <note>Hugh of Saint Victor (c. 1096–1141), a mystical theologian.</note>, Albert the Great, Thomas [Aquinas], and others, who are distinguished either by the decree of our Church among the saints or by the consensus of the world in letters: then, if you were to oppose such a great and clear cloud of witnesses with two or three at most who felt differently—men otherwise of good reputation in letters—we have won, we have surely won, both in number, in authority, and in favor. 

But if you think their reasons should be examined with authority set aside, victory will likewise stand with us, although in this matter I judge that authority stands above reason. 
<margin>Much depends on history regarding who should be considered the author of a book.</margin>
For it depends very much on history as to who should be considered the author of any book; but if the reasons come under examination, they will pertain either to the title, or the style, or the subject matter. Regarding the title, no controversy would now exist. For the title itself appeals to ancient history, which is not of a double or conflicting reputation, but a single one. Moreover, it is certain and received beyond all doubt: that all the Greek and Latin manuscripts about which people are uncertain are marked with the very name of Dionysius the Areopagite. Furthermore, those who wrote either <term>Scholia<note>Brief explanatory notes or comments written in the margins of a manuscript.</note></term> or <term>Prolegomena<note>Formal introductory essays or preliminary remarks explaining the purpose and context of a work.</note></term> in Greek on the *Hierarchies*, the *Divine Names*, and *Mystical Theology*, clearly testify that Dionysius the Areopagite is the author of those works. If, however, you inspect the style, that would certainly not be a reliable judge: for even in different times, the eloquence of writers can be very similar through study and diligence, and in the same person, for many reasons, the style can be clearly different...

<summary>Pico della Mirandola argues for the authenticity of the Dionysian corpus by citing a long lineage of ecclesiastical authorities and scholars, asserting that historical tradition and manuscript evidence outweigh the stylistic criticisms of a few contemporary skeptics.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument for the authenticity of the Dionysian corpus. Pico della Mirandola is addressing the humanist critique that the style of the Greek text does not match the apostolic era. He shifts here from historical authority to the unreliability of "style" as a definitive proof of authorship.</meta>

<header>ON THE BOOKS OF DIONYSIUS</header>

...of a man <note>original: "hominis". This page begins mid-sentence, following a discussion of how an author's personal character or circumstances influence their writing.</note> is abundantly varied according to the variety of the material you treat, or the emotions of the mind, or the body, by which you are led. But if the subject matter itself comes into judgment, who, I ask—who, I say—was more prepared than Dionysius for writing about heavenly things? For he was the disciple of Paul, to whom the third heaven was opened <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 12:2, where the Apostle Paul describes a mystical vision of the "third heaven."</note>. But see how far I have gone; whereas there were arguments to be refuted from those who felt otherwise, I have brought forward arguments by which Dionysius might be proven the author of the books that have come into controversy.

But perhaps you will think this was not done by us without reason, since one of our adversaries <note>Likely referring to Lorenzo Valla (1407–1457), who famously used philological methods to argue that these works were medieval forgeries.</note> resorts to the matter of style—to which, although I would not deny that it carries great weight in distinguishing authors, I would nevertheless affirm that this weight is so uncertain that if we appeal to it as a <term>touchstone</term> <note>original: "lydium lapidem" (Lydian stone). A stone used since antiquity to test the purity of gold, here used as a metaphor for a definitive test of truth.</note>, it clearly offers us no reliable distinction. <margin>Marcus Varro, authority on the Latin language.</margin> Marcus Varro will bear witness: though he is considered a great authority on the Latin language, he still could not accurately distinguish and separate the Comedies of <term>Plautus</term> <note>Titus Maccius Plautus (c. 254–184 BC). Ancient scholars struggled to identify which of the 130 plays attributed to him were genuine; Varro identified only 21 as certainly authentic.</note> from those falsely attributed to him, having mostly been deceived by the mere similarity of style. But if some mark peculiar to the Plautine style had been fixed in his mind, the style would have cast no mist or darkness [on his judgment].

In the books of Hippocrates and Galen <note>Two of the most influential physicians of antiquity; their "corpuses" were known to contain many works by their students or later imitators.</note> as well, due to the similarity of style, there was once no small dispute in Greece, which no judgment of phrasing or property of style was able to settle. Regarding the uncertain books of Aristotle and his disciples, which antiquity was unable to distinguish by any mark of elocution or character, it is not necessary to say much: since we have discussed this very matter extensively in the fourth book of our <term>Examination</term> <note>original: "examini". Likely referring to Pico’s work *Examen vanitatis doctrinae gentium* (Examination of the Vanity of Pagan Doctrine).</note>.

Indeed, since just as the title itself belongs to history, so the force of style and phrasing can be relegated to the grammarian who used to be called a <term>Critic</term> <note>original: "Criticus". In the Renaissance sense, a scholar who evaluates the authenticity and quality of ancient texts.</note>; whose role it is to remove books from a family [of works] and admit them into it, and both to organize authors and remove them from the list. Someone will perhaps say: the heavenly and superhuman subject matter itself ought to be held as the greatest piece of evidence in this matter. If he says this: well and good, it is certainly fitting. Come then, let us now have a questioning concerning that heavenly material. Who, I ask? Who has treated divine matters properly...

<summary>Pico argues that literary style is an unreliable "touchstone" for proving authorship, citing historical examples like Varro's confusion over Plautus and disputes over Aristotle's works. He concludes that the profound "heavenly" subject matter of the Dionysian texts is a more significant indicator of their apostolic origin than their linguistic character.</summary>

<keywords>philology, authorship, Dionysius the Areopagite, Marcus Varro, Plautus, literary criticism, style, apostolic authority</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the defense of Dionysius the Areopagite’s authorship. Following the previous page’s argument that "style" is an unreliable metric for dating a text, Pico now moves to internal evidence: the author's proximity to the Apostles and the specific historical events described in the texts.</meta>

-># FOR THE DEFENSE AGAINST SLANDER <-

<margin>Pay attention to the reasons on which he relies: namely, that these were the books of Dionysius, etc.</margin>

...lacking in divine matters? <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing the thought from the previous page about who is best suited to write on the divine.</note> Who among men should be considered more knowledgeable in divine things than Paul, who, through a great and marvelous miracle, looked upon celestial and divine things? And who should be judged more rightly a sharer of Paul’s secrets than Dionysius, Paul’s own most learned and holy disciple? Indeed, in what age should the heavens have been more open and visible to man than in that time when Christ, both God and man, was seen by men and moved among them, teaching them in his own tongue and his own words? 

Now, if these books—whose author they doubt—were marked by no title at all, they rightfully could and should be attributed to someone who had seen Christ, God and man, in the flesh, or who had lived long and familiarly with those who had seen Him. But now we have them distinguished by a title, signed with the very name of that man. Mention of him in the sacred books of the Acts of the Apostles is considered celebrated and beyond controversy; and yet we still doubt, and search entirely in vain, for the author to whom they should be assigned? 

This is a greater sin because the author himself addresses these books to his fellow disciple, Timothy—the same man to whom the Apostle Paul also wrote. He mentions Paul himself as his teacher. In the course of discussing divine names, he testifies that he saw the Apostles Peter and James by name, and even sang hymns with them when they had gathered together, as he himself says in his own words, "for the vision of the life-giving and God-receiving body." <note>original: "ἐπὶ τὴν θέαν ζωαρχικῶν καὶ θεοδόχου σώματος". This refers to a famous tradition that the Apostles were miraculously gathered together to witness the Dormition (passing) and burial of the Virgin Mary.</note> He also sends a letter to the Apostle John; and as one well-versed in divine things and foreknowing the coming age, he predicts that John will be freed from his custody on Patmos and will return to the land of Asia. To his fellow disciple Titus, he clearly reveals and explains the "house of wisdom," the "mixing bowl," the "foods," and the "cup"—mysteries wrapped in mystical veils which he pulls back and explains.

<margin>Take note.</margin>

By what injustice, then, and beyond all measure of audacity (to say nothing harsher), could that man Lorenzo <note>Referring to Lorenzo Valla (1407–1457), the famous humanist scholar who used philological analysis to argue that the works of "Dionysius" were actually later forgeries from the 5th or 6th century.</note> mock the author's letter to Polycarp? <note>Polycarp (69–155 AD) was a bishop and a major figure in the early Church, said to have been a disciple of the Apostle John.</note> This letter concerns the supernatural eclipse of the sun while Christ was fixed to the cross, which Lorenzo treats as if it were a fiction forged under the name of Dionysius. Did Polycarp not live at that same time? Does the style sound different?—though we have already proven that "style" cannot be granted the title of an exact judge. Are there no witnesses cited who lived in that same season? Or the very words of Dionysius himself to Apollinaris the Sophist, whom he greatly desires to see refuted by the sight of the miracle...

<summary>Pico argues for the authenticity of the Dionysian corpus by pointing to internal references where the author claims to have interacted with Paul, Peter, James, and John. He specifically attacks Lorenzo Valla’s skepticism regarding Dionysius's account of the solar eclipse during the Crucifixion.</summary>

# A LETTER ON THE BOOKS OF DIONYSIUS
L

...he does not record the responses made to him, such as: "O noble Dionysius, these are the exchanges of divine matters." <note>original: "ω̃ καλὲ διονύσιε θείων ἀμοιβαὶ πραγμάτων"</note> Are these same things not repeated by others? Indeed, are those things that Dionysius himself answered not also added, though he passed over them in silence so they might not perhaps be turned toward his own praise? Yet they are expressed in the records of others in this manner: "Either a god suffers, or he suffers with the one who is suffering." <note>original: "ἢ θεὸν θεὸν πάσχει ἢ τῶ πάσχοντι συμπάσχει." This famous quote is attributed to Dionysius when he witnessed the supernatural eclipse during Christ's crucifixion while he was still a pagan in Egypt.</note> And also that saying: "An unknown god suffers, and because of this the whole world is shrouded in darkness and shaken." <note>original: "ἀγνωστος πάσχει θεὸς δ' ἰὸν τὸ πᾶν νεζόφωται καί σεσάλευται."</note>

What, I ask you, would <margin>Pope Nicholas the Fifth.</margin> Nicholas, the fifth Pope of that name, have said if he had known these things? I speak of that Nicholas who was angry with Theodore Gaza <note>A 15th-century Byzantine humanist and translator who was among the first to argue that the works of "Dionysius" were actually written by a later author, likely from the 5th century.</note>, a man of the same century, for declaring that Dionysius the Areopagite was not the author of the *Celestial Hierarchy*, making his conjecture based only on the style of the writing. 

If Nicholas took it poorly that Gaza judged Dionysius by his style (as he himself complains in a letter), do we think he would have endured in any way a man who utterly and entirely did away with the whole elocution of Dionysius—along with all his arrangement and invention—and hissed it away as if it were some empty, fictional invention? 

"It is likely," someone says, "that he thought those shadows of darkness did not reach as far as Athens." But it was not in Athens, but in Egypt—which is closer to Jerusalem than Athens is—that the eclipse was observed, as is clear from the letter itself and most evidently from other authors. 

Again, a skeptic will say that when the writer of the Gospel says "darkness was made over the whole earth," he meant the land of that specific region, a view with which even Saint Jerome agrees. But did he therefore exclude Egypt? Did he exclude other neighboring regions as well? If I say that the darkness of a solar eclipse arose in Mirandola <note>The author, Pico, frequently uses his home territory of Mirandola and nearby Italian cities as relatable geographic examples.</note>, will I be claiming that it did not also arise in Modena? That it was not in Ferrara? Not in Reggio? Not in Mantua? Not in Bologna? Those are all nearby and neighboring cities. 

Again, the skeptic will press: why did no author, whether Latin or Greek, remember this event? But did not the Greek Dionysius himself remember it, a man born in the midst of Athens? Does he not himself cite his adversary Apollophanes in this matter, bringing forward a witness of such a great miracle? He brings a witness so well known for his profession of the art of rhetoric that <margin>Polemon of Laodicea.</margin> Polemon of Laodicea—who was the teacher of Aristides <note>Aelius Aristides, a famous orator of the 2nd century AD.</note>—is recorded by Greek authors to have been his pupil. 

Was it necessary for everyone to have written about these things if they saw them? Is it not enough at that time to bring forward one or two witnesses in the records of literature? Those who [wrote] the Gospel...

<summary>The author defends the authenticity of Dionysius the Areopagite by citing his famous observation of the supernatural eclipse during the Crucifixion and rebuking 15th-century critics like Theodore Gaza. He argues that geographic proximity and the testimony of contemporary witnesses like Apollophanes validate the historical claims in Dionysius's letters.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the defense of the authenticity of the writings of Dionysius the Areopagite. The author is systematically dismantling the arguments of critics who suggest the "Corpus Areopagiticum" (the books on the Celestial and Ecclesiastical Hierarchies and Divine Names) was written by someone other than the biblical Dionysius mentioned in the Acts of the Apostles.</meta>

->## FOR DEFENSE AGAINST SLANDER<-

...they would subscribe to it. We have vindicated this most brilliant letter from injury, shielded by countless thousands of witnesses, yet the dispute is not yet settled. This is because they put forward another Dionysius, or perhaps an Apollinaris <note>The author is referring to theories that the works were written by Apollinaris of Laodicea or another later figure to explain why the style seems more advanced than 1st-century prose.</note>, to whom the books on the hierarchies and divine names might be attributed. They even assert that the Areopagite judges <note>The Areopagus was the high court of Athens; Dionysius was a member before his conversion by St. Paul.</note> were not actually philosophers. Who then could this Dionysius be? Was it the one from Corinth whom Jerome mentions in his *Catalog of Illustrious Men*? <note>Saint Jerome’s *De Viris Illustribus*, a famous 4th-century biographical dictionary of Christian authors.</note> But Jerome, while mentioning that man, also mentions our Areopagite, and he in no way ascribes these specific books to the Corinthian; furthermore, he enumerates the works that the Corinthian actually wrote. Moreover, the timeline does not allow for it: the Areopagite Dionysius flourished under the Emperor Tiberius and received the crown of martyrdom under Domitian—as those who write his life in Greek maintain, though other Greeks prefer the reign of Trajan. The Corinthian Dionysius, however, lived during the time of Antoninus the Second <note>The author refers to Marcus Aurelius.</note>, a period which neither the Apostle James nor Peter reached, as they achieved the palm of martyrdom under Nero. Yet, in the book *On the Divine Names*, mention is made of Peter and James as being among the living at that time.

It is even less possible, due to his age, to credit these volumes to Dionysius of Alexandria, as he was a student of Origen "the Adamantine" <note>Origen (c. 184–253 AD) was one of the most prolific early Christian theologians; "Adamantius" was his nickname, meaning "man of steel/unbreakable."</note> and passed away under the Emperor Gallienus. If, however, the judgment of Apollinaris of Hierapolis is accepted in this case, the dispute would certainly be settled in our favor, since he also flourished under Antoninus the Second and wrote the works cited by Jerome; yet among those, there is not even a word concerning these books currently under controversy. It would be even more absurd to attribute these works to Apollinaris of Laodicea; the era of the Emperor Theodosius, under whom he departed this life, clearly forbids it, as does the reputation of heresy for which we read he was both blamed and condemned. 

Dionysius the Areopagite was so far removed from such a crime of heresy that Constantine the Philosopher <note>Also known as St. Cyril, the 9th-century missionary to the Slavs, who was a great proponent of Dionysius's writings.</note> decreed that his books were sufficient to abolish every heresy. With their help, he claimed, sacred writers could achieve a swift and easy triumph over heretics—a victory they previously achieved only with difficulty and after a long time because they lacked these very manuscripts. It is recorded in Greek monuments and repeated by Anastasius <note>Anastasius Bibliothecarius (c. 810–879), a scholar and translator who helped bring Dionysius’s works to the Latin West.</note> that these books lay hidden for many centuries, having been buried <note>original: "defoſſos," literally "dug down" or "interred."</note> by heretics. Therefore, certain people should cease to wonder why Jerome and other theologians of his age made no mention of the books of Dionysius.

<summary>The author uses chronological evidence and the testimony of historical figures like Jerome and Constantine the Philosopher to prove that the "Divine Names" and "Hierarchies" could only have been written by Dionysius the Areopagite, arguing the books were hidden by heretics for centuries.</summary>
