<meta>This page continues the author's defense of Dionysius the Areopagite. In the previous section, the author argued against the theory that the works were written by a later Dionysius of Corinth or by Apollinaris, noting that the timelines and bibliographies provided by Jerome do not support such claims.</meta>

->### AN EPISTLE CONCERNING THE BOOKS OF DIONYSIUS ###<-

Nor, likewise, is it fitting for them to wonder why no mention was made of the volumes of the deeds of Hierotheus <note>Hierotheus is described in the Dionysian writings as the teacher of Dionysius the Areopagite.</note> by Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), the influential author of *Ecclesiastical History*.</note> and others in the previous age; for they had not yet been rediscovered. The same is true of Athenagoras <note>A 2nd-century Christian philosopher and apologist.</note> the Christian philosopher, whose works—and many others besides—remained hidden from Latin-speaking scholars for more than thirteen hundred years. Furthermore, there are the works of Hymenaeus <note>Mentioned in the New Testament (2 Timothy 2:17).</note> and others which Eusebius did not see, but which others saw later and committed to literary records. We ourselves have seen, and we possess, several works which are clearly very ancient, yet Eusebius did not pass judgment on them, nor does Jerome testify to having seen them.

It should be even less surprising to those critics that this same Dionysius was both a judge and a philosopher. For these roles do not conflict with one another; indeed, they are perfectly consistent. We read in the records of the Greeks that the knowledge and wisdom of Dionysius were proven by the very fact that he was among the number of the Areopagite judges <note>The Areopagus was the supreme court of Athens; membership was reserved for the city's most distinguished citizens.</note>. He would not have been enrolled among them by the learned Athenians if his own learning had not been outstanding. Add to this that laws, by which cities are governed and directed in their judgments, depend heavily upon and are often established by philosophers. That well-known and celebrated saying—that "cities would be happy when either philosophers ruled, or rulers philosophized"—is attributed to Plato of Athens. From him also came the clear principle that the leaders of cities and republics ought to be the guardians of the laws.

Dionysius himself was deeply imbued with the doctrine of the philosopher Plato. In the first flowers of his youth, he was educated in the studies of the Academy (to use the words of John the Monk <note>Likely referring to John of Scythopolis, the 6th-century commentator on Dionysius.</note>). This can be inferred from his books, especially during his treatment of the *Divine Names*, in the explanation of which he mentions the *Celestial Hierarchy*. Therefore, let them cease their efforts to deny that Dionysius is the author of both works.

I find myself wondering at one thing regarding those critics: why they wrote that Gregory <note>Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604 AD).</note>, when he cites the writings of Dionysius, does not indicate that he was the Areopagite. These are the words of Gregory in his 34th sermon (or homily) on Luke, in which he discusses the orders of angels: "It is said that Dionysius the Areopagite, an ancient and venerable father, says that from the lesser ranks of angels, messengers are sent forth to fulfill their ministry, either visibly or invisibly." <note>original: "fert̃ uero Dionysius areopagita antiquus uidelicet & uenerabilis pr̄ dicere qd̄ ex minoribus angelorū agminibɔ foras ad explēdū ministeriū uel uisibiliter uel inuisibiliter mittunt̃."</note> This is what Gregory says in that codex and in all the copies produced by the same press, in which he testifies that Dionysius the Areopagite discussed the celestial hierarchy—a fact supported by Gregory himself...

<sig>L ii</sig>

<summary>This page defends the authenticity of the Dionysian corpus by explaining why earlier historians like Eusebius might have missed certain ancient texts and by arguing that Dionysius's status as a judge in Athens was entirely consistent with his philosophical training under Platonic influence. The author also provides a direct quote from Pope Gregory the Great to refute claims that early Church Fathers did not identify the author as the biblical Areopagite.</summary>

<keywords>Dionysius the Areopagite, Eusebius, Plato, Pope Gregory the Great, Celestial Hierarchy, Divine Names, authenticity, Athens, Platonic philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the defense of Dionysius the Areopagite's authorship. The previous page argued that just because certain early historians like Eusebius or Jerome did not mention these works, it does not mean they didn't exist, as many ancient texts remained hidden for centuries before being rediscovered.</meta>

->## FOR DEFENDING THESE WORKS FROM SLANDER ##<-

<margin>On Origen.</margin> There is a very old version of Origen <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 185–253 AD), one of the most prolific early Christian theologians.</note>—if the title is not mistaken—printed in Paris and elsewhere. Certainly, it is either Origen or another early author of equal learning and eloquence who mentions Dionysius the Areopagite. While explaining the first chapter of the Gospel of John, this author cites words from the *Celestial Hierarchy* <note>The *Celestial Hierarchy* is one of the primary works attributed to Dionysius, detailing the orders of angels.</note>. He says: "The great Dionysius the Areopagite says (I am citing the Latin translation) that the being of all things is the divinity above essence." <note>original Latin: "esse omnium, est super essentia & diuinitas."</note> These words are from the fourth chapter of the *Celestial Hierarchy*, but they were not correctly translated into Latin. In the original Greek, they read: "For the being of all things is the divinity that exists beyond being." <note>original Greek: "τὸ γὰρ εἶναι πάντων ἐστὶν ἡ ὑπερ τὸ εἶναι θεότης"</note>

To those who believe that there were not as many ceremonies among the early Christians as Dionysius describes, this must be considered: we cannot know how many ceremonies they had except through evidence—specifically, from someone who committed those very ceremonies of the ancient time to writing for posterity. To this day, we know of no one who did this besides Dionysius himself. When we compare and contrast the many ceremonies we have now with those the ancients had (as recorded by Dionysius), we are indeed permitted to know them. However, whether the ancients had more or fewer ceremonies than those Dionysius numbered is by no means certain. That they did not use a minimal number of ceremonies can be gathered both from the Apostle Paul and from the decrees of our Church, since it has been handed down to memory that many dogmas of our religion were spread through succession and confirmed by the customs of those practicing them—things which later flowed down to us not through writings, but by word of mouth.

These arguments ought to have seemed sufficient to defend Dionysius the Areopagite against those who have not feared to attribute his books to others, and who mocked his letter concerning the supernatural eclipse <note>Dionysius famously claimed to have witnessed a miraculous solar eclipse in Egypt at the moment of Christ's crucifixion.</note> as if it were a forgery. For by so many reasons and witnesses, both Greek and Latin, we believe that both his name and his books are so defended from all slander that the blame can even be turned back upon those who felt otherwise. They judged without looking at the whole law, which among legal experts is considered a grave offense <note>In Roman law, a *piaculum* was a sin or an action requiring expiation.</note>. As <margin>Aristotle.</margin> Aristotle himself would not tolerate, they "looked at only a few things." <note>original Greek: "πρὸς ὀλίγα ἐπιβλέψαντες"—a reference to Aristotle's critique of those who form theories based on insufficient data.</note> But there is no shortage of other reasons why we should find it even less tolerable for those learned in Greek letters to disparage Dionysius, especially since they could have learned the truth "from the threshold," as the saying goes, even in the collections of Suidas <note>The *Suda* (or Suidas) is a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world.</note>...

<meta>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page with the rest of the reference to the Suda encyclopedia.</meta>

<summary>The author cites Origen and early church traditions to prove the antiquity of Dionysius's writings, arguing that his detailed descriptions of ceremonies and supernatural events are legitimate records of the early Church rather than later forgeries.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument for the authenticity of the works of Dionysius the Areopagite. The author is refuting the theory that these texts were actually written by a later figure, such as Apollinaris, and is now citing historical and contemporary witnesses to support Dionysius.</meta>

->### EPISTLE ON THE BOOKS OF DIONYSIUS ###<-

...it is easily demonstrated that Dionysius the Areopagite <note>Dionysius was a judge of the Areopagus in Athens who was converted by St. Paul; traditionally, he was considered the first Bishop of Athens.</note> was the author of those books titled *The Hierarchies*, *On Mystical Theology*, and *On the Divine Names*. It is far from the truth that these books could be attributed to some other Dionysius (not the Areopagite) or to Apollinaris <note>Apollinaris of Laodicea (c. 310–390 AD), a bishop whose views on the nature of Christ were declared heretical.</note>. In fact, since twelve works are cited there as belonging to Dionysius, the Areopagite is established as the primary author. His literary works are recorded, and what he recounted in his letter regarding the solar eclipse <note>Dionysius famously claimed to have witnessed a miraculous solar eclipse in Egypt at the moment of Christ's crucifixion.</note> is highly celebrated.

Furthermore, the books of Apollinaris are not only numbered separately in those same records, but his father of the same name is also cited. The time in which he lived is clearly narrated as being long after the time of Dionysius. His heretical dogma is both cited and refuted, appearing both in the Greek collections and in the writings of the Greek historian Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus (c. 380–439 AD), a Christian church historian.</note>, who recounts with great diligence the occasion and reason why he devised that wicked doctrine. It is also found in the works of Theodoret <note>Theodoret of Cyrus (c. 393–458 AD), a highly influential theologian and bishop.</note>, a truly distinguished and most eloquent theologian famous for his Greek history. In his history, the dogma of Apollinaris is both reported and rejected, and it is explained through written records how he was condemned by the most holy Pope Damasus <note>Pope Damasus I (reigned 366–384 AD), known for his efforts to combat heresy.</note>.

<margin>Pope Damasus</margin>
Certainly, that dogma is as different from the teaching of Dionysius as light is from darkness, or truth from a lie. Dionysius himself taught the truth sincerely and confirmed it with his blood through martyrdom. Following the footsteps of his doctrine were the ancient fathers and—aside from a very few—even those who have flourished in letters in our own times. Among these was my uncle, Giovanni Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), the famous Renaissance philosopher.</note>, as I believe you have easily learned both from his own works and from my defense of his book *On the One and Being* <note>original title: "De uno et ente," a work attempting to reconcile Plato and Aristotle.</note>. I wished to direct these writings of mine to him, so that I might do something pleasing for you and useful for others; for you do not need these things yourself, being a learned man. Indeed, you have been affected by the same distress as I—that our contemporaries have possessed such audacity that they would not even spare a most excellent theologian and martyr.

If his most blessed soul, joined again to his body, were to visit the world, we imagine he might address those very people in this manner (though perhaps in a far better way): "Is it thus that I find you mortals grateful for my labors and hardships? Is it thus you are favorable to the doctrines I committed to paper? Is it thus you speak well of my blood which I shed, and my head which the impious sword took away? Are you not ashamed to appear to compete in bad faith alongside my killers before the enemies of our religion? To appear..."

<sig>f</sig>

# FOR DEFENDING AGAINST SLANDER #

...to surpass these very people in impiety? For the nation that once impiously sought my death <note>Dionysius refers to the pagans of Athens who initially opposed the Christian message.</note> is now remarkably pious: it venerates both my mortal remains and my name, and pours out constant prayers to me. Yet you have attempted to slaughter me a second time, and indeed more savagely. For you have brought destruction—as much as lies within your power—not now to the death of the body (which is made blessed by that very act), but to the memory of my Doctrine and my deeds. It is through these that I live on in the immortal minds of men, and by them the very path that leads to heaven is revealed. 

You have stripped the doctrine from the martyr, and the martyr from the doctrine. The title of the Areopagus <note>The Areopagus was the high court of Athens; Dionysius’s status as a judge there lent great intellectual authority to his conversion and writings.</note>, by which I was once famous among the Athenians—and among the followers of our faith, even more famous for having held that status in contempt—you have struck down, along with both the doctrine and the martyr. While the ignorant and envious enemies of the Christian faith once sought to bury my literary monuments <note>In this context, "monuments" refers to his written works, seen as lasting testimonies of faith.</note>, you, who are followers of that same faith, erase my name from these unearthed monuments which are famous throughout the entire world. 

By the happiness which I now possess, and which mortals expect to gain, I would plead with those who do not cease to harass me (though I have deserved well of everyone): they do no harm to me, but only trouble themselves. I wish they would instead turn their studies toward attacking the enemies of the Christian name, and toward spreading far and wide the faith itself—which I proved with my writings and confirmed with my blood. Let them not only spare me, but take care to become eternally blessed by following in the footsteps of myself and those like me. 

At Mirandola <note>A city in northern Italy, home to the famous Pico della Mirandola family.</note>, after your departure, as soon as I obtained enough leisure to satisfy your wish in part.

->FINIS.<-

<summary>This concluding section of the treatise features a dramatic appeal by Dionysius the Areopagite, who "speaks" to defend his legacy and writings from modern critics, urging them to focus on defending the faith rather than questioning his authorship.</summary>

<meta>This page marks a transition from the prose defense of Dionysius the Areopagite to a poetic work by the same author, Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola. While the previous page dealt with historical and theological authenticity, this section shifts to a moral and philosophical exhortation, using classical imagery to wake the reader from spiritual lethargy.</meta>

### An Awakening by the same Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola, Lord and Count of Concordia, from the wretched sleep of a dying life to the perpetual wakefulness of a blessed life.

<margin>
He addresses the human race like a man who is half-asleep.
</margin>
O mortal race, will it never be granted for you to be shaken from this sluggish sleep? Why do you open your eyes now, only to close them again? Perhaps you lie stunned and heavy by the murmurs of the Lethean shore? <note>original: "lætheæ ad murmura ripæ." In Greek mythology, Lethe was the river of forgetfulness in the underworld. Pico uses it here as a metaphor for spiritual ignorance.</note>

<margin>
He alludes to the river Lethe and to the "murmur" celebrated by Ovid, using it as an irony for the river of forgetfulness that shares its name. <note>Ovid was a famous Roman poet; Pico is likely referencing Ovid's descriptions of the House of Sleep in the "Metamorphoses."</note>
</margin>
For you see glimmering figures through a varied void, appearing different from how they truly are. You do not grasp true joys; instead, a greater image, simulating reality, deludes your senses with a vain and false desire.

<margin>
For the allurements of the senses are greater than the truth, and their joys are simulated, not sincere.
</margin>
Do you turn your face away? And raise your dimming eyes with a slow motion, shunning the heavens. Let it be permitted—let it finally be permitted for the eyes, once blocked by shadows and a malignant cloud, to be cleared and turned toward the celestial regions: to the heights of Olympus. <note>Pico uses "Olympus," the home of the Greek gods, as a classical synonym for the Christian Heaven.</note>

<margin>
In the manner of one who, having been woken, wishes to go back to sleep.
</margin>
It is right for us to ascend to that place where we are granted to go, on the wings of virtues, after the seasons of this miserable life. And to reach the eternal goal in a brief race. There lies the perpetual kingdom and tranquil pleasure, filled with every kind of good, and entirely free from evils, perpetually fortunate and blessed without end.

<margin>
He briefly indicates happiness, about which he writes more extensively at the end.
</margin>
This life in which you enjoy this sleep is brief, full of great evils and lust, mournful and more fleeting than the swift east wind. What you desire and what you fear is always in flux: illustrious kingdoms, riches, beauty, and the honors of human praise pass away as quickly as the wave of a rapid river.

<margin>
He explains what was said generally before: according to that saying of the Apostle, "the figure of this world is passing away." <note>This refers to St. Paul's statement in 1 Corinthians 7:31.</note>
</margin>
And a huge sea of cares boils around you. Just as when smoke wandering about rages against bleary eyes and then turns toward the upper regions, so do cares afflict the soul. Desire itself rages in its seeking, and, trembling, it holds on, always gripped by a wretched fear lest it be robbed of the thing it loves.

Therefore, why do you pursue the dreams of things in vain—things which you will never find, and which, once found, immediately slip from your hands into the thin air like smoke? Why are you gripped by such dread for what you love, lest it... <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>This page contains a Latin poem by Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola that exhorts humanity to wake up from the "sleep" of worldly attachments and seek eternal life. He uses the classical metaphor of the river Lethe to describe the state of spiritual forgetfulness and warns that earthly honors are fleeting.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola, spiritual awakening, Lethe, vanity of the world, virtue, celestial life, Ovid, St. Paul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Pico della Mirandola's poetic exhortation. Having previously warned the reader against the "sleep" of spiritual apathy, he now uses historical examples of fallen greatness—specifically Pompey, Caesar, and Ludovico Sforza—to illustrate the radical instability of worldly power and the fickle nature of Fortune.</meta>

# AWAKENING FROM SLEEP

<margin>Since that which has passed is not restored to men.</margin>
You fear in vain that the past might be brought back. For it has already fled before 
you could grasp it, flying behind your back into nothingness with a swift flight. 
Nothing on earth is stable. The ancient poets in their custom did not imagine 
**Proteus** <note>In Greek mythology, Proteus was a sea god capable of changing his shape at will. Pico uses him as a symbol for the ever-changing nature of the world.</note> changing into so many shapes 
as the various faces which human affairs turn toward us. 
Hence, those things which now seem bright with a visible face 
are soon hidden in darkness; and those which now smile with favorable 
success, suddenly mourn with a changed and ghastly countenance, 
hurled headlong from their peak of prosperity.

<margin>Not only did Pompey’s triumphs fail to secure his happiness, but they made his calamity more notable; a point noted by Aristotle in his *Rhetoric* in a general sense. <note>original: "Aristotele in rhetoricis." Pico references Aristotle’s observation that a fall from a great height is more tragic and noteworthy than a common misfortune.</note></margin>
The "pirate laurel" <note>original: "piratica laurea." This refers to Pompey the Great’s famous victory over the Cilician pirates in 67 BCE, which cleared the Mediterranean and brought him immense glory.</note> of the wretched **Pompey the Great** 
and his many blood-stained crowns won from defeated enemies 
could not save his head when it was torn from his neck— 
a head once revered by the Roman people and the August Senate. 
The more that swift Victory adorned him with her bloody wings, 
the more that most wretched slaughter, carried out by a 
servant’s hand, shone forth as a spectacle. 
A provident mind saved his father-in-law <note>Julius Caesar, who was Pompey’s father-in-law through his daughter Julia. Pico highlights the tragic irony that Caesar was "saved" from many wars only to fall later.</note> through countless wars 
only so that, having possessed the affairs of Europe and Asia 
far and wide, and distinguished by his triumph in Libya, 
victorious over his own son-in-law and master of Rome as 
**Dictator**, he might fall in his own fatherland—a more conspicuous

<margin>In that way, the calamity was more notable because it was adorned with many crowns; for it is known that sacrificial victims were often crowned. <note>Pico draws a dark parallel between a general’s triumphal crowns and the garlands placed on an animal before it is sacrificed.</note></margin>
Victim: he who was once bound by so many and such great crowns. 
When those laurel-bearing chariots drew them to the high Capitoline Hill 
amid the joyful applause of the people and the fathers, 
did they not seem to warn, as if with a silent mouth, of this? 
"From this height, one may learn by how much more grievous a fall 
men are ruined, and how those things which will cause harm 
are sought after with great effort." 
Even him whom we saw in our own time, <note>Pico moves from ancient history to contemporary events he witnessed personally.</note> who possessed 
the scepter of the **Insubres** <note>term: <term>Insubres</term> (The ancient Celtic people of the Milan region; Pico uses this to refer to the Duchy of Milan).</note> through countless arts, 
wealth, and much labor—at last, a harsh cage 
enclosed him. Here, bound for three times three years, <note>original: "triplici trieteride." This refers to Ludovico "Il Moro" Sforza, Duke of Milan, who was captured by the French in 1500 and died in captivity in the castle of Loches.</note> 
worn out by old age and cares, he left behind mortal life. 
And a short time later, we saw another raised high 
with lofty titles: whom Rome surrounded in triumph, 
whom the sea that is above [the Adriatic] and the sea that washes below [the Tyrrhenian] feared. 
A mockery of human fate, he was three times bound in chains; 
standing here, finally free at the third attempt, 
he was slaughtered as a victim in a **Pyrenean valley**. <note>This likely refers to Cesare Borgia, who rose to incredible power in Italy, was imprisoned multiple times, and finally died in a skirmish at Viana, in the foothills of the Pyrenees, in 1507.</note>

<meta>catchword: Mente (Mind)</meta>

<summary>This page uses the dramatic falls of Pompey, Julius Caesar, Ludovico Sforza, and Cesare Borgia to argue that worldly success is a deceptive prelude to tragedy. Pico suggests that the higher a person rises in power, the more visible and sacrificial their eventual "awakening" to misfortune becomes.</summary>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of fallen greatness begun on the previous page. Having discussed the personal downfall of leaders like Pompey and Caesar, the author now expands his scope to the ruin of entire civilizations and famous cities, using them as a "memento mori" (reminder of death) for the reader.</meta>

# THE LIFE THAT DIES

<margin>Caesar Borgia <note>Caesar Borgia (1475–1507) was a notorious Italian prince whose rapid rise and even faster fall from power served as a contemporary moral lesson for Pico’s readers.</note></margin>
I recall in my mind, remembering how I have seen many, 
Placed atop **crumbling masses** <note>original: "caducis molibus." This refers to the unstable foundations of worldly power.</note>, fall in a great ruin. 
It is known that these things also happen to cities, 
Whose severed corpses lie prostrate everywhere 
<margin>Those who have been in Assyria report this: that the area near Babylon is uninhabited because of the multitude of serpents.</margin>
In deserted fields. Renowned **Babylon** has fallen, 
Which now lies as a bed spread for serpents; 
Once the famous queen of the Assyrian soil, 
Fertile with so many triumphs. 
She lies in the land of Egypt, buried by the plow, 
<margin>Egyptian Thebes.</margin>
She who once stood most celebrated for her **hundred gates** <note>Thebes in Egypt was famously described by Homer as having a hundred gates, symbolizing its immense size and power.</note>. 
<margin>Those who saw them report that they are now meager villages.</margin>
Now **Lacedaemon** <note>Another name for Sparta.</note> is a thin village: once famous 
For laws and arms; and **Athens** is now a vile village, 
<margin>These histories are very well known; they are found in many authors, but especially in Plutarch and Probus.</margin>
Once distinguished for wisdom and the defeats of the Persians, 
Whom first the land, and then the sea, had driven out far and wide. 
Now deserted **Paros**, known for its white stone <note>Parian marble was the most prized building and sculpting material in antiquity.</note>, 
Was the conqueror of great **Miltiades** <note>Miltiades was the hero of the Battle of Marathon, but he died in disgrace after failing to capture the island of Paros.</note>, who once 
Conquered the treacherous enemy shut in the narrow field of **Marathon**. 
<margin>Above were examples from Asia and Europe: now an example from Africa.</margin>
Now wild **Carthage**, bristling with forest thickets 
And inhabited by a few tiny huts, hopes 
That no wild beast of the Libyan land tears her apart. 
<margin>Alexander ruled in all these parts.</margin>
And she [Pella] which once ruled over the Median **Hydaspes** <note>The Hydaspes is a river in modern-day Pakistan; it marked the eastern limit of Alexander the Great's conquests. Pella was the capital of his Macedonian kingdom.</note> 
Is now pressed by the hard use of the curved plowshare. 
<margin>An argument for future ruin based on the past and the publicly witnessed ruin of Rome.</margin>
Just like these, those celebrated cities which are seen now, 
Prone to all softness and luxury, whose walls 
Purple covers, and whom tapestries woven with proud gold 
Adorn—if you did not think they were perhaps destined to be flattened: 
Let the fragments of **Roman ruin** now warn you. 
<margin>Epilogue: a summons based on this collection of misfortunes.</margin>
Therefore, why do you waste away like a sick man in soft feathers, 
Perpetually blinking? Why, in the manner of a beast, 
Do you turn your back to Heaven? And, skillfully 
Indulging your belly in all things, do you devote yourself 
To wealth and seek honors? 
<margin>Present calamities expressed briefly.</margin>
Which, even if you are unwilling, **pale Death** will destroy 
In a certain course, like a violent whirlwind, 
Snatching away all things in which we exultantly take delight. 
Have not the wars shrieking too much around you, 
With much blood already shed, or the swelling, wicked **plague** 
And **famine**, warned mortals to proceed with a swift course 
Toward death? Of which there is no doubt it will be present 
Even if other things are lacking, while stooped **Old Age** hastens on.

M

<summary>The author lists famous fallen cities—Babylon, Thebes, Sparta, Athens, and Carthage—as proof that all earthly power eventually decays. He concludes by urging the reader to abandon luxury and prepare for inevitable death, especially given the contemporary threats of war, plague, and famine.</summary>
<keywords>transience of power, ruins of Rome, Miltiades, Alexander the Great, memento mori, fall of empires, plague and famine</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the ruin of physical cities to the decay of the human body and the loss of intellectual works. It continues the "Memento Mori" theme by showing that neither physical beauty nor literary fame can withstand time.</meta>

-> # AWAKENING FROM SLEEP <-

<meta>catchword: But it helps</meta>

<margin>When strengths recede—which physicians call by the single name of "vitality" <note>original: "uirtutis"</note>—death follows. Brief remedies for individual diseases of the soul.</margin>
Mourning, full of evils, and leaning on a trembling staff,
When wrinkles furrow the body and strengths recede—
If wealth is dear to your heart, then let the Lydian treasure
Of **Croesus** <note>Croesus was the King of Lydia, legendary for his vast wealth until his defeat by the Persians.</note> come to mind, seized in a bloody war.
If the systematic conquest of nations delights you,
And a scepter sought for long with much blood,
Let **Cyrus** <note>Cyrus the Great, founder of the Persian Empire; his violent death was often cited as a lesson on the limits of ambition.</note>, thirsting for blood through wars until he was exhausted,
Teach you by example to live content with little.

<margin>An allusion to that line of **Ovid**: "She also weeps as she looks in the mirror."</margin>
If beauty captures you, imagine you see the aged
**Helen** <note>Tindaris refers to Helen of Troy, daughter of Tyndareus.</note>, marvelling at her flaccid neck in the mirror,
And her teeth rough with rusty decay,
And her excessively drooping cheeks, noticing her wrinkles.

<margin>He notes a small work by the author on **Expelling Venus** <note>This likely refers to the author Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola's own poem "De expellenda Venere."</note>, and its first verse is cited.</margin>
And those things I once sang when it pleased me to expel from Rome
The **Idalian fires** <note>Fires of Venus, who was worshipped at Idalium on Cyprus.</note> and the weapons of blind Cupid;
Let them return to your mind and heal a sick love.

<margin>Likewise, four books on love dedicated to **Leo X**, Supreme Pontiff.</margin>
And those things which, freed from the law of poetic meter,
Cast far away vain madnesses in twice two books.

<margin>Causes of the corruption of books: for they happen mostly by fire; sometimes by water, as in the history of Hydaspes; sometimes in subterranean caverns, as in **Strabo** concerning the books of **Aristotle** and **Theophrastus**; sometimes by the plundering of cities, as in the time of the **Goths**.</margin>
If you burn with the desire for study to give your name
To the ages through learning: ask how easily these monuments perish.
Nowhere are the celebrated discoveries of **Varro** <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BC), considered Rome's greatest scholar; the vast majority of his 400+ books are lost.</note> found throughout the world.
Who, although he embraced the heavens, the lands, and every
Study of war and peace, yet from them there barely survive
Even broken elements of the Latin tongue
And his work on the cultivation of a small plot of soil <note>Refers to Varro’s surviving work "On Agriculture" (De Re Rustica).</note>; fire or water 
Consumed the better parts, or a dark cavern swallowed them,
Or war snatched them away and the victor's wrath destroyed them.
Nor the mourning for his daughter, with which he consoled himself as a lover.
Not the outstanding deeds of Rome once performed during his consulship.
Not the praises of wisdom, nor the deeds of **Cato of the Porcian line**.
The monuments of the Greek or Latin Muses <unclear>do not survive.</unclear>
And as for the once numerous volumes of **Livy**, with few
Exceptions: decaying antiquity has long since eaten them away.
Add how many books a small fire burned by the Nile <note>A reference to the destruction of the Great Library of Alexandria in Egypt.</note>.
How many books most learned Greece might count: of which,
I ask you, what is known to us but an empty name?
But imagine that the monuments of learned labor did endure;
Once you have died, how does renowned fame help you?

<summary>This page reflects on the vanity of physical beauty, wealth, and intellectual legacy, citing the loss of works by Varro and Livy as proof that even fame is mortal. It encourages the reader to seek spiritual rather than worldly contentment.</summary>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Memento Mori" (Remember Death) theme, shifting from the decay of the body and fame to the theological and philosophical conclusion: the final judgment, the resurrection of souls, and the transitory nature of time itself. It provides a bridge between classical philosophy (Stoicism, Epicureanism, Plato) and Christian doctrine.</meta>

# OF THE DYING LIFE

XI ->M ii<-

<margin>The fire of judgment, toward which either those "hallucinating" <note>The author suggests pagans might have glimpsed the truth through intuition or "hallucination."</note> or those drawing knowledge from the Old Testament—such as the Greek Stoics, both Senecas, the Epicurean Lucretius, and others—have looked.</margin>
But suppose it were helpful and nothing was a lie: therefore,
What will others say who have already completed the duty of life?
A time will come when the fields will provide nourishment 
For no living things; both humans and beasts will fall.
Only humans will rise from their tombs: then a swift fire
With little effort will devastate all that has been written.
<margin>Rewards of the righteous.</margin>
And the very ranks of the pious shall ascend to the 
Heavenly seats, to live eternally with the Prince of things <note>God.</note>: and
<margin>Punishment of the damned.</margin>
The wicked crowd will be plunged into the Tartarean deep <note>Tartarus; a classical name for the underworld or Hell.</note>.
<margin>Mention of Tartarus is very ancient, repeated from Homer through Plato in the *Phaedo* and the *Republic* <note>original: "R.P." for *Res Publica*</note>; and called by the same name in the sacred writings of the New Testament.</margin>
Noticing these things, you will be shaken from your deep sleep,
Whether you were sad or joyful; and you will see 
Yourself to be like a man who, while snoring lying back 
In deep feathers, is deceived by a false image:
Whether he rejoices while clutching gold in a great chest 
With his hands, but a little later, shaken from his greedy sleep, 
He is saddened, re-evaluating the vanishing joys within himself;
Or if he thinks he is being tormented in agony 
During the sleepy night, when there is then an exit from 
That sluggish rest, he rejoices in triumph; and with a watchful mind 
He reflects that his losses have fled through the breezes 
Faster than the North Wind.
And thus you will weigh the thousand joys or thousand troubles 
Of this present life as being of such little value, that you will
Believe they once fled just like a winged dream.
<margin>Summary of the entire consideration.</margin>
From here you will finally lead your mind above the cloud 
Of black darkness: which, having gained a clear seat, 
Will look down from a lofty watchtower, [seeing] how much firmer 
Things stand over perishable ones, how much heaven stands over earth, 
How much glory over punishment.
And no differently than a rapid river, he will watch mortal things 
Flow out into death, and all the human crowd 
Trusting in vain things: 
And like a tender flower, which the spring breeze of Favonius <note>The West Wind.</note>
Brought forth, and which fell before it could give out its scent.
<margin>We possess nothing in a moment of time, as is proven by Aristotle and others.</margin>
We live—that is but a point; for what was before has receded,
And what follows is not yet here: place the future in succession.
<margin>From the Apostle Paul saying, "In him we live, move, and have our being."</margin>
Indeed, even this very fact that we live and move 
And are: God imparts it, who created all things 
From nothing, always unmoved, who governs all things.
Who willed the Begotten <note>Jesus Christ.</note> to descend from the highest heaven.

<summary>This page concludes the poem by comparing the present life to a dream from which one awakens at death, arguing that the only reality is the eternal judgment and God's sustaining power. It synthesizes classical views on the end of the world with Christian teachings on the resurrection and the transience of time.</summary>
<keywords>Last Judgment, resurrection, Tartarus, Plato, Stoicism, transience of life, Saint Paul, Aristotle, Memento Mori</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Excitatio a Somno" (Waking from Sleep) theme. While the previous page dealt with the terrifying aspect of the Last Judgment and the destruction of the material world, this page focuses on the "Awakening" of the faithful: the incarnation of Christ, the resurrection of the body, and the specific supernatural qualities (the "Four Gifts") that a glorified body was believed to possess in Heaven according to medieval and Renaissance theology.</meta>

# THE AWAKENING FROM SLEEP

<margin>
The reason why God was made man <note>original: "Deus homo factus." This refers to the doctrine of the Incarnation.</note>.
</margin>
To take up flesh from the Virgin’s sacred womb,
And through the cross to erase the sins of the first offense <note>Original Sin, committed by Adam and Eve.</note>.
The Almighty, kindled by wondrous love, 
Bestowed this upon the human race, to lead them away from the underworld <note>original: "orco" (Orcus), a classical name for the realm of the dead.</note>.

<margin>
The soul’s happiness is achieved through the intellect and the will; desire is always fulfilled according to that sacred word: "delights at your right hand forevermore" <note>A reference to Psalm 16:11.</note>.
</margin>
So that He might reopen the gates of Olympus <note>A classical metaphor for Heaven.</note> closed by sin,
Where one might now enjoy happiness; and then be able 
To grasp rewards greater than any prayer could wish for,
When the mind itself, joined deep within to the Supreme Good,
Will cling to the joyful sight and cling with love,
Always desiring and always enjoying, blessed without end.

<margin>
The gifts of the glorified body <note>In theology, these are the four supernatural qualities of a resurrected body: Radiance, Agility, Subtlety, and Impassibility.</note>.
</margin>
And so, the body is reborn so happy that it is carried
With Him into the high air, into the lofty halls of the great Thunderer <note>original: "tonantis." A classical epithet for Jupiter, used here to refer to God the Father.</note>,
Drenched in the immense light of a flashing glow.

<margin>
Radiance <note>original: "Claritas." The gift of shining with divine light.</note>.
</margin>
So that it surpasses the sun’s rays, from the sun’s rising
Unto the sun’s setting, and suddenly to the Great Bear <note>original: "septemtrione," referring to the seven stars of the northern constellation.</note>

<margin>
Agility <note>original: "Agilitas." The gift of moving through space at the speed of thought.</note>.
</margin>
And the Arctic pole, it is suddenly carried back to the south.

<margin>
Subtlety <note>original: "Subtilitas." The gift of passing through material objects, like Christ passing through the tomb or closed doors.</note>.
</margin>
It moves with such ease, and shines with such clear light.
Indeed, it permeates all bodies at the whim 
Of the happy soul, so that no mass can slow it down.

<margin>
How the body is impassible <note>original: "impatile" (impassibilis). The gift of being immune to pain, injury, or decay.</note>.
</margin>
So strong and powerful, that not even the great force of the 
Three-pronged lightning bolt <note>The traditional weapon of Jupiter/God.</note> can affect it, let alone earthly things weary it.

<margin>
What was promised in baptism and the sacrament of Penance: according to that sacred word...
</margin>
These beauties, these gifts, these happy rewards of the mind
Which cherished the Highest; it worshipped the Highest Parent
On earth, and compelled the limbs joined to it 
To be driven by the command of reason, and to keep the venerable things
Promised at the waves of the sacred font of rebirth <note>The waters of Baptism.</note>
Which we sanctify: or if any plague from the foul abyss 
Of Cocytus <note>A river in the Greek underworld, used here to represent the "sludge" of sin or Hell.</note> should attack, the soul taught how to dispel it with abundant
Tears <note>original: "vbertim lachrymis." The "tears of contrition" in the sacrament of Penance.</note>, and to beat the panting breast, and
To bow the proud head to the hands of the priest.

<margin>
Death shall be no more, and that saying: "Death is swallowed up in victory."
</margin>
Thence, the gate of high heaven being unlocked, the hinge turned,
It leads the watchful to the high thrones of the immense Thunderer;
Once death is undergone and sleep is chased far and wide,
To the tenth inner chamber of the Highest Parent.

<margin>
Eternal life enclosed by no limit.
</margin>
Thus, O thus it pleased the Triune King to decree;
Thus, O thus will it be permitted to enter the furthest heaven,
And to climb the ethereal mass of the celestial seat,
And to live a life now enclosed by no boundary.

<margin>
Allusion to ancient philosophers and poets: there are those who thought the Elysian Fields were the Fortunate Isles <note>The author bridges classical mythology with Christian theology, suggesting that the "Elysian Fields" of the Greeks were an early, imperfect understanding of Heaven.</note>.
</margin>

<summary>This page describes the rewards of the virtuous soul after the Resurrection, specifically detailing the "Four Gifts" of the glorified body—radiance, agility, subtlety, and impassibility. It blends classical imagery (Olympus, the Thunderer, Cocytus) with Christian doctrine on baptism and penance to describe the transition into eternal life.</summary>
<keywords>Resurrection, Glorified Body, Four Gifts, Incarnation, Baptism, Penance, Eternal Life, Heaven, Classical Mythology in Christianity</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page concludes the poem "Moribundae Vitae" (The Dying Life). While the previous page described the physical resurrection and the perfection of the glorified body, this final section contrasts the mythological afterlives described by ancient poets with the true Christian promise of Heaven. It ends with a formal colophon providing publication details from 16th-century Bologna.</meta>

<margin>
There are some who have placed the Elysian Fields <note>In Greek mythology, the final resting place for the souls of the heroic and the virtuous.</note> as being situated beneath the earth. 
Certain idolaters placed them in the Galaxy <note>The Milky Way.</note>; the poet Lucan <note>Marcus Annaeus Lucanus (39–65 AD), a Roman poet who described the soul rising to the stars in his work "Pharsalia."</note> touches upon this very opinion.
The Table <note>original: "Mesa." Likely a reference to the "table" or banquet of the Lord in the Kingdom of Heaven.</note> of which Christ speaks in the Gospel. 
According to that verse: "You shall give them to drink from the torrent of your pleasure" <note>A reference to Psalm 36:8.</note>.
The Apostle <note>Saint Paul.</note> says: "What eye has not seen, nor ear heard, nor has it entered the heart of man" <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 2:9, describing the wonders of Heaven.</note>.
</margin>

# OF THE DYING LIFE

Eternal life: to be fed through all ages with prayers fulfilled,
With happy ambrosia and happy nectar <note>The poet uses the classical terms for the food and drink of the gods to metaphorically describe the sweetness of divine grace.</note>.
Not those things on which the deluded ancient poets fed,
Who speak of souls thrust back into the unfortunate earth,
Which is beaten by the great tide of the ocean,
Set apart as the Elysian Fields; or those souls shut
Within the high limit of pure air, where the Milky Way
Shines: a place once made very famous by lying song.
But rather those things which the eternal offspring <note>Jesus Christ.</note> of the eternal Father
Promised: to satisfy the pious above the heavens, above 
The glowing table of the tenth kingdom <note>original: "decimi mensam... regni." This refers to the Empyrean, the highest heaven in medieval cosmology, located beyond the nine moving spheres.</note>, as final gifts.
Where perfect youth remains in ethereal flower,
Where pleasure flows forever from a steady torrent.
A pleasure such as no eyes have been permitted to drink in, nor any 
Ear to catch, nor to be held deep within any heart.
For whatever mortals addicted to the senses once thought 
Happy and auspicious, it is right to despise 
As miserable, if it be compared to such great honor.
Since this one glory already surpasses the comprehension of men:
That the radiant stars of heaven shall be beneath their feet.
Those stars which, like starry curtains with shimmering
Light, now shine stretched out before our eyes,
So that, as they observe souls bent toward the earth and observe 
Dying prayers—souls that have forgotten their Father and their 
Heavenly homeland—they might invite them to eternal joys by their perpetual light.

->T H E   E N D.<-

->R E G I S T E R <note>A "Registrum" or Register was a guide for the bookbinder, listing the first words or letter-marks (signatures) of each gathered section of pages to ensure they were bound in the correct order.</note><-

->A ii B ii C ii D ii E ii F ii G ii H ii I ii<-
->K ii L ii M ii<-

->Printed at Bologna by Girolamo di Benedetti of Bologna.<-
->In the year of our Lord 1523, in the month of May, during the reign of Pope Adrian VI.<-

<summary>This concluding page of the poem contrasts the "lying songs" of pagan poets regarding the Elysian Fields with the Christian promise of an eternal banquet in the Empyrean heaven. The text concludes with a formal 1523 printing colophon from Bologna.</summary>
<keywords>Elysian Fields, Lucan, Milky Way, Empyrean, Tenth Kingdom, Girolamo di Benedetti, Bologna, Pope Adrian VI, afterlife, celestial banquet</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the "Errata" or correction list for the preceding volume. It addresses typographical errors and textual slips found in the three books "On Witches" (De Strigibus), an oration, a letter, and a poem. Based on the previous page's mention of 16th-century Bologna, this likely refers to Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola's work "Strix" (The Witch), published around 1523.</meta>

# ERRATA

<margin>In the first book, *On Witches* <note>original: "De Strigibus." A reference to screech-owls or, more commonly in this era, the "strix" or witch who was believed to fly at night.</note></margin>
On the first leaf, on the second side, you find "wings" <note>original: "penis," a typo for "pennis"</note>, replace it with "wings" <note>original: "pēnis," adding the abbreviation for the missing 'n'</note>; and "of the beak" <note>original: "roftri"</note> with "with beaks" <note>original: "roftris"</note>. Leaf 2: "the nursing women ambushed" <note>original: "nutricis infidiatis"</note>, read "the nursing women now ambushed" <note>original: "nutricis mō infidiatis"</note>. Leaf 4, side 2: "whirlpool" <note>original: "gurgitē," accusative</note>, read "whirlpool" <note>original: "gurgite," ablative</note>; "they might shrink from" <note>original: "abhorrerent"</note>, read "they might shrink from" <note>original: "abhorrerent," likely correcting a spacing or ligature error</note>. Leaf 4: "Teleiarchus" <note>original: "Teleiarchus"</note>, read "Teleiarchus." "Hipplytus," read "Hippolytus" <note>original: "Hippolitum"</note>. "Polayanthus," read "Polyanthus." Leaf 9: "they might have perceived" <note>original: "perceperint"</note>, read "they might have commanded" <note>original: "præceperint"</note>. "They might have stood through" <note>original: "perstauerint"</note>, read "they might have prostituted/stood forth" <note>original: "proſtauerint"</note>. In the second side: "to those about to hear" <note>original: "audituris"</note>, read "those about to hear" <note>original: "audituros," changing the case to accusative</note>.

<margin>In the second book.</margin>
Leaf 1: "with things applied" <note>original: "adhibitis"</note>, read "you apply" <note>original: "adhibetis"</note>. --- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the list of typographical corrections (Errata) to the formal dedicatory preface of the work. The writer, Julius Caesar Flaminius, is addressing the author, Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola, regarding the publication of his dialogue "On Witches" (De Strigibus).</meta>

# Julius Caesar Flaminius to the Lord Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola, greetings <note>original: "S." stands for "Salutem," a standard Latin greeting.</note>.

There is nothing, most illustrious and learned Prince, that I have ever more vehemently desired than to be loved not only by famous but also by good men, and to please them greatly, and to be counted among their friends—especially where nobility is accompanied by kindness, the brilliance of virtue, and excellent learning. Since I have often sought this among other Princes and learned men, I thought that at present, having been offered an especially great and welcome opportunity, I ought to try it with you as well, so that if I might, I could join myself to you with such devotion and be taken into your patronage <note>In the Renaissance, "clientela" (patronage) was a formal social bond between a scholar and a powerful protector.</note>. For what can be more pleasant to a man than to be received into the fellowship and friendship of the good? Without which, as Cicero <note>original: "Tullius," referring to the Roman statesman Marcus Tullius Cicero.</note> says, virtue can hardly exist; and to be bound in intimacy with one who is most worthy of everyone’s love. Driven by these reasons, I have dared to send this letter to you, so that you might understand that I will be yours in whatever ways I am able, and most ready for everything in which I may be of service to you. 

For since Leandro Alberti <note>original: "Leander Albertus," a Dominican historian and mutual friend of the author and editor.</note>, who is most fond of us both, was unable—on account of the business by which he is distracted on all sides—to be present as he wished and as was necessary for the printing of the *Dialogue on Witches* <note>original: "de Strigibus Dialogo." This refers to Pico's "Strix," a foundational text in the history of demonology.</note> recently published by you, he chose me to take a part of that labor. In this matter, I judged that I would be doing something equally pleasing to you and to him if, setting aside all else, I focused on this most elegant little work. I have labored on it with such faithfulness and diligence that I have not applied more care and industry to the monuments of my uncle Flaminio <note>Julius Caesar Flaminius was the nephew of the poet Marcantonio Flaminio; here he emphasizes the familial level of care he took with the manuscript.</note>—or more truly, my parent. In these books, indeed, your greatness is so apparent and stands out so much that this sacred undertaking could, by itself, suffice for the immortality of your name, even if no other monuments of your genius existed. From this work, besides incredible pleasure, I have certainly gathered rich fruit; for, by Hercules! <note>An ancient Roman oath used for emphasis.</note> nothing more healthy or powerful could be brought forward to drive out the great errors of certain people from our religion. Nothing (in my opinion) could be described more fully, elegantly, and effectively for the critique of morals in that genre; and nothing more certain could be produced for Dionysius the Areopagite <note>A 5th-century Christian theologian whom Pico defended against contemporary claims of being a later forgery.</note>, nor proved with truer testimonies and evidence than what you intend to demonstrate.

<summary>This page contains a dedicatory letter from Julius Caesar Flaminius to Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola, explaining that Flaminius took over the editorial supervision of the book "On Witches" from Leandro Alberti. Flaminius praises the work's theological importance and its defense of the legacy of Dionysius the Areopagite.</summary>

<keywords>Julius Caesar Flaminius, Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola, Leandro Alberti, De Strigibus, Strix, witchcraft, patronage, Cicero, Dionysius the Areopagite, book history</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page concludes the introductory letter from Julius Caesar Flaminius to Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola, which began on the previous page. Flaminius is finishing his praise of the work’s poetic merit and formally offering his loyalty to Pico as a client or friend.</meta>

<meta>The lower two-thirds of this page are obscured by heavy bleed-through and ghosting of reversed text from the opposite side of the leaf; only the clear text at the top is translated here.</meta>

Moreover, regarding the Heroic Verse <note>original: "Heroico Versu," referring to dactylic hexameter, the meter used for epic and serious poetry.</note>, because I also gladly devote my efforts to the poetic craft <note>original: "poeticæ facultati"</note>, there was nothing that could be discussed more aptly and divinely—nor anything more necessary for a person, if they read it diligently. For all these reasons, I believe that I, along with all those others whom you strive to benefit through your labor, owe you a great deal. I give thanks that so many of your labors have now come forth into the public for the sake of the Christian Commonwealth <note>original: "Christiana Republica," a term for the collective political and spiritual body of Christendom.</note>. I promise that if you judge Julius Caesar <note>The writer, Julius Caesar Flaminius, referring to himself in the third person.</note> worthy of your friendship, I will strive with all my might to ensure you understand and experience that nothing more welcome could have happened to me at this time than to be received into the number of your friends or clients <note>original: "clientum." In the Renaissance, a "client" was a learned person or artist supported by a powerful "patron."</note>. 

->Farewell, and keep me in your affection.<-

<summary>Julius Caesar Flaminius concludes his dedicatory preface by praising Pico's poetic skill and his service to the Christian world, while formally requesting to be accepted into Pico's circle of friends and protégés.</summary>
<keywords>patronage, Heroic Verse, Christian Commonwealth, Julius Caesar Flaminius, Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola, Renaissance poetry</keywords>
